DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY ix

SECTION 1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
1.1 Overview
The objective of this project was to advance two key technological components—aeration control
strategies and process design methodologies—to support the development and broader adoption of
suboxic biological nitrogen removal (SBNR). The project focused on achieving the following three goals:
 Enhance Model Predictive Control (MPC) Technology: Advance the DO/Nmaster MPC platform
 from its initial 2018 pilot deployment at the Chico Water Resource Recovery Facility in California to
 full-scale integration. This included partnering with a blower technology commercialization partner
 and incorporating machine learning (ML) capabilities to enable nationwide deployment.
 Bridge Knowledge Gaps in SBNR Process Design: Address fundamental gaps in SBNR process
 understanding through controlled pilot-scale testing at a dedicated pilot facility. These efforts
 supported the development of robust kinetic models to inform reliable SBNR control, operational
 strategies, and design frameworks.
 Demonstrate Full-Scale Implementation of Low DO/SBNR with ML: Transition low dissolved
 oxygen (DO)/SBNR coupled with ML from pilot-scale trials to full-scale demonstration in flow-through
 biological nutrient removal (BNR) systems, with the goal of enabling scalable, nationwide adoption in
 activated sludge treatment processes.

The project included demonstration of SBNR at the pilot scale as performed by Hampton Roads
Sanitation District (HRSD) and at the full-scale as performed by the Los Angeles County Sanitation
Districts' (LACSD) Pomona Water Reclamation Plant (POWRP).

1.2 Results

1.2.1 Pilot Results
Learnings gained from this project ranged from the fundamental to applied research. For fundamental
learnings based on HRSD's pilot, one major conclusion was that systems without an anaerobic selector
or zone can successfully perform SBNR. The kinetics for nitrification, denitrification, and biological
phosphorus removal were quantified and verified against the full-scale application at LACSD POWRP.
The pilot also added to the industry knowledge on microbes present at SBNR conditions, which
included complete ammonia oxidizers (comammox) as often detected at DO of less than 0.4 milligrams
per liter (mg/L).

1.2.2 Full-Scale Results
From an applied research perspective, POWRP maintained SBNR operations at an average operating DO
of 0.75 mg/L for more than 3 months while maintaining effluent quality of less than 2.2 mg/L as
ammonia, less than 6 mg/L as nitrate, and less than 0.22 mg/L as total phosphorus. At the same time,
POWRP was able to decrease their blower electricity consumption by 57 percent through

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 1

implementation of SBNR operations. This feat was accomplished through a variety of equipment and
controls upgrades including:
 Installation of blowers with sufficient turndown to meet low DO demands overnight. To accomplish
 the blower control, actuators, valves, and meters were installed.
 Implementation of an (1) ML-based controls system that operated in ammonia-based aeration control
 and proportional integral derivative control modes and (2) a solids retention time (SRT) controller.
 Implementation and integration of instrumentation to compliment the controllers.

In addition to the equipment installed, the project established approaches for gradually decreasing DO
setpoints to achieve stable SBNR operations. The project also had an objective of identifying critical key
design considerations for low DO operations, which are summarized in Section 4.5.

Greenhouse gas emission (GHG) measurements demonstrated that there was no discernable difference in
nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions in the typical DO operation at 2.0 mg/L (N2O flux of 1.89 ±0.99 gN2O/m2d)
versus low DO operations at 0.75 mg/L (N2O flux of 2.16 ±0.49 gN2O/m2d).

From a microbial community and microscopy perspective, LACSD did not see a significant shift in
microbes or filaments present, and no comammox were detected. It is likely that the average DO were not
low enough to encourage comammox growth.

Ultimately, LACSD completed the full-scale implementation for about $2,100,000, with a return on
investment (ROI) of approximately 8 years.

1.3 Conclusions and Recommendations
The project was highly successful at addressing the feasibility of SBNR operations at full-scale including
required equipment, controls needed, establishing operator buy-in, and transition to low DO or SBNR. The
project was so successful that LACSD is actively pursuing low DO operations at their other eleven plants.

While LACSD is one example, several other plants across the United States have implemented low DO
operations with different process configurations. Completion of the national survey started in this report
could provide additional operational and design guidance for low DO systems including:
 The impact of advanced controls on low DO savings. Other plants are performing low DO operations
 with less sophisticated controls systems (proportional-integral-derivative [PID] control, manual
 control, etc.), and it is unclear what incremental GHG and electricity savings advantages are associated
 with the controls.
 The impact of temperature on nitrification performance. POWRP was able to perform SBNR in all
 seasons with wastewater temperatures ranging from 18 to 29 degrees C. This project developed an
 aeration controls approach that allows any utility to safely reduce operational DOs without risking
 permit compliance to evaluate site-specific low DO/SBNR endpoints. Low DO/SBNR endpoints may
 differ between facilities depending upon factors like wastewater temperature, solid retention time,
 process configuration, hydraulic retention time, etc.

The industry would also benefit from understanding how to improve settleability of low DO sludges.
Recommendations include exploring how to retain larger floc with technology like hydrocyclones or
implementation of SBNR with other densification processes including membrane bioreactors.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 2

SECTION 2 PROJECT OVERVIEW
2.1 Background
The activated sludge process is an essential function for a healthy society and environment: it provides
removal of excess chemical oxygen demand (COD), ammonia, nitrogen (N), and phosphorus (P) in BNR
processes. The paradigm of conventional activated sludge treatment is for water resource reclamation
facilities (WRRF) to aerate at 1.5 to 4 milligrams DO per liter (mg DO/L) to maintain sufficient DO for
optimal oxidation of ammonium to nitrite by ammonium oxidizing bacteria (AOB) and nitrite to nitrate by
nitrite oxidizing bacteria (NOB). However, WRRFs are estimated to consume 2 percent of the United
States' energy, with activated sludge and BNR treatments utilizing greater than 50 percent of a WRRF's
energy consumption (Shen et al., 2015; USEPA, 2023).

Utilities and consultants throughout the United States use the term "low dissolved oxygen" for a variety of
different operational and process conditions that vary widely in energy efficiency and nutrient removal
performance. Here, the term SBNR is used to describe the most stable and energy efficient of these low
DO processes. SBNR systems are defined as activated sludge processes where each basin's aerated zone is
reduced to 0.7 mg DO/L or lower with precise control such that the microbiology acclimates to the low
DO "suboxic" redox zone (Rauch-Williams et al., 2020). SBNR is primarily observed in oxidation ditches in
North America with long hydraulic residence times (HRT) and SRT. However, plug flow BNR processes are
in the majority among BNR facilities in the United States, many of them located in seasonal climates.
Previous work over the last two decades has demonstrated effective strategies to achieve biological
acclimation of nitrifiers to suboxic DO concentrations, while also achieving reliable and improved total
nutrient removal (i.e., N and P), compared to conventional high DO BNR systems (Ekster et al., 2019;
Keene et al., 2017; Klaus and Bott, 2020; Park et al., 2004, 2007). Yet, existing challenges with
implementing SBNR in seasonal climate, plug-flow facilities include slower biological growth kinetics in
cold temperatures, stable DO concentrations in dynamic plug flow conditions, and sludge settleability.

Interest in low DO and SBNR have grown rapidly in the last several years, generating a significant amount
of information that is changing how we think about biological nitrogen and phosphorus removal in low
DO conditions and its implementation into new and existing WRRFs. As part of a 2-year United States
Department of Energy (DOE)-funded project (DE-EE0009509), "Transforming Aeration Energy in WRRFs
through Suboxic Nitrogen Removal," a team of researchers, consults, instrumentation and controls
experts, and utilities are working together to identify system requirements and operational conditions for
success SBNR operations.

2.2 Project Goals
The goal of this project was to advance two technological components – aeration controls and process
design – associated with SBNR through the following three project goals:
 Advancing the MPC technology DO/Nmaster from its first 2018 pilot implementation at the Chico
 Water Resource Recovery Facility (WRRF), CA, to full-scale integration with the Blower Technology
 Commercialization Partner for nationwide commercialization through implementation of ML.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 3

 Filling fundamental process knowledge gaps of SBNR process operation under controlled pilot-tests
 at the Pilot Testing Facility to develop kinetic relationships for process modeling and reliable SBNR
 control, operation, and design.
 Advancing low DO/SBNR coupled with ML from pilot to full-scale demonstration for flow-through
 BNR activated sludge treatment for nationwide commercialization.

2.3 Project Objectives and Report Format

2.3.1 Objectives
These goals were met by completing our project objectives, which were grouped into three objective
areas: Scientific Process Understanding, Engineering Design and Operational Boundary Conditions, and
Technology Evaluation and Commercialization.

For Scientific Process Understanding, the objectives were:
 To address critical questions related to SBNR.
 Successful operational strategies for microbial acclimation to low DO.
 Kinetic and process limitations.
 Key microbial populations and metabolic functions.
 Aeration control performance specifications.
 Process stability and resilience over a range of wastewater temperatures.
 Sludge settleability and mitigation strategies.
 Enhanced biological phosphorus removal stability (i.e., phosphorus uptake kinetics).
 GHG emissions.
 Organic carbon demand distribution and carbon diversion potential.

These objectives were addressed during pilot-scale investigations at the Pilot Testing Facility at HRSD.

For Engineering Design and Operational Boundary Conditions, the objectives were to:
 Define engineering and operational technology factors for successful technology commercialization.
 Obtain process modeling kinetic rates, stochiometric parameters, and switching functions for process
 modeling and design.
 Determine impacts on oxygen transfer efficiencies and design sludge volume indices (SVI).
 Establish capacity rating (minimum SRTs versus temperature versus DO, volumetric loading rates,
 minimum hydraulic residence time, footprint).
 Establish standard operating procedures for process transitioning/adaptation/MPC/ML tuning.
 Determine aeration control system performance specifications.
 Perform process optimization and troubleshooting guidance.
 Elaborate on effective mixer, diffuser, and tank layout designs.
 Identify process electrical and instrumentation controls and sensor needs and suitable sensor technology.
 Draft specifications, control descriptions, and operation and maintenance (O&M) manuals.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 4

These objectives were addressed in Phases 1 and 2 during pilot- and full-scale investigations at the Pilot
Testing Facility at HRSD and the Full-Scale Testing Facility at POWRP.

For Technology Evaluation and Commercialization, the objective was to complete Techno-Economic
Analysis (TEA) and Life-Cycle Analysis (LCA) for each phase of operation and prepare technology
packaging for commercialization. These efforts were led by Carollo Engineers (Carollo) and their
commercialization partners, APG-Neuros for the blower technology and Ekster & Associates for the
process control software, with support from HRSD, POWRP, and the Water Research Foundation (WRF).

SECTION 3 SUMMARY OF PILOT PERFORMANCE
3.1 Pilot Testing Objectives
The research objectives for the pilot focused on feasibility of operations and evaluating the adaptation of
BNR under steady operation to SBNR conditions over the course of the testing period. This section
outlines the objectives of the pilot-scale investigation conducted at the Pilot Testing Facility at HRSD's
Virginia Initiative Pilot (VIP) treatment plant in Norfolk, VA. The major objectives of pilot testing were to
increase scientific process understanding by:
 Identifying successful operational strategies for microbial acclimation to low DO/SBNR conditions.
 Quantifying kinetic and process limitations.
 Identifying key microbial populations and metabolic functions.
 Identifying process stability and resilience over a range of wastewater temperatures.
 Quantifying sludge settleability and mitigation strategies.
 Gaining understanding on the impacts of enhanced biological phosphorus removal stability
 (i.e., P uptake kinetics).
 Quantifying GHG emissions.

Key hypotheses of interest include:
 AOB and NOB will acclimate to low DO (half saturation of oxygen [KO] decreases while µmax
 remains constant).
 Capacity is not impacted and SRT will not need to increase compared to conventional DO operation.
 Nitrifiers acclimated to low DO conditions can buffer short-term ammonia peak loadings through
 (small) DO adjustments, like AOB and NOB acclimated to high DO concentrations, given sufficient
 steady-state SRT for the given wastewater temperature.
These hypotheses are critical to understand to be able to design adequate control responses that allow
WRRFs to handle diurnal and other influent ammonia peaks.

3.2 Pilot Process Layout and Operations

3.2.1 Pilot Process Layout
Details of the pilot are included in Appendix A. The pilot was operated at the HRSD VIP treatment plant.
The pilot is an anoxic-oxic (AO) configuration, as shown in Figure 3-1. Raw wastewater entering the HRSD
VIP treatment plant was initially pumped from the head of the influent channel into an equalization tank
followed by screening and grit removal. After grit removal, the influent undergoes primary clarification
prior to flowing into the AO system. Primary clarification had a target of providing 40 to 50 percent
removal of total suspended solids (TSS). Primary clarifier and grit tank underflows were sent back to the
main plant. The effluent from the primary clarifier was an equalization tank prior to the AO process.
Wastewater was monitored to maintain 20 degrees Celsius (C).

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 8

Figure 3-1 Process Flow Schematic of HRSD VIP BNR Pilot

The AO process consisted of six completely
stirred tank reactors (CSTR) in a sequential
arrangement that was implemented to replicate
plug flow characteristics within the BNR system
configured to maintain a 7.2-hour HRT. The
"anaerobic" (i.e., anoxic) tank was covered with
ping pong balls to reduce air intrusion. All tanks
were mixed using propeller mixers. For aerobic
zone aeration, compressed air was supplied to
the tanks through fine bubble diffusers
(Figure 3-2). The effluent from the BNR process
was then directed to a secondary pilot clarifier
and subsequently to a secondary equalization
bucket. The return activated sludge (RAS) was
pumped from the secondary clarifier and
returned to the anaerobic tank at the same rate
as the influent flow. The aerobic solids retention
time (aSRT) was maintained at approximately 7
to 9 days which was achieved by adjusting the
wasting rate from the BNR system. To ensure
consistent conditions, the pilot room was
regulated at a temperature of 20 degrees C
throughout the entire process. Figure 3-2 BNR Aerobic Tank Setup with Fine
 Bubble Diffusor, DO Probe, and Mixer

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 9

The pilot incorporated various probes that provided real time data to the Digital Control System (DCS)
(Figure 3-3), including temperature probe in the anaerobic tank and DO probes (Insite Model 10, LA, USA)
in the aeration tanks. Aeration in the aerobic tanks was regulated by a PID loop, which controlled the
valve position of the mass flow controller to maintain the desired DO setpoint.

Figure 3-3 DCS Screen with Controls to Operate the BNR System in the HRSD VIP BNR Pilot

Table 3-1 summarizes all probes, analyzers, and flow meters that will be used in the HRSD pilot site. The
locations where these instruments are located are shown in the process flow diagram in Figure 3-1.

3.2.2 Pilot Process Monitoring and Operations
Data were collected on effluent quality including carbon, nitrogen, and phosphorus removal, process
stability, operational parameters, kinetics, settling, microbial community composition, and GHG emissions
using Standard Methods (2023) when applicable. GHG emissions methods are summarized in Section 5.
Microbial community data is summarized in Section 6.

The pilot maintained a pH range of 6.7 to 7.2, and alkalinity was provided by adding sodium bicarbonate
to the third aerobic tank as needed. pH measurement in the fourth aerobic tank was conducted using a
Foxboro pH probe (Invensys, London, UK). In the last aerobic tank, the system employed a TSS and an ion-
selective electrode (ISE) ammonia probe. Additionally, the first aerobic tank housed a N2O probe. To
analyze nitrogen species (ammonia, nitrite, and nitrate (NH3-N, NO2-N, and NO3-N)) in the first and last
tank, the system was equipped with an in-house-built wet chemical analyzer developed by HRSD. This
analyzer could extract filtered samples from the first and last BNR tanks. The entire process was controlled
using a DCS, and a snapshot of the control screen is depicted in Figure 3-3.

3.3 Operations

3.3.1 DO Control and Nitrogen and Phosphorus Removal
The pilot study demonstrated the feasibility of operating a BNR system under low DO conditions,
achieving complete nitrification, excellent enhanced biological phosphorus removal (EBPR), and stable
settleability. Process stability was evident through robust nitrification and phosphorus removal, consistent
settleability, and low filament counts, despite variations in floc size distribution. The adaptation of nitrifiers
to low DO, highlighted by a reduced oxygen half saturation coefficient, underscores the microbial
community's capacity to adapt to SBNR conditions while maintaining complete nitrification and excellent
ammonia removal performance. These findings highlight the potential for energy-efficient SBNR
operation strategies, maintaining treatment efficacy while leveraging microbial adaptation to
optimize performance.

The pilot was successfully transitioned to low DO conditions by incrementally decreasing the DO
concentration in the aerobic zone from 2.0 mg DO/L to 0.2 mg DO/L over 19 months. This was done
across 11 set points, while maintaining a consistent aSRT of 6 to 8.5 days. The pilot operated at a DO of

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 11

0.2 mg DO/L for over 3 months. The pilot achieved stable nitrification at each DO set point which resulted
in an average ammonia removal of 99 ± 4 percent, with minimal process upsets throughout the transition
to low DO. At pilot DO set points of 2 mg/L and 0.2 mg/L, the average percent P removed was 94 ±
7 percent and 90 ± 11 percent, respectively. Although there is a minimal decrease in average P removal,
the decrease is not statistically significant. These results suggest that excellent EBPR can be achieved
under low DO operations, even in an AO system with RAS returning nitrate to the anaerobic zone. The
reduction in operating DO from 2.0 mg/L to 0.2 mg/L corresponded to a 39 percent reduction in airflow
required for the process (2.40 standard cubic feet per minute [scfm] to 1.46 scfm).

Different profiles were taken throughout the transition of the DO setpoint. Figure 3-4 and Figure 3-5 show
the profile data for DO setpoints of 1 mg/L and 0.2 mg/L respectively at an aSRT of 8 days. The profile
data show that efficient NH3 removal can be achieved even at low DO setpoints without the loss of
capacity of the treatment train. However, it is important to note that even though the DO setpoints were
decreased, the total inorganic nitrogen (TIN) in the effluent from the pilot did not significantly decrease
over the transition to low DO. This phenomenon needs to be further evaluated for ordinary heterotrophic
organism's (OHO) adaptation to low DO and their ability to efficiently use available oxygen. This shows
that even though full-scale systems see simultaneous nitrification and denitrification (SND) at lower DO
levels, the adaptation of OHOs might hinder SND efficiency at low DO systems.

Figure 3-4 Ammonia and TIN Profile through Pilot Reactors at Steady State Operation with 1 mg/L DO and
 8 days aSRT

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 12

Figure 3-5 Ammonia and TIN Profile through Pilot Reactors at Steady State Operation with 0.2 mg/L DO and
 8 days aSRT

3.3.2 Settleability, Filaments, and Floc Size
Settleability was maintained throughout the transition to low DO, with settled SVI (measured at
30 minutes) consistently ≤ 100 grams per liter (g/L). Filamentous bacteria counts remained low during the
study. These findings demonstrate that settleability can be maintained under low DO conditions.
Throughout the operating period floc size measurements fluctuated and ranged from 50 to
350 micrometers (µm) with a lower average size of about 75 µm, the changes and variability in floc size
did not appear to impact settleability (e.g., SVI).

below.

3.4.1.2 SND/P uptake rate test
To investigate the kinetic parameters, a series of batch tests were conducted. SND/P uptake rate test was
conducted to model the kinetics of AOB and denitrifiers, as well as to gather information on the extent of
SND in the batch test. A 4L batch of sample was obtained from the anaerobic BNR tank and was set up
with mixing, pH control and aeration. 25 mg/L of NO3-N, 4 mg/L of NO2-N and 20 mg/L of NH3-N spiked
into the batch. The addition of these nutrients occurred immediately after reaching the desired initial DO
concentration. No additional carbon was added to the batch; instead, the carbon already present in the
anaerobic tank was utilized to simulate the influent soluble chemical oxygen demand (sCOD)
characteristics in the BNR system. During the test, five samples, each comprising 10 to 15 milliliters (mL)
were taken at 10-minute intervals. A total of six different batches were taken and analyzed at DO setpoints
ranging from 0 mg/L to 4 mg/L.

The speciation of nitrogen species, specifically NH3-N and NOx, at each DO setpoints was sorted and
recorded in an Excel spreadsheet. TIN values were calculated by summing the measured concentrations of
NH3-N, NO2-, and NO3- from the samples. NH3 and TIN removal rates respectively, were used in the model.

3.4.1.3 Declining DO test
In this respirometry test, the objective is to predict oxygen kinetic parameters for endogenous respiration,
NOB respiration and AOB respiration. This test is conducted using a 4L sample obtained from the end of
the aerobic zone of the BNR system set up with a mixer, pH control, and aeration control.

This experiment is divided into three phases:
1. Endogenous Respiration: After aerating for 12 to 15 hours to remove the exogenous oxygen
 demand, aeration is cut off, and the DO is allowed to be utilized by microorganisms for endogenous
 respiration (i.e., without any external substrate). The oxygen data is logged every second in the
 Pyroscience software and saved for further analysis.
2. Endogenous + NOB respiration: Once the DO reaches zero, aeration is reintroduced to raise the
 concentration to 6-7 mg/L. When the DO reaches 5 mg/L, NO2-N is added to the batch to achieve a
 concentration of 4 mg/L. The aeration is kept constant and a DO setpoint of 6-7 mg/L is maintained
 using the PLC controller for 5 minutes. Then, aeration is cut off until the DO drops to 0 mg/L. The
 oxygen data is logged in the Pyroscience software which is further analyzed. This process is
 repeated twice.
3. Endogenous + NOB + AOB respiration: Following the second run for endogenous + NOB
 respiration, the batch is aerated to reach a non-limiting DO concentration (6-7 mg/L). NH3-N and
 NO2-N at concentrations of 25 mg/L and 4 mg/L, respectively, are spiked into the batch to ensure
 nonlimiting substrate concentrations for AOB and NOB. The aeration is then kept constant (as in the
 previous run) for 5 mins, and subsequently, the air supply is cut off from the system. This process is
 repeated twice. The drop in oxygen concentrations is logged in the Pyroscience software and
 analyzed later using R-script.

Each run was set to start at 5 mg/L and end at 0 mg/L. The R-script calculated the OUR at all the DO data
points and fitted the data simultaneously. The parameters for max rates and KO values for endogenous,
NOB and, AOB respiration were fitted using the least squares method, which is a common approach for
curve fitting. This initial fitting process is referred to as the forward run. Subsequently, the data obtained
from the forward run was further analyzed using Bayesian methods and Markov Chain Monte Carlo

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 17

(MCMC) techniques. This involved performing 100,000 simulations to predict the posterior distribution of
the parameters. This is called the inverse run and the predictions for the parameters were noted.

3.4.1.4 Maximum Denitrification Rate Test
The test involves taking a 2L sample of sludge from the anaerobic tank and mixing it with 2L of primary
effluent in the pilot. This ensures that there is non-limiting sCOD available in the batch. The batch is then
set up with pH control and mixing, and it is covered with foam to prevent air intrusion. To create anoxic
conditions in the reactor, 4 mg/L of NO2-N and 25 mg/L NO3-N are added. Six samples are taken from the
batch at intervals of 5 minutes. These samples are then analyzed for concentrations of NH3-N, NO2-N,
NO3-N, and sCOD.

The calculated nitrite and nitrate data, as well as NOx data, were plotted to visualize their trends. The
removal rates were calculated using general linear regression methods which is a common method for
fitting a line to data points and determining the rate of change. To compare the removal rates with the
denitrification rates from the SND/P test, the data were further normalized with VSS.

3.4.1.5 P Release/COD Uptake Rate Test
To conduct the test, a 4L sludge sample is obtained from the end of the aerobic zone and set up with a
mixture, foam cover, and pH control. The test is run under anaerobic conditions. To simulate P release in
the batch, 200 mg/L of acetate and 200 mg/L of propionate are added. Six samples are collected from the
batch at intervals of 5 minutes. These samples are then analyzed for OP, NO2-N, NO3-N, sCOD and volatile
fatty acids.

P release rate was calculated using linear regression fit. The NOx data were also analyzed, and the
chemical oxygen demand (COD) uptake by nitrate and nitrite species was calculated. Furthermore, the
COD uptake specifically by polyphosphate accumulating organisms (PAOs) was calculated after
subtracting the usage of COD by the NOx species.

3.4.1.6 Nitrifier Kinetic Adaptation
Nitrifier adaptation was observed in both in-situ and batch tests. Complete nitrification was achieved at
each DO set point and the ammonia uptake rate observed in-situ did not decrease meaningfully. The
average nitrifier oxygen half saturation coefficient decreased from 1.4 mg DO/L to 0.20 mg DO/L as the
pilot operating DO was reduced from 2.0 mg/L to 0.20 mg/L. These results indicated that even in a
controlled environment, with consistent temperature, SRT, and flow/load, a change in oxygen half
saturation coefficient could be induced by a reduction in operating DO. Additionally, the reduction in
nitrifier oxygen half saturation coefficient demonstrates that nitrifiers were capable of achieving a higher
affinity for oxygen throughout the adaptation period while the maximum ammonia removal rates
remained relatively constant.

3.4.1.7 Denitrifier Kinetic Adaptation and SND
As the pilot transitioned to low DO, heterotrophs also appeared to adapt to low DO conditions, similar to
nitrifiers. SND (TIN removal across the aerobic zone) was expected to improve under low DO conditions,
however, steady and minimal SND was observed. At an operating DO set point of 2.0 mg/L, SND
contributed an average of 22 percent of TIN removal, which unexpectedly decreased to 20 percent at

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 18

0.2 mg/L, but this difference was not statistically significant. This suggests that heterotrophs adapted
similarly to how nitrifiers adapted, with a reduction in oxygen half saturation coefficient. Because SND is a
function of heterotrophic oxygen half saturation coefficient and operating DO, the constant SND
observed in this study as the operating DO was lowered suggests that there was a reduction in
heterotrophic oxygen half saturation coefficient. It is relevant to note that mixing energy remained
approximately the same at low DO, relative to high DO, with mechanical mixing throughout the aerobic
zone. The pilot reactors were well-mixed, reducing the likelihood of anoxic pockets that could drive SND.
This differs from full-scale operations, where less consistent mixing may create pockets of anoxic zones
within an aerobic basin that could facilitate SND.

3.5 Milestone Summary
These results verified the research hypothesis numbers 1 and 2. Also, the fully equipped and functionally
tested pilot train at the Pilot Testing Facility at suboxic conditions completed Milestone 2.1, as well as the
biological acclimation of the bacterial community to SBNR conditions with the SRT used under normal
BNR operating conditions without compromising effluent quality was quantified as the completion of
Milestone 3.1. The pilot process was able to achieve full nitrification (effluent ammonia < 1 mg/L) at DO
concentrations (<0.7 mg/L, and a stretch goal <0.5), while keeping SVI <150 milliliters per gram (mL/g) at
steady state conditions at similar SRTs operated under baseline conditions. This achieved successful
completion of Milestone M6.1.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 19

SECTION 4 FULL-SCALE LOW DO DEMONSTRATION
4.1 Objectives and Hypotheses
The research objectives for the full-scale demonstration focused on feasibility of operations and
evaluating the adaptation of SBNR under dynamic conditions while gaining insights into the importance
of controls implementation and selection. This section outlines the objectives of the full-scale
investigation conducted at the LACSD's POWRP in Pomona, CA. The major objectives of full-scale
demonstration testing were
 Advancing low DO/SBNR coupled with MPC and ML from pilot to full-scale demonstration for flow-
 through BNR activated sludge treatment for nationwide commercialization.
 Identifying successful operational strategies for microbial acclimation to low DO/SBNR conditions.
 Quantifying process modeling kinetic rates, stochiometric parameters, and switching functions for
 process modeling and design.
 Identifying process stability and resilience over a range of wastewater temperatures, influent loading
 changes, and process interruptions.
 Establishing standard operating procedures for process transitioning and adaptation and MPC and
 ML tuning.
 Quantifying oxygen transfer efficiencies and design sludge volume indices.
 Quantifying impacts on plant operational capacity rating including minimum SRTs at different
 temperatures and DOs, volumetric loading rates, and minimum HRT.
 Gaining understanding on the impacts of enhanced biological phosphorus removal.
 Develop approaches for process optimization and troubleshooting.
 Quantifying GHG emissions at full-scale.

4.2 Full-Scale Demonstration Site: Prior Condition

4.2.1 Plant Overview
The full-scale facility investigation of SBNR was conducted at POWRP. POWRP is a 15-million-gallon-per-
day (mgd) design capacity decentralized WRRF that produces California Title 22 disinfected tertiary
(unrestricted) recycled water for landscape irrigation, various industrial and commercial uses, and
groundwater recharge. POWRP has three primary sedimentation tanks, three aeration tanks, six
rectangular secondary clarifiers, eight granular media tertiary filters, and three chlorine contact tanks for
disinfection via chloramination (Figure 4-1). Originally, POWRP was designed and operated as a
conventional activated sludge facility; in 2004, it was converted to a BNR facility using the Modified
Ludzack-Ettinger (MLE) process. POWRP currently processes a daily average flow of about 9 mgd. Due to
topography, raw sewage is conveyed by gravity into the facility without any influent pumps. Grit, primary
and waste activated sludges, and primary and secondary skimmings are returned to the sewer and treated

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 20

downstream at LACSD's A.K. Warren Water Resource Facility in Carson, CA (formerly known as the Joint
Water Pollution Control Plant).

Figure 4-1 POWRP Process Flow Diagram

The aeration tanks operate as three parallel units consisting of five zones each: small anaerobic selector
zone, anoxic zone, swing zone (end of Grid A), and two aerobic zones (Grids B and C). Each zone is
separated from the next zone by a baffle wall. A mixed liquor recirculation pump returns mixed liquor
suspended solids (MLSS) from the end of the aeration tank to the anoxic zone. The aeration system
includes 9-inch fine bubble, porous ceramic disc diffusers with 13/64-inch orifices. Each diffuser grid is
also equipped with moisture blow-off piping for venting water from the diffuser grid piping during start-
up of the aeration system. Three single-stage, integrally geared, Dresser Roots OIB centrifugal blowers
with inlet guide vanes supply process air for the aeration system. The blowers are each rated at
13,300 scfm at 7.5 pounds per square inch (psi), with a turndown of approximately 6,500 psi.

The blower supplies air to a single header for Aeration Tank 1 and a common header for Aeration Tanks 2
and 3. Actuated butterfly valves on two headers are modulated with PID controllers to maintain DO
setpoints in the selected diffuser grids downstream of the valve. Manual operated butterfly valves are used
to further control the air distribution to the grids. An operator-selected DO probe in one of the diffuser
grids is also used to increase or decrease air flow by moving the inlet guide vanes on the blower. The mixed
liquor and RAS flows are flow-paced to the influent flow based on ratios established by the operator. A 2-
hour ratio setpoint table provides the operator with the ability to pre-establish hourly ratios throughout the
day. Mixed liquor and RAS ratios are typically 210 percent and 0.95 to 100 percent, respectively.

4.2.2 Existing Power Usage and Distribution
Secondary aeration accounts for most of the energy demands at water recovery facilities in the United
States. At the POWRP, the aeration blowers accounted for 70 percent of the total energy demand of
13,400 kilowatt-hours per day (kWh/d) in 2019. This is slightly higher than the 45 to 60 percent usually
observed at WRRFs, likely due to the lack of solids treatment onsite as shown in Figure 4-2.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 21

Source: DHI Report (2019), based on 2018 data.

Figure 4-2 POWRP Energy Consumption by Process

requirements as shown in Table 4-1. The plant does not have a phosphorus discharge limit.

4.3 Full-Scale Demonstration Site Project Upgrades
Historically, POWRP has been used for LACSD research since influent flow reductions and full plant
diversions are possible if needed, making it ideal for this project. However, such diversions were not
needed during the project. In addition, the older blowers were reaching end-of-life. POWRP was a suitable
full-scale demonstration site since LACSD was embarking on significant infrastructure upgrades to
improve process controls and reduce energy consumption. These upgrades had proceeded just far
enough to allow the equipment and piping to be modified to meet SBNR project requirements. The site
improvements undertaken are described in the following sections.

4.3.1        Blowers
A detailed report of APG-Neuros activities is included in Appendix B. At the start of the project, LACSD
was in the process of replacing one of the single stage, integrally geared centrifugal blowers. APG-Neuros
has extensive experience in designing and implementing master control panels (MCP) and control
systems, particularly for aeration processes in wastewater treatment plants. Their MCPs are integral to
optimizing the performance and efficiency of high-speed turbo blowers, which are a critical component in
these systems. LACSD provided APG-Neuros with a test report with performance curves and tables with
design points for the existing blower. After reviewing the performance test report on the blower, APG-
Neuros optimized the new blower design for maximum efficiency with a wide flow turn down and the
ability to vary the operating discharge pressure. Figure 4-2 shows the original and new blowers.

(a) Old Centrifugal Blower (b) New APG-Neuros Turbo Blower

Figure 4-3 Old Centrifugal Blower (a) and New APG-Neuros Turbo Blower (b)

Table 4-2 summarizes the performance comparison between the old centrifugal blowers and new
APG-Neuros turbo blowers. The new turbo blower provided a significantly lower turndown capability of
2,600 scfm, which was suitable for the project.

The MCPs can control the DO concentration in the individual aeration control zones using the
following modes:
 Cascaded DO Control – The MCP calculates an airflow setpoint required to maintain the DO at an
 operator set DO setpoint for each aeration control zone using a Proportional type controller. The

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 23

 airflow setpoint is cascaded to the valve control loop where a second Proportional type controller
 adjusts the position of the valve to meet the airflow setpoint.
 Constant Flow Mode – The operator enters a constant airflow setpoint for an individual aeration
 control zone. A Proportional type controller adjusts the position of the valve to meet the
 airflow setpoint.
 Manual Control Mode – The operator enters a fixed valve position for an individual aeration
 control zone.
 DO/Nmaster Control Mode – The MCP receives a valve position setpoint from the DO/Nmaster
 controller and adjusts the valve position to that setpoint.

In April 2023, the new APG-Neuros blowers were commissioned in PID control mode. In August 2023 prior
to DO/Nmaster integration into the controls, a follow-up meeting between the plant and APG-Neuros
established that the controls that the existing controls were providing smooth and functional control.

The number of diffusers heads in the aeration basins decreased as shown in Figure 4-4 to meet predicted
average, peak, and minimum airflow scenarios under SBNR operations. Operators were hesitant to make a
drastic change to their diffuser counts, and did not feel comfortable reducing the diffusers by what was
originally recommended, especially in Grid B which is the first dedicated aeration zone. LACSD staff
provided the following recommendations that were implemented in the design:
 Diffuser counts were not reduced in the swing zone since DOs can achieve a maximum of 0.6 mg/L
 with the valves fully open.
 Diffuser counts were more aggressively reduced in Grid B with minor changes to Grid C to balance the
 air demand between grids and enable the process to achieve consistently lower DOs for improved
 biological acclimation.

4.3.3 Control Valves
Although other valves like Iris have been touted for low DO operations, POWRP opted to continue using
butterfly valves due to their less expensive price, experience with the valves, and the determination that
the valves could likely meet the stringent airflow demands for the project (Figure 4-6). The existing
manual butterfly valves were oversized for the precise air control required for suboxic/low DO operation.
At low air demands (i.e., low DO operation), the butterfly valves are at the minimum percent opening
(<20 percent), which makes precise air control difficult. As a result, the existing valves in the swing zones
(Grid A), first aerobic zone (Grid B), and second aerobic zone (Grid C) were reduced in size from 8- to
4-inch, from 12- to 8-inch, and from 8- to 6-inch, respectively, in order to achieve a typical valve operating
range between 20 and 80 percent opening (Figure 4-7).

Figure 4-6 Example of Valve and Actuator Installed at POWRP

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 27

Figure 4-7 An Example of Dwell Time Graph for Selected Butterfly Valve at Actual Airflows

Butterfly valves have highly non-linear characteristics (Figure 4-8) that can also make precise air control
difficult. Although valves with linear characteristics (i.e., diaphragm control valves) are sometimes utilized
for this reason, these valves are considerably more expensive than butterfly valves. Properly sized
industry-standard butterfly valves with the right actuators have been shown to be capable of precise air
control (T. Jenkins, 2021) (Table 4-4).

Industry-standard American Petroleum Institute (API) 609 (Category A) butterfly valves,
manufactured by Pratt Industrial (BF2-8687), were selected based on availability, performance,
and cost. Source: City of Chico Water Pollution Control Plant, 2023.

Figure 4-8 Typical Flow Characteristic of Butterfly Valve

4.3.4 Valve Actuators
Precise control with a butterfly valve can be accomplished with the appropriate instrumentation and
positioning actuator (Figure 4-9). Airflow repeatability of the actuator position is the most important
parameter of an actuator used for control valve modulation. Several actuators were considered, including
Rotork Model control valve actuator (CVA) (range linear) with quarter turn drive action (CVQ), Rotork IQ3
and AUMA actuators, that can provide increased accuracy while coupled with butterfly valves. In
particular, Rotork CVA/CVQ actuators were noted to have high accuracy and continuous unrestricted
modulation duty, which is important for low DO operations. Another important parameter for airflow
control is the maximum number of actuations per hour because the airflow set point continuously
changes. Based on this specification, Rotork CVA/CVQ actuators were selected for use at POWRP with the
optional manual override. The manual override is important as it allows valves to be manually adjusted by
the operator in the event of a communication or electrical issue associated with the actuator control loop.

Figure 4-9 Impact of Actuator Positioning Repeatability on Precise Flow Control

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 29

4.3.5 Air Flow Meters (Thermal-Mass Flowmeter)
The air flow control loop is generally a secondary loop in the DO-airflow cascade controls and therefore
accuracy is not as critical as time constant and repeatability.

The time constant (i.e., the time it takes the air flow sensor to reach 63.2 percent of its final reading after a
change in flow) is a critical parameter for the airflow meter. Fast, rapidly moving actuators like those
selected for the POWRP require fast accurate, stable (low noise signal) air flow measurements to obtain
the precision needed for tight control. If the time constant for the flow meter is not fast enough, the
actuator needs to be slowed down to make sure the valve position is not changed before the flow
measurement is stable. Repeatability is important, especially in ML applications, where an exact valve
position yields essentially the same flow each time. Thermal-mass flowmeters, manufactured by Kurz were
selected for the project based on availability, performance, and cost (Table 4-5).

4.3.6 DO Sensors (Optical or Fluorescent Technology)
DO control loops are either primary loops when DO set points are established by operators or secondary
loops in the case of ammonia-based aeration control (ABAC) when DO set points are determined by the
ABAC control loops. As described for air flow meters, the time constant needs to be fast enough so that
the DO sensor does not contribute to control loop delay.

In a secondary control loop, accuracy is less important than repeatability and the time constant. For DO
control, accuracy and repeatability is important especially at low DO. Since most wastewater process
applications are typically greater than DO of 1.0 mg/L, many manufacturers have focused more on
providing accuracy at higher DO levels than those under suboxic conditions. Self-cleaning options (i.e.,
air-blast cleaning system) are recommended for installations where DO control loops are normally primary
control loops. HACH optical luminescent sensors were already being used at LACSD's WRPs and were
selected. Fluorescent DO sensors, a specific type of luminescent sensors, (i.e., Insite IG®) are generally
considered faster than luminescent sensors and may be advantageous for applications using only DO
Control. DO Sensors, manufactured by HACH, were selected for the project since LACSD had standardized
HACH DO sensors at its WRP facilities (Table 4-5).

4.3.7 Ammonia Sensors (Ion Selective Technology)
For the demonstration project at POWRP, ammonia control is a primary loop. Although accuracy is
important, sensor repeatability, especially at low ammonia levels, is the most important parameter.
Ammonium readings with ISE sensors that are consistent and repeatable with downstream wet-chemical
ammonium analyzer data (i.e., secondary effluent) can be relied on even with some inaccuracy. The
ammonia loop is relatively slow; therefore, the time constant is not as important as in the case of DO and,
especially, airflow meters. ISE sensors with self-cleaning functions (including sonic or air blasts) are
recommended. ISE sensors should be located in well mixed aerated zones for best performance. Although
not ultimately utilized at POWRP, feed forward options that place ISE sensors up front (i.e., primary
effluent) should be thoroughly field tested as these zones are typically less mixed and more prone to
fouling. Horiba ammonia sensors were selected for the project based on LACSD previous pilot testing to
assess performance and maintenance requirements (Table 4-5). The electrodes lasted more than
12 months before needing replacement.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 30

4.3.8 Total Suspended Solids Meters
When TSS meters are used for SRT control, repeatability is the most important parameter. If TSS meters
are used for TSS control, accuracy becomes the most critical parameter. TSS sensors with self-cleaning
features (i.e., wipers) are recommended, the Solitax model manufactured by HACH were selected for the
project since the meters could utilize the same HACH controllers as the DO sensors (Table 4-5).

4.3.9 Nitrate Sensors (UV Adsorption)
Although the nitrate concentration in the aeration tanks was not utilized for process control, the data
provided valuable information on the impact of process changes on denitrification. Nitratax sensors,
manufactured by HACH, were selected for the project since the meters could utilize the same HACH
controllers as the DO sensors (Table 4-5) and LACSD had extentive experience using them.

4.3.10 Summary of Installed Actuators and Sensors Characteristics
Based on project team's experience and preferences of LACSD, the following actuators and instruments
were procured and installed (Table 4-5).

4.3.11 Sensors Placement
Identical DO, airflow, ammonia, and TSS meters were installed in all three aeration tanks. DO and airflow
meters were installed in the swing zone (Grid A) and in both aerobic zones (Grids B and C). Ammonia
meters were installed at the approximate end of both aerobic zones (Grid B and C). One immersion-type
TSS meter was installed at the end of the last aeration zone (Grid C). One in-line TSS meter was also
installed in the RAS line from which waste activated sludge (WAS) is wasted to the sewer for processing
downstream at LACSD's A.K. Warren Water Recovery Facility. A diagram for the ammonia, DO, NO3, TSS,
internal mixed liquor recycle (IMLR) sensors, as well as valve and flow meter locations are provided in
Figure 4-10.

4.3.12 Control System
SRTmaster and DO/Nmaster software packages developed by project partner Ekster & Associates were
installed on an industrial PC incorporated into APG-Neuros' MCP. A detailed report of the controls system
is included in Appendix C. This control architecture differed from the previous installation at the Chico
WRRF where DO/Nmaster and SRTmaster were installed on a standalone server. Figure 4-11 summarizes
the sensors and controls scheme used in DO/Nmaster.

DO/Nmaster, is a multi-function ML-based control system consisting of the following blocks:
 A database where all incoming and outgoing data was filtered using a variety of data filtration
 techniques and stored.
 ML modules that calculated set points for dissolved oxygen, airflow, and valve position in each
 aeration grid. Each of the control loops can operate independently or be integrated into a complex
 aeration control structure.
 Visualization Graphical User Interfaces used to generate customizable charts.
 Output data filtration system.
 Communications that exchanged data with Plant SCADA system utilizing standard open platform
 communications (OPC; object linking and embedding for process control) protocol and de-facto
 standard protocols for communicating between any SCADA and Windows-based system.

SRTmaster is an MPC system consisting of the following blocks:
 A database where all incoming and outgoing data was filtered using a variety of data filtration
 techniques and stored.
 The MPC which calculated a waste flow setpoint for the system.
 Visualization graphical user interfaces were used to generate customizable charts.
 Output data filtration system.
 Communications identical to the DO/Nmaster communication module.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 34

Figure 4-11 Detailed integration diagram for the DO/Nmaster and SRTmaster Control Systems

4.3.13 Cybersecurity
Due to the sophisticated nature of the state-of-the-art control algorithms in these control systems,
periodic vendor tuning and troubleshooting was required. As is customary for complex and proprietary
control systems, remote access was highly desirable (if not required) for occasional and timely vendor
tuning and troubleshooting. In addition, the demonstration project had unique needs for research process
monitoring and reporting.

LACSD understood the challenges related to providing remote access to outside parties and developed a
cybersecurity architecture with firewalls that minimize the risk related to remote access Figure 4-12). One
firewall was responsible for controlling general remote access, and the other firewall limited access of the
industrial computer (DO/Nmaster and SRTmaster) to aeration input/output (I/O) only. The rest of the
plant I/O remained inaccessible. LACSD had the ability to fully monitor and control access throughout the
duration of the project.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 35

4.4 Full-Scale Operation

4.4.1 Evaluation of DO/Nmaster Controller

4.4.1.1 Airflow Control Accuracy
Airflow accuracy and repeatability were evaluated over a 24-hour period using field data. The frequency of
data sampling was 30 seconds. Airflow set points were changed every 3 seconds simultaneously at all
grids. Airflows were monitored using the permanently installed airflow meters described in Section 4.3.5.
The average airflow control error was 0.75 percent, the median error was 0.25 percent (Figure 4-13).

Airflow control at the POWRP was significantly more accurate than at the Chico Water Pollution Control
Plant (WPCP) despite using an identical ML control library, airflow meters, and similar butterfly valves. The
improvements are attributed to the use of precise positioning actuators, which are specifically designed
for accurate process control. Unlike typical actuators, these are engineered for continuous modulation and
have an exact position accuracy of 0.1 percent.

The achieved airflow accuracy is comparable to valves with linear characteristics (i.e., diaphragm control
valves). Combining ML control with butterfly valves and precise actuators was more economical than
using significantly more expensive valves with linear characteristics.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 36

Figure 4-13 Typical Performance of Airflow Control Loop at POWRP

4.4.1.2 DO Control Accuracy
The performance of DO/Nmaster DO control loops was compared to the traditional PID control system.
Accuracies of control were compared under variable DO setpoints that changed from 0.5 mg/L to 2 mg/L.

The average error of PID control was more than three times greater than that of ML-based control, i.e.,
20 percent error with the PID control versus 6 percent using DO/Nmaster. The absolute error of ML-based
control was nearing the error margin of HACH DO meters, which is 0.1 mg/L (Figure 4-14). The mean PID
error was 0.2 mg/L, and the relative error was 20 percent. The mean error of DO/Nmaster control of
dissolved oxygen was 0.06 mg/L, and the relative error was equal to 6 percent. Although not tested on
this project, the control error might be further reduced with a DO meter with greater repeatability, such as
those provided by Insite IG® Optical Probes.

The DO/Nmaster control accuracy of the DO loops at the POWRP surpassed that at the Chico WPCP, likely
due to improved airflow control and the upgraded DO control algorithm. The DO meter models and ML
control libraries used at both plants were identical.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 37

Figure 4-14 Comparison of DO Values and Setpoints at the POWRP

4.4.2 Evaluation of Ammonia-Based Aeration Controller
As part of this project, criteria were developed to evaluate the performance of ammonia control systems.
Assessing ammonia control performance is not a trivial task due to several factors. First, ammonia removal
depended upon SRT and DO concentration. The SRT setpoint was determined based on multiple factors,
such as sludge settling and foam avoidance, rather than solely on ammonia removal efficiency. The target
SRT was maintained at a constant value during the evaluation of the ammonia controller to ensure
consistency. Second, evaluating the ammonia control system presented challenges because the
manipulated variables, DO and airflow, were clamped. When DO or airflow reached their upper or lower
limits, ammonia removal is no longer influenced by the ammonia control system.

Reliable and efficient ammonia control required precise control during underloaded conditions and quick
adaptability during rapid load increases, such as those typically observed in the morning (Figure 4-15).

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 38

Figure 4-15 Typical Diurnal Pattern of Incoming Ammonia Loading

Based on historical data analysis, a decision was made to exclude instances when DO reached its
maximum or minimum values, as well as data within one hour of these events when assessing the
accuracy of the ammonia controller during underloaded conditions.

The agility of the ammonia controller was evaluated using data from within the hour preceding the time
at which DO reached its maximum value. Data used in the evaluation of the ammonia controller was
collected every 2 minutes.

The utilized accuracy indicator for ammonia control performance is a convenient tool for benchmarking
ammonia controllers. During underloaded conditions, the average absolute error during underloaded
conditions was 0.23 mg/L, representing 10 percent of the set point, with a standard deviation of 0.2 mg/L and
a 95th percentile value of 0.57 mg/L. During the hour when the maximum DO limit was reached, the average
absolute error was 1.2 mg/L, with a standard deviation of 1.54 mg/L and a 95th percentile value of 5.07 mg/L.

This agility performance indicator could be applied to evaluate ammonia controllers at other plants,
provided the following limitations are addressed:
 The indicator must be scaled to account for changes in incoming ammonia loads, as controlling
 ammonia under rapidly fluctuating loads is significantly more challenging than during gradual increases.
 The indicator should be scaled to DO limits, as tighter DO limits can impact control error, although
 stricter upper control limits may result in energy wastage.

DO/Nmaster demonstrated high precision, providing an opportunity for the reduction or even complete
elimination of aqueous ammonia addition required for chloramination. Daily average ammonia
concentrations, maintained under constant ammonia and SRT targets, displayed remarkable consistency
(Figure 4-16), showcasing the accuracy and robustness of the DO/Nmaster control. Composite samples
analyzed in the laboratory revealed no significant statistical differences in ammonia concentrations at the
end of September and the beginning of October, given the analytical method's standard deviation of
0.04 mg/L per Standard Methods (2023).

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 39

Figure 4-16 Typical Composite Effluent Ammonia Concentration

Ammonia control at the POWRP was more precise than at Chico WPCP due to the following factors:
1. Improved accuracy of DO, ammonia and airflow controllers, as previously discussed.
2. Installing ammonia meters in each aeration compartment enables compartment-specific ammonia
 control rather than limiting control to one compartment per tank.
3. Superior accuracy and repeatability of the ammonia meters used at the POWRP.

4.4.2.1 Sensitivity of Ammonia Removal
POWRP's permit includes seasonal ammonia limits of 3.0 mg/L from April through September and
3.9 mg/L during the rest of the year. During the relaxed NPDES limit timeframe, the ammonia setpoint was
increased from 1.4 mg/L (Figure 4-17) to 2.5 mg/L in hopes of decreasing airflow demand which would
reduce energy consumption. Surprisingly, the median DO increased by only 0.03 mg/L (six times smaller
than the standard deviation of the PID APG-Neuros controls), while median ammonia increased from
1.3 mg/L to 2.6 mg/L (see Figure 4-16A, 4-16B). While the cause of the high sensitivity of ammonia
removal to DO at levels just slightly below 1 mg/L warrants additional investigation, these data highlight
the critical importance of ammonia controller accuracy for operation at DOs below 1 mg/L.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 40

Figure 4-17 Effluent Ammonia and DO Concentrations at ABAC Setpoint of 1.4 mg NH3-N/L

Figure 4-18 Effluent Ammonia and DO Concentrations at ABAC Setpoint of 2.5 mg NH3-N/L

4.4.3 Evaluation of SRT Controller
The accuracy of the SRT controller was evaluated by comparing the SRT target with actual SRT, which was
calculated by SRTmaster using online TSS measurements in real-time and stored information.

Typical performance of the SRT control is depicted in Figure 4-19. The average of absolute control errors
was 0.13 days or less than 2 percent. SRT control at the POWRP demonstrated precision comparable to
that observed at other facilities where SRTmaster is installed (Ekster, 2016). Accurate SRT control allowed
for better adaptability of biomass to changing DO levels and timely adjustments to SRT set points when

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 41

biomass performance declined. Notably, even a less than 10 percent change in the SRT set point often led
to noticeable improvements in activated sludge performance.

Figure 4-19 Typical Performance of the SRTmaster at the POWRP

Comparison of the POWRP activated sludge system's performance with and without SRTmaster (Figure 4-20)
shows that automatic SRT control by SRTmaster improves sludge settleability. This chart demonstrates that
while SRTmaster could not prevent the annually observed seasonal deterioration of settleability during the
coldest time of the year, automatic SRT control using SRTmaster yielded faster recovery of settleability.

It is hypothesized that the sludge settleability improvements were improved through the reduction in
sludge age variability. As shown in the year-over-year comparison in Figure 4-20, the standard deviation
of SRT in February exceeded 14 days when sludge age was controlled manually, while the standard
deviation of SRT was less than 1.5 days when controlled by SRTmaster.

Stdev - Standard Deviation; DOave - Average DO

Figure 4-20 Comparison of Manually Controlled and SRTmaster-Controlled SVIs During Same Time of Year

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 42

Considering that manual SRT control typically results in an average error of 20 percent, it can be argued
that precise automated SRT control is essential for the robust operation of any activated sludge system,
particularly BNR systems operating at low DO levels.

4.4.4 Low DO Operations
After confirmation that the controllers would meet the required repeatability and accuracy for ammonia and
DO setpoints, the plant was operated with SRTmaster, DO/Nmaster with ABAC at an aSRT of 6 days, maximum
ammonia of 5.5 mg N/L throughout operations, and a maximum DO per zone of 3.0 mg/L at the starting
January 2024 and eventually reduced to as low as 0.8 mg/L in Summer 2024. This was important to obtain
operator buy-in that the controller was accurate and reliable. Important steps in getting team buy-in included
providing simulations of the new controller performance based on current diurnal cycling, understanding
boundary conditions in which the plant was comfortable operating (i.e., minimum aSRTs, maximum and
minimum DOs, etc.), and outlining a systematic reduction of DO setpoints using ABAC controls to reduce the
likelihood of plant upsets and ensure regulatory compliance. As the project went on some of the original
boundary conditions (i.e., minimum DO) were lowered as operators got more comfortable with the results.

4.4.4.1 Approach to Start-up and Transition to Low DO
To ensure the transition to low DO operations was successful, several strategies were identified
and followed:
 System adjustments to lower DOs (2 to 2.5 mg DO/L) were started in one basin at a time.
 The maximum DO setpoint in Grid B was set at 3.0 mg/L and gradually lowered as the process
 adapted to the current DO setpoints.
 The maximum DO setpoint in Grid C was set at 1.3 mg/L and gradually lowered as the process
 adapted to the current DO setpoints.
Maximum ammonia concentrations in the ABAC control were allowed adjusted between 4.5 to 6 mg N/L
depending upon operational phase to help put selective pressure on the nitrifying community while
balancing effluent quality.

System adaptation was defined as either no change in effluent quality with a DO change within 4 days,
meaning that the process adapted without issues, or at least three SRTs if the system showed some upset
or variation from status quo immediately after the DO was lowered. These criteria largely applied to
changes in Grid B performance, as discussed below and shown in Table 4-6.
Table 4-6 Startup Timeline Prior to ABAC Controls and Adaptation to SBNR

New Blowers Online April 1, 2023 June 1, 2023

4.4.4.2 DO Controller without ABAC
Because an ABAC controller with ammonia setpoints would not necessarily place the pressure needed to
adapt the biomass to low/suboxic DO, DOs were reduced to suboxic conditions by lowering the DO
setpoint in both Grid B and Grid C without the ABAC controller to force the DOs lower (Figure 4-21). This
was also consistent with the approach used in the pilot study work and the City of Pueblo's wastewater
plant. Although this initially appeared to be promising, the process struggled to adapt to the large swings
in ammonia loading conditions, as seen in Figure 4-22. In addition, the POWRP experienced several rain
events which increased hydraulic loadings and washed out aeration solids. It became obvious that the
project team needed to try another approach to deal with the rapid daily increase in ammonia loading.

Figure 4-21 Tank 3 DO Concentrations Under PID Control

Figure 4-22 Tank 3 Ammonia Concentrations Under PID Control

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 44

4.4.4.3 ABAC with DO constraints (Maximum/Minimum Handcuffs)
For the low DO transition, ABAC was implemented using ammonia setpoints as the primary controller and
DO setpoints as a secondary controller loop. True ABAC control over 24 hours would have resulted in high
DOs during the peak ammonia loading period of 8:00 a.m. to 2:00 p.m. The DO setpoints are maximum
aeration setpoints during this peak period to force the system to overall lower operational DOs over
11 phases, as summarized in Table 4-7. This amounted to a compromise with respect to effluent
ammonia, allowing more ammonia at peak without severely impacting the daily average.

Figure 4-23 illustrates average DO and effluent ammonia concentration for all phases, along with the
DO setpoints in Grids B and C during those phases. During Phases 1 through 6, adaptation was rapid
above 1.1 mg DO/L, requiring between 2 to 7 days (0.3 to 1.2 aerobic SRTs, or aSRTs). For DOs below
1.0 mg/L, a minimum of three aSRTs were required between DO changes for consistent system
performance and adaptation.

A combination of ABAC controls on ammonia and limiting the DO operating range allowed the POWRP to
maintain ammonia discharge limits at the levels comparable to before the low DO through the transition
(Figure 4-23). This excellent performance was achieved at the same aSRTs (6.5 days) and total SRTs
(9 days), demonstrating that process capacity was not compromised by low DO operations. At the same
time, aeration energy consumption was reduced from 1,300 kilowatt-hours per million gallons (kWh/MG)
influent baseline to 550 kWh/MG influent (Figure 4-24), well below the industry standard. In fact, replacing
the 50-year-old centrifugal blowers with high-efficiency blowers reduced blower energy consumption by
34 percent. Reducing DO setpoints beyond that has added an additional 30 percent blower energy savings.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 45

Figure 4-23 Average DO and Effluent Ammonia Concentrations by Phase and Grid Setpoint

The ABAC system allowed the process to respond quickly to changing DO demands due to the sudden
daily influx of total Kjeldahl nitrogen (TKN) and ammonia. However, due to the low SRT setpoint, the
ABAC controls were not allowed to reduce ammonia bleed-through to less than 6 mg/L during the four to
six hours of high influent TKN loading each day. Another interesting finding is that the plant can control
the effluent ammonia to an accuracy level that will significantly reduce the amount of supplemental
ammonia required for tertiary chloramination.

POWRP achieved consistent low DO operations in Phases 8 to 10 (i.e., averaging less than 1 mg DO/L in
all aeration zones). At the beginning of June, DO was reduced to 0.8 mg/L. Within a week, effluent
ammonia was increased from 1 to 2 mg/L to more than 3 mg/L. During the same time energy demand
was reduced from 700 kW/MG to 750 kW/MG to less than 600 kW/MG. To avoid NPDES violation DO was
increased to 0.9 mg/L and then to 1 mg/L and maintained at this level for more than 2 weeks. Ammonia
removal recovered and, during the last week of June, DO decreased again to around 0.85 mg/L. The rest
of the study ammonia was maintained less than 2 mg/L most of the time, never exceeding 3 mg/L.
DO/Nmaster maintained average DO less than 1 mg/L, while energy demand was further reduced
reaching as low as 500 kW/MG.

Total Nitrogen Removal
Figure 4-25 illustrates that TIN concentrations did not vary significantly before or after the low DO
transition, with before and after low DO transitions averaging 84 percent removal based on influent
ammonia concentrations only (and not including TKN). This was largely due to the plant being able to
maintain similar ammonia concentrations in the effluent even though the operational DOs decreased to a
daily average of 0.75 mg/L.

 Addressing Ammonia Breakthrough
Figure 4-29 demonstrates that high concentrations of ammonia enter the plant (along with higher flow
rates) between the hours of 8:00 a.m. and 2:00 p.m., daily. Consequently, at the given SRT, Grid B does not
have enough HRT and/or DO (Figure 4-30) to reduce the diurnal peak, causing ammonia bleed-through
into Grid C and the effluent from the plant. Thus, it could not fully counteract the diurnal peak. To
counteract the diurnal daily spike, POWRP allowed the DOs in Grid B to go to1.3 mg/L from 8 a.m. to
2 p.m. As shown in Figure 4-29b, this resulted in the diurnal peak in Grids B and C being reduced and
preventing ammonia bleed-through to the secondary clarifier.

Another cause of elevated ammonia concentrations was inconsistent loading between tanks (Figure 4-31).
The consistently higher concentrations coming from Tank 3's Grids B and C indicate that there was
significantly higher loading to that tank. The inconsistent load is due to the position of the two influent
sewers lines with respect to the primary clarifiers; while gates can be repositioned to improve the balance
of loading between tanks, the higher load to Tank 3 also drove the need for increased aeration during
peak ammonia loads.

SVI Performance
Figure 4-32 shows that SVIs increased during the transition to low DO, averaging between 197 and
231 mL/g during the low DO transition in Phases 1 through 6. While the plant generally has higher SVIs in
winter (Figure 4-33), higher SVIs were prolonged during the low DO transition. Once the process ran
consistently at low DO during Phases 9 and 10, SVIs averaged 129 to 135 mL/g. Several phenomena likely
contributed to the improvement including higher temperatures and biomass acclimation.

Figure 4-34 shows the results of microfilaments analysis before and after the low DO transition. The
results include the type and abundance of each filament ranging from 0 to 6 with 0 being none and 6
being excessive. The figure also shows the corresponding tank and effluent ammonia concentrations, DO
concentrations, and average SVI. The abundance of microfilaments does not increase as DO is lowered in
each phase of the project. The most abundant microfilament types remained the same as DO was
decreasing and effluent ammonia is at its lowest when DO is at its lowest. The increase in SVI during
Phases 1 through 8 is not reflected in the filament results, and once DO gets below 1 mg/L, SVI returns to
its original level of below 150 mL/g.
