4.4.5 Swing Zone Considerations
Although the aeration tanks included a swing zone that could be used as either an aerated zone or anoxic
zone, historically the swing zone was used only as an anoxic zone. The swing zone was designed for
seasonal changes and not intermittent/diurnal operation. Since ceramics diffusers allow water to enter
piping when air is shutoff, intermittent/diurnal operation with ceramic diffusers requires operators to
manually open the valves on the moisture blow-off piping to vent water from each swing zone diffuser
grid after each shutoff. The Sanitaire 9-inch ceramic diffusers were replaced with Sanitaire Silver Series II
membrane diffusers, which act like an integrated check valve. This allowed diurnal operation of the swing
zone and allowed intermittent operation of the aerated zone if needed to achieve suboxic/low DO
conditions. The Sanitaire Silver Series II membrane was selected since it was a fast changeout that
required no piping modifications and LACSD had the required stock at its facilities.

4.4.6 System Response to Perturbations
POWRP experienced several operational disturbances during the transition to and at SBNR operations.
Construction projects required taking Tank 1 and its corresponding clarifiers out of service starting
April 14. Tank 1 did not resume operations during the duration of this project.

There were also numerous hydraulic shutdowns of the plant. These shutdowns resulted in either
operational interruptions or solids loss including:
 A temporary shutdown of the effluent channel and one clarifier on April 26, 2024.
 RAS line taken down for construction on September 25, 2024.
 A plant shutdown on October 11, 2024.
 DO/Nmaster was offline due to communications issues October 18-23, 2025.

These events often led to spikes in effluent ammonia which were caused by lower levels of nitrification,
which had to be countered with higher DO setpoints in the system. However, the DO setpoints were
temporarily increased for 3 to 7 days, and the process was returned to SBNR operations with immediate
acclimation of the biomass.

For example, as shown in Figure 4-35, proceeding the biomass loss event on April 26, daily ammonia
spikes from Grid C steadily increased from 1.2 mg N/L to as high as 8 mg N/L while DO was maintained at
1.1 mg/L. To ensure effluent discharge compliance, the plant increased maximum DO setpoints to as high
as 2.4 mg/L for 1 week to immediately suppress the peaks. After 1 week, DOs were reduced to pre-
April 26 levels with immediate adaptation of performance.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 54

Figure 4-35 Tank 3 Grid B DO and Secondary Clarifier Effluent Ammonia Concentrations During and After Biomass Loss
 Event in April 2024

Storm events in Winter 2024 resulting in solids wash out and may explain higher cold weather SVIs and
shallow 10-foot deep, rectangular secondary clarifiers with sludge hopper located on the effluent end of
the clarifiers, designed for significantly lower pre-NDN MLSS concentrations, no polymer/settling aids.
LACSD has projects in the works to test hydrocyclone performance and modification of secondary clarifier
– changing longitudinal launders to transverse launders and adding baffles that will be tested in 2025.

4.4.7 Kinetics
Five kinetic tests were performed as part of this study to determine if there were impacts to the following
parameters due to low DO operation:
 Declining DO Test for half saturation constant, KDO.
 Substrate Utilization Test. Substrate utilization tests were run to determine the maximum nitrification
 rates for the activated sludge to demonstrate whether this parameter adjusted to lower DOs as well.
 Maximum Denitrification Rate Test. Denitrification rate tests were run to determine whether low DO
 operation had any impact on denitrification rates, positive or negative.
 Phosphorus Release Test. It was thought that low DO operation might allow phosphorus removal in
 plants that were not designed for phosphorus removal. Kinetic tests for phosphorus release were run
 to characterize the phosphorus removal kinetics before and during low DO operation.
 Maximum Nitrifier Growth Rate Test. The last test that was conducted was a nitrifier maximum specific
 growth rate test. This test was used to determine whether there are any inhibitors in the wastewater
 that are affecting the growth and thus reaction rate of the nitrifiers in the plant.

The complete kinetics report is attached in Appendix D.

4.4.7.1 Declining DO Test
The declining DO test was one of the two main methods in the literature to measure KDO. This test was run
the most often and was perhaps the most important. This test documented changes in the nitrification
reaction as a function of the operating DO in the system. The hypothesis was that KDO would decrease as
the DO changes the activated sludge floc morphology adapted to lower operating DO.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 55

The declining DO method used on this project is a variant of previous methods (Guisasola 2005)
appearing in the literature and was created by Kyle Malin at HRSD for use on this project (Malin 2021).
The basic procedure involves aerating activated sludge in a small tank until the DO reaches a high level
(>6.0 mg/L) and then cutting off the air and logging the decline in DO over time. The oxygen uptake rates
(OUR) were calculated from the DO decline. OURs are measured for endogenous respiration, nitrite
respiration, and nitrification. The data was fitted to a Monod equation to calculate the DO half saturation
coefficient. The detailed steps and equipment utilized for the procedure appear in Appendix D. The half
saturation parameter can be calculated from the data in Excel via least squares type analysis. For this
project, the parameters were calculated using an R-script written by Malin for use at HRSD. This script was
used by the LACSD for this project in order to be consistent with HRSD's methodology used.

A compilation of the Declining DO test data can be found in Appendix D. Figure 4-36 is a box and whisker
plot which depicts the DO half saturation results for the project. The data indicates a decrease in KDO as
the DO dropped to about an average of 1.0. Decreases in the average DO below 1.0 did not seem to
change the KDO at all. Perhaps the difference between 1.0 and 0.8 were not significant enough. It should
also be noted that the DO at POWRP is not constant; in ABAC control, the DO varied throughout the day.
In 2023 the plant was operating one zone at high DOs (>3.0) with the second zone at low DOs (<1.0);
again the average may not be an adequate descriptor of the activated sludge conditions.

Figure 4-36 Full-Scale KDO as Function of Operational DO

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 56

4.4.7.2 Substrate Utilization Test
The substrate utilization method (Ghimire 2012, De Mulder 2014, Keene 2107) involves measuring the
nitrification rates in a small reactor at four to six DO setpoints. The DO must be maintained at the exact
DO set point for 30 to 60 minutes to measure the nitrification rates. This is done by removing and
analyzing samples for NH3, NO3-, and NO2- concentrations. The DO setpoints used would vary from zero
to three to four mg/L. There are several challenges to this method, including maintaining exact DO
concentrations in the reactor. The nitrification rates at the DO setpoints were fit to a Monod curve, and a
best fit procedure is used to calculate the half saturation coefficient. The detailed procedure appears in
Appendix D.

While the original plan was to run this test parallel to the Declining DO test, this test was too difficult to
run simultaneously, very time consuming, and difficult to obtain results that were reproducible; thus,
fewer of these tests were run. The data is presented in Appendix D. The variability of the data seemed in
excess to the normal variability of the lab procedures and the poor DO control, and the cause of this
problem was undetermined. There were very few runs that yielded usable data at all DO setpoints, as
shown in Figure 4-37. More typical were runs such as shown in Figure 4-38, where data at one or more
DO setpoints did not appear reasonable. In this test, the removal rate at about a DO of 1.0 mg/L appeared
unreasonably high.

Figure 4-37 Example of Low Variability in KDO Using Substrate Utilization Test

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 57

Figure 4-38 Example of High Variability in KDO Using Substrate Utilization Test

4.4.7.3 Maximum Denitrification Rate Test
The maximum denitrification test is a batch procedure similar to that described in "Experimental Methods in
Wastewater Treatment" (IWA 2016). Activated sludge was placed in a small tank and spiked with COD
(acetate), nitrate, and nitrite. Samples were taken from the reactor and the nitrate and nitrite concentrations
were measured over time. The NO2-, NO3-, and total NOx denitrification rates can be calculated from this data.

The maximum denitrification test was run to determine whether there would be any changes in this rate due
to low DO operation. This was not likely to occur unless there was a shift in microbial populations that
resulted in poorer denitrification. While there was some variability in the test results, there did not appear to
be any impact on denitrification with respect to low DO operation. The test results are listed in Appendix D.

4.4.7.4 Phosphorus Release Test
The phosphorus release test is a batch procedure similar to that described in "Experimental Methods in
Wastewater Treatment" (IWA, 2016). Activated sludge was placed in a small tank and spiked with COD
(acetate) in an anaerobic condition. Samples were taken from the reactor and the COD and phosphorus
concentrations were measured over time. Phosphorus release rates can be calculated from this data.
These rates gave some indication as to the activity of the PAO population. The detailed procedures are to
be found in Appendix D.

This test was done to chart any changes in phosphorus removal characteristics by the plant as it
transitioned to low DO operation. The assumption for this test was that a MLE plant is not designed for
phosphorus removal and would have little phosphorus removal at high DOs; however, as the DOs were
lowered phosphorus removal, more bio-P may start to occur. However, the POWRP had 80 to 85 percent

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 58

phosphorus removal at the beginning of the project. Evidently, the small anaerobic mix zone in
combination with the anoxic zone was able to grow and sustain a population of PAOs. This test measured
phosphorus release under anaerobic conditions and measured how much COD was needed. The data in
Appendix D shows significant variability, and the test was only run six times since it did not appear to be
useful with respect to documenting low DO transformation at POWRP.

4.4.7.5 Maximum Nitrifier Growth Rate Test
The last test that was conducted was a maximum nitrifier growth rate test. There are several different
methods for this test found in "Methods Wastewater Characterization for Modeling Activated Sludge"
(IWA, 2003). The test procedure utilized in this project was the "High Food to Mass Ratio (F:M)," which
started with a very low MLSS content in a reactor spiked with ammonia. The ammonia concentration was
such that the nitrifiers growth rate was theoretically at maximum. Results were measured over the course
of a week. Curve fitting of the ammonia oxidation over the 7 to 9 days allowed estimation of the growth
rate by curve fitting. The detailed procedures are also found in Appendix D.

This test is used to determine the nitrifiers growth rate. This test was run four times during the project.
Two of the test runs had pH control issues and the results were not calculated. The other two runs
resulted in values of 0.69 and 0.55 mg VSS/mg NH3/day. While this is a fairly simple test to run, past
results from other plants seem to indicate substantial variability for test-to-test results. The results for
POWRP could be interpreted to indicate some level of inhibition; additional testing runs would be needed
to verify these values. BioWin model runs used to calibrate the plant data have used 0.8 and had
reasonably good agreement with the plant data.

4.4.8 Conclusions
The default half saturation value for DO used in Biowin is 0.7 mg/L. These test results Indicate that, at high
DOs, this value could be greater than 1.0 and, at DOs at or below 1.0 mg/L, the values were between 0.7
and 0.8. Thus, running the activated sludge at lower DOs had no clear impact on maximum nitrification
rates, denitrification rates, and phosphorus removal rates.

4.5 Design Considerations

4.5.1 Controls
Controlling DO in activated sludge is difficult in plug flow processes for several reasons including process
response is nonlinear, reactions rates and biological responses are slow (i.e., on the orders of minutes to
hours versus chemical reaction rates that are at the seconds scale), and site-specific factors can vary quite
a bit. Based on the instrumentation and controls upgrades implemented at POWRP, several design
considerations were identified including:
 Controls response. Many factors diminish controls response in aeration basins. Wastewater
 characteristics like loading, temperature, and total dissolved solids can DO solubility and microbial
 activity as well as diurnal and seasonal changes.
 Operating characteristics. Include HRT and SRT that the facility is comfortable operating at.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 59

 System components. Aeration basin configuration including separation walls, and diffuser layout,
 density, and condition can impact how quickly DOs change in a zone.
 » Process monitoring and components directly affect control response. These are your probes, how
 many you have, where they are located, and their accuracy and response time.

A variety of control approaches are currently used for low DO operations with varying control
performance (from DO setpoint control, ammonia versus nitrate (AvN) control, ABAC, intermittent
aeration and model predictive or machine learning control).

There are several parameters to consider regarding controls and instrumentation. Required accuracy,
precision, and repeatability of measurements will vary depending upon the controls scheme used. For
example, POWRP found that lower accuracy (±0.1 mg DO/L) Hach LDO luminescent probes were
adequate for ABAC controls. However, it is likely that a higher accuracy probe like an optical DO sensor
(±0.05 mg DO/L) would be beneficial for DO setpoint control. Another important parameter is response
time, or how quickly the sensor reacts to changes in the system or the time to reach equilibrium with the
true value.

Instruments with high accuracy and repeatability were specified at POWRP, as shown in Table 4-8.
Although selected this project specifically, the chosen equipment and controls led to highly accurate
control and energy; however, specification information is not intended to suggest that these instruments
are required to operate low DO or suboxic systems nor to endorse specific equipment. The information
can serve as a benchmark for facilities that are interested in achieving a high level of process control. This
information table can provide those interested in low DO operations with something to compare to when
looking at options and speaking with manufacturers.
Table 4-8 Instruments with High Accuracy and Repeatability Specified at POWRP
 Parameter Air Flow Meter DO Probe(1) Ammonia Probe(1) TSS Probe(1)

(1) A self-cleaning feature is recommended.

4.5.2 Instrumentation
Required instrumentation is, in part, dependent upon the type of controls system operated. For POWRP,
ammonia and DO probes were essential for ABAC controls. However, the nitrate probe was not required
for the controls process and was instead used for mechanistic understanding of biological processes. TSS
sensors are required for SRT control but not for aeration controls. Any sensor recommendations should be
weighed against the type of controls system used.

Sensor location and numbers are an important consideration in low DO operations. Full system
monitoring is the most ideal; in this case, one DO probe per aeration zone and multiple ammonia probes
per aeration basin. DO requirements will vary throughout the day depending upon influent ammonia and

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 60

COD loading. Installing multiple probes per aeration tank provides design and operational guidance
based on DO profiles during low to high loading periods, the ability for early detection of high and low
loads which impact airflow to the system and confirm process performance. Full monitoring allows
operators to see profiles across the length of each basin, and this can be important for some facilities with
low nutrient limits who also have uneven flow/load split between their basins. However, this amount of
instrumentation can be costly in terms of capital and O&M.

An example of why multiple probes is useful was the identified load imbalance at POWRP. The extensive
instrumentation used at POWRP highlighted the uneven load split and how one basin was impacting the
overall performance. The daily ammonia concentrations from the aeration zones are lowest between
4:00 a.m. and 8:00 a.m. and highest between 10:00 a.m. and 2:00 p.m. However, daily loadings are
somewhat different from weekday to weekend and from week to week (i.e., weekend versus weekend and
weekday versus weekday). Another important trend is that the loading in Tank 3 was higher than in the
other two tanks, as identified by the green solid and dotted lines (i.e., ammonia in Tank 3 Zones B and C)
being larger by as much as 5 mg/L than the other tanks.

Many facilities opt to have one DO probe per aeration tank due to capital and ongoing maintenance
costs. Although this is the most inexpensive option when compared to full monitoring, it does not allow
operators to see the DO profile across the basin or how it changes diurnally or weekly. The risks
associated with only one probe are over aeration, inefficient energy use, and DO sags which can lead to
incomplete nitrification. Avoiding sags in DO becomes even more important when operating at low
DO/suboxic conditions.

As a compromise, facilities may consider hybrid monitoring, meaning full monitoring in some basins is
used as a guide for control in the other aeration basins. This could be a good solution for facilities that
have relatively even flow/load split to balance costs and performance but may not be a good fit for
facilities with uneven flow/load split between their basins, like POWRP.

Sensor location is critical for accurate aeration control. For example, POWRP utilized ISE ammonia probes,
and those probes have less accurate measurements below 1.0 mg N/L. Thus, the probes need to be
located where there is constantly sufficient ammonia for detection. Under ABAC control the ammonia is a
setpoint and can be controlled to always be above 1 mg N/L. The ammonia probes were also prone to
reliability issues when located in the influent channel to the aeration basins due to electrode fouling.

Other instrumentation takeaways include:
 When operating in ABAC control mode, ammonia sensor repeatability is critical, especially at low
 ammonia levels.
 While some controls systems like AvN require nitrate sensors, they were not as useful for DO setpoint
 and ABAC operations. However, nitrate sensors can provide insight into mechanistic processes
 including simultaneous nitrification/denitrification.
 Phosphorus monitoring might be beneficial to some plants when operating low DO since some plants
 can slip into Bio-P even if they are not designed for it which can lead to struvite issues. This
 monitoring can be using online instrumentation or through composite sampling.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 61

4.5.3      Blowers
One critical consideration for blower selection and system design for low DO/suboxic operation is the
required turndown. For perspective, a typical blower has a maximum turndown of around 50 percent.
While a well-designed system with multiple blowers can achieve a system turndown of 80 percent or
higher. Facilities interested in retrofitting a process are not going to be immediately operating at low DO
and will need to be able to be able to provide sufficient airflow for high and low DO conditions in their
aeration basins. Some facilities in colder climates may even consider alternating from lower and higher
DOs seasonally as a strategy to combat nitrification challenges at low temperatures. Each of these factors
affect the required blower turndown at a specific facility.

The success of this project heavily relied on the use of highly accurate and precise instrumentation. The
DO/Nmaster controller, developed by Ekster and Associates, necessitated the integration of high-
performance field devices to ensure optimal performance. The combination of Kurz airflow meters and
Rotork CVQ actuators allowed precise control required for DO/Nmaster. Additionally, the DO/Nmaster
system's advanced control capabilities demanded more precise control of the blowers and valves than
traditional systems. The blower output needed to be adjusted at one-second intervals, which is three
times faster than typical control methods. This rapid adjustment capability allowed for more responsive
and accurate control of the aeration process, ensuring optimal DO levels in the treatment basins. Similarly,
the valve positions are adjusted every 3 seconds, up to ten times faster than traditional control systems.
This high-frequency adjustment facilities precise control over the flow and distribution of air, ultimately
leading to more efficient and effective wastewater treatment. However, controller response time was not
optimized in this project.

4.5.4 Aeration Piping and Valves
For low DO operation, aeration piping and metering is critical, especially in facilities with inconsistent flow
to different aeration basins. DO/Nmaster was programmed to adjust total airflow to the demand of each
grid while also adjusting valve position to allow the target airflow to each header. This helped the system
maintain the exact airflow demand by basin and provide almost instantaneous changes in valve position
to provide target airflow as demand changed.

The industry paradigm is that iris and jet valves provide better accuracy, repeatability, and larger
operating ranges for low DO operations, while butterfly valves have low sensitivity to control. However,
the POWRP system demonstrated that butterfly valves can work well in low DO systems with a compatible
actuator, fast flow meter, and ML-control system. For example, POWRP installed butterfly valves with
electric actuators to control air individually to each grid and, in spite of a limited operating range, the
valves could control within 6 percent of DO setpoint while maintaining tight control.

4.5.5 Actuators
Airflow valve actuator accuracy and response is important for accuracy around a DO setpoint. The project
concluded that high accuracy actuators like the electric actuators used at POWRP can have a very low
time delay of less than 0.1 seconds, rapid response time and high position accuracy as shown in Table 4-9
are critical if butterfly values are used for airflow control. The unlimited movement specified for the valve
actuators was critical to stay ahead of the change in airflow requirements diurnally.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 62

4.5.6 Diffusers
Ceramic and fine bubble diffusers can be used for low DO operations, and the choice will likely be based
on site preference. Plants can use ceramic diffusers if constant airflow operations are anticipated.
However, ceramics are not likely compatible with intermittent DO aeration from zero to a setpoint since
counter diffusion of water through the ceramics can cause moisture in the aeration header. For situations
where intermittent DO is anticipated, membrane diffusers are recommended. Information on the efficacy
of other aeration technologies like combined aerator-agitator systems for low DO is unknown.

Other diffuser design considerations and operations flexibility alternatives for low DO that should be
considered prior to implementation include:
 Include designing for more than one aeration grid per zone to individually turn grids on and off to
 meet minimum-to-peak aeration conditions.
 The impact of taking basins in and out of service on aeration calculations and reporting.
 Consider diffusers that allow for higher flow ranges.

4.5.7 Other Operational Strategies

Swing Zone Operation
Swing zones will allow facilities, like POWRP, that experience significant increases in diurnal ammonia and
COD loading to reduce load entering the aeration zones. In POWRP's case, the zones were only capable of
providing up to 0.5 mg DO/L, which provided only minor mitigation of daily high ammonia loads (8 a.m.
and 1 p.m.). POWRP observed no negative impacts on denitrification. While the efficacy of swing zones for
load reduction is not known, having swing zones allowed operations to be more comfortable with low DO
transition and operation.

Continuous versus Intermittent DO Control
WRRFs employ a variety of controls strategies around targeting operational DO, including continuous
setpoint targeting and varying the DO target either spatially (i.e., some aeration zones have higher DOs
they can operate than others) or temporally (i.e., targeting higher DOs in one more aeration zones during
high influent loading). Each of these controls strategies are valid approaches to operating at low DO. As
mentioned in "Addressing Ammonia Breakthrough" section, POWRP opted to implement a temporal
increase in the upper DO limits from 9 AM to 3 PM to address the high ammonia loads that enter during
the morning hours.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 63

POWRP converted purposely from ceramic to membrane diffusers to allow for flexibility in aeration. While
not required for low DO operation at POWRP, membrane diffusers would allow the plant to briefly turn off
aeration without saturating the membranes, whereas ceramic diffusers are more likely to have water
permeate through the diffusers and leak into to the aeration header. This may be an aeration strategy for
facilities that do not have adequate turndown but can provide sufficient mixing to keep biomass
suspended in the mixed liquor.

required), nitrate sensors (not required) and high-quality level of permanent outside conduit and piping
associated with the instruments and controls (not required but delays longer term capital investments). In
addition, LACSD was already planning on replacing their 50-year-old centrifugal blowers for more efficient
blowers. Based on this information, it is estimated that $2,000,000 of the costs were associated with
essential operations for SBNR.

SECTION 5 GHG EMISSIONS
The primary object of off-gas testing was to assess if there was any difference in N2O emissions between
typical aeration operating conditions (i.e., 2.0 mg/L) and low DO operations. This was assessed at the pilot
and full-scale plants. Off-gas testing was performed by DRH2O unless otherwise noted. The pilot at HRSD
was tested on August 2, 2022, at a target DO of 2.0 mg/L and on August 30, 2023, at low DO (<0.4 mg/L).
At POWRP, the system was tested at an operational DO of 2.0 mg/L on June 9, 2023, and a low
operational DO that ranged between 0.6-1.3 mg/L on November 4, 2024. The complete reports from off-
gas testing are included in Appendix E.

5.1 Pilot-scale GHG Results from the Virginia Initiative Plant
The pilot reactors at VIP in Norfolk, VA were tested on August 2, 2022, at a target operational DO of
2.0 mg/L and August 30, 2023, at a target DO of 0.3 mg/L following the American Society of Civil
Engineers (ASCE) Standard Guidelines for In-Process Oxygen Transfer Testing (ASCE, 2018). The testing
was performed in five of the aerated reactors in series (T202-T206). Reactors 2 through 5 are aerated with
one 9-inch ethylene propylene diene terpolymer (EPDM) fine-pore membrane diffuser mounted on each
reactor floor. The off-gas hood was placed next to the mixer in Reactors 2 through 5.

Details of the sampling procedure are included in Appendix E. Briefly, gas was collected in Tedlar bags
connected to an off-gas analyzer that passed the gas through a Drierite-containing column to remove
water vapor and sodium hydroxide pellets to eliminate carbon dioxide (CO₂). This process resulted in the
collected gas consisted only of nitrogen, oxygen, and trace gases, including N₂O. To mitigate potential
issues during sample shipping, two types of collection bags made from different materials were used,
ensuring at least one sample per reactor would remain intact.

Table 5-1 shows the flow-weighted results for all aeration performance and emission metrics for both the
high and low DO tests, except for DO. Average process data from the pilot is also included.

The fixed instrumental error in oxygen gas measurement was approximately ±20 percent due to the small
size and shallow depth of these pilot reactors (30 inches of submergence). Therefore, caution is advised
when interpreting the calculated oxygen transfer efficiency (OTE) values (OTE and aSOTE). Air fluxes also
displayed different values between the measured hood flux and the process air flow meter. Hence, flux
measurements obtained from both the hood and the process air flow meters are included in Table 5-1.

In terms of direct N2O and equivalent CO2 emissions, the results from the two tests indicate that emissions
are lower when the DO set point is low (averaging 0.22x). This finding supports the project's hypothesis
that suboxic operations provide environmental benefits without leading to increased N₂O emissions.

5.2 Full-Scale GHG Results from POWRP
For the first testing campaign, the reactors had been operating at target DO concentrations of 2.0 mg/L
using a DO-setpoint control system in all zones. The diffusers were in operation for approximately 6 to
8 months. This testing campaign focused on sampling off-gas from the first and second aeration zones
of Tank 1.

The second off-gas testing campaign was conducted after the reactor had stabilized at lower DO
concentrations, varying from 0.5 to 1.3 mg/L as controlled by the ABAC system. At the time of testing,
Tank 1 was offline for maintenance, so sampling was conducted in Tank 3 instead. For this testing
campaign, two different off-gas testing hoods and analyzers were deployed—one by DRH2O and another
by Carollo's Inflatable Fast and Lightweight Off-Gas Analysis Technology (I-FLOAT™). The DRH2O-led
testing campaign sampled the swing zone (which was active at the time) as well as the first and second
aerobic zones (Figure 5-1). The I-FLOAT™ hood was placed in a fixed position in the first aerobic zone
(Zone 2) to monitor temporal variations in aeration and N₂O metrics.

Flow-weighted N₂O emissions from the two testing campaigns by DRH2O suggest slightly lower emissions
in Tank 3 under low DO conditions. However, differences in operational conditions—such as Tank 1 being
offline and variations in aeration control—make direct comparisons challenging.

Tank 3 exhibited a more balanced airflow distribution between its first and second aerobic zones
compared to the testing performed in Tank 1 in June 2023. While the overall reduction in N₂O emissions
was modest, the observed aeration and emission metric profiles were across Zones 2 and 3 were different
between the two testing campaigns.

N₂O flux estimates from the I-FLOAT™ measurements, though based on only two hours of data in Zone 2,
also indicated lower emissions. These findings suggest that low DO operation may help reduce direct N₂O
emissions from aeration tanks, likely due to reduced stripping rates at lower air flux levels.

5.3 A Comparison of Pilot and Full-Scale Emissions Testing
Due to differences in process configuration and geometry, direct comparisons between HRSD and LACSD
results are challenging. Based on these results, we have concluded low DO systems can have comparable
or lower N2O emissions than traditional, higher DO BNR processes.

N₂O flux at the VIP pilot is consistently lower than at POWRP in all tests. Possible reasons for the variation
include that HRSD was running an AO process versus MLE at POWRP and more complete mixing in the
pilot system may lead to fewer microclimates which may promote N2O production. Another reason for
lower emissions in general at HRSD may be that the air flux from the pilot is significantly lower than at
PWRF. Finally, sampling locations with low DO (<0.50 mg/L) show lower N₂O emissions in both processes,
while locations with high DO (>0.50 mg/L) consistently exhibit higher N₂O emissions, regardless of their
position in the process, suggesting a correlation between high air fluxes and high N2O flux rates.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 68

SECTION 6 MICROBIAL COMMUNITY ANALYSIS
6.1 Relevance
Shifts in microbial communities can be investigated using metagenomic tools. One common approach is
to quantify the relative abundance of a phylogenetic marker gene, such as the 16S ribosomal ribonucleic
acid (rRNA) gene (via 16S rRNA amplicon sequencing). This method can reveal which microorganisms are
enriched and which are diminished or lost in a suboxic nitrogen removal system. However, the resolution
of this approach is limited: relying on a single gene often makes it difficult to distinguish between closely
related microbial strains. For example, it cannot reliably differentiate PAOs that are capable of
denitrification from those that are not.

A more informative alternative is whole-community deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) sequencing, which
captures the full genetic content of the microbial community. Bioinformatics are then used to assemble
draft genomes for individual community members. If high-quality, near-complete genomes are recovered,
this approach allows for detailed analysis of the presence or absence of specific functional genes within
the dominant organisms—providing much deeper insight into the metabolic capabilities and ecological
roles of the community members.

The complete microbial communities analysis report is included in Appendix F.

6.2 Method Overview
Sample collection and preservation, DNA extraction, metagenomic sequencing, and data processing are
described in Beach et al. (2021). Activated sludge biomass samples were collected from both field sites
(pilot- and full-scale) before and after the transition to suboxic conditions (four total samples). Biomass
was centrifuged (with the supernatant discarded) and preserved at -80 degrees C. These samples were
later used for DNA extraction and whole genome sequencing to better understand the microbial
populations under both traditional (high DO) and suboxic/low DO conditions.

The standard platform for whole genome sequencing of microbial communities is the Illumina system,
which produces millions of short reads (typically ~300 base pairs). While widely used, genome assembly
from these short reads is often incomplete. Nonetheless, thousands of public draft genomes have been
generated from activated sludge using Illumina sequencing.

In this project, we proposed using the PacBio sequencing platform, which produces much longer reads—
typically 6,000 to 10,000 base pairs. These longer reads enable more complete and accurate genome
assemblies, allowing for clearer identification of the presence or absence of specific genes.

Using the project's allocated budget for metagenomic analysis, we collected four samples (two from the
pilot-scale site and two from the full-scale site) and submitted them for PacBio sequencing at the
University of Wisconsin Biotechnology Center (UWBC). These datasets were used to:
 Assemble high-quality, high-completeness genomes that represent members of the sampled
 microbial communities.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 69

 Assess the abundance of microbial populations by comparing the number of DNA reads that
 correspond to each assembled genome and to publicly available genomes from activated sludge.
 Release the highest-quality genome assemblies to public repositories to support the scientific,
 academic, and professional communities.
 Perform analysis of key functional populations involved in nitrification and phosphorus removal.

6.3 Results and Discussion
This study employed metagenomic sequencing to investigate how microbial communities in wastewater
treatment systems respond to reduced DO conditions. Samples were collected from LACSD's full-scale
POWRP and HRSD's pilot BNR facility during periods of high DO (>2.0 mg/L) and low DO operation.
Notably, the average low DO concentrations differed between the two utilities: HRSD maintained an
average DO of approximately 0.3 mg/L, while LACSD operated at a higher average of around 0.7 mg/L.
These differences in operational DO levels may have influenced the extent and nature of microbial
shifts observed.

Sequencing depth was significantly greater in low DO samples (24–60 billion bases) compared to high DO
samples (2 to 4 billion bases). However, this disparity is attributed primarily to methodological factors
rather than biological differences. According to Dr. Noguera, the early high DO samples were extracted by
a new graduate student with limited lab experience, and sequencing was multiplexed—meaning multiple
samples shared the same sequencing lane. In contrast, the low DO samples were extracted after the
student gained experience and sequenced individually. Although these factors do not fully explain the
depth gap, they are likely the primary contributors and highlight the importance of consistent methods
when comparing metagenomic data.

After quality filtering and dereplication, 304 unique metagenome-assembled genomes (MAG) were
recovered from LACSD and 207 from HRSD. Only 8 MAGs (LACSD) and 16 MAGs (HRSD) were shared
between high and low DO conditions, suggesting notable shifts in community structure that may be
associated with changes in DO levels.

Phosphorus Removal Populations at LACSD

At LACSD, Accumulibacter MAGs—key organisms responsible for EBPR—were recovered only in the high
DO samples. These MAGs were closely related to Accumulibacter propinquus, a well-characterized lineage
commonly found in EBPR systems. However, Accumulibacter was not detected in the low DO
metagenomes, despite higher sequencing depth. Notably, LACSD maintained stable phosphorus removal
performance throughout the transition to suboxic conditions (average DO ~0.7 mg/L), indicating that the
absence of Accumulibacter in the low DO metagenomes does not necessarily reflect a loss of function.

Several studies have shown that EBPR activity can be maintained at low DO, even when Accumulibacter
abundance appears low or undetectable in molecular datasets. This may be due to methodological
challenges, including biases in DNA extraction efficiency, especially from organisms with robust cell walls
or low biomass yields, as well as limitations in assembling high-quality genomes when community
complexity increases under low DO. Additionally, EBPR function can sometimes be carried out by
alternative organisms, such as Tetrasphaera, which has been identified in both lab-scale and full-scale
systems as a potential phosphorus-accumulating organism with distinct metabolic strategies

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 70

(Nguyen et al., 2011; Kristiansen et al., 2013). These alternative PAOs may not have been well represented
in the genome bins recovered from the LACSD samples.

Given these considerations, and the fact that phosphorus removal performance remained stable, we
conclude that no definitive shift in Accumulibacter populations can be inferred from the metagenomic
data alone. Further investigations—such as targeted gene quantification, transcriptomic analysis, or
enrichment-based studies—would be needed to determine whether Accumulibacter persisted at
undetectable levels or if other organisms assumed the PAO role under suboxic conditions.

Phosphorus Removal Populations at HRSD

At HRSD, Accumulibacter MAGs were recovered from both high and low DO samples. Interestingly, these
MAGs formed a phylogenetically distinct cluster, separate from the canonical A. propinquus lineage
observed at LACSD. This suggests the presence of alternative Accumulibacter subgroups, possibly adapted
to the more oxygen-limited environment at HRSD (average DO ~0.2 mg/L). Similar diversity in
Accumulibacter clades has been reported in prior studies, where different clades (e.g., Clade I and II)
exhibit varying preferences for carbon sources, electron acceptors, and oxygen availability (Flowers et al.,
2009; Camejo et al., 2016).

The ability of HRSD's EBPR population to maintain representation across both DO conditions is consistent
with literature demonstrating the metabolic flexibility of certain Accumulibacter subclades, including their
capacity for denitrifying phosphorus removal under low oxygen or anoxic conditions. Some genomes
have been shown to carry the full suite of genes for both polyphosphate accumulation and denitrification
pathways, supporting this functional versatility (Oyserman et al., 2016; Rubio-Rincón et al., 2019). The
detection of distinct Accumulibacter MAGs at HRSD may reflect a selection for lineages capable of
adapting to suboxic conditions, a finding with practical relevance for utilities seeking to optimize EBPR
under energy-saving aeration strategies.

Nitrifier Community Responses at LACSD

At LACSD, nitrification under moderately low DO conditions (~0.7 mg/L) appeared to be maintained
through conventional two-step pathways involving distinct AOB and NOB. Specifically, Nitrosomonas
MAGs—representing AOB that convert ammonia to nitrite—were recovered exclusively from low DO
samples. These MAGs formed a novel phylogenetic group, suggesting the enrichment of previously
uncharacterized Nitrosomonas populations adapted to suboxic environments. Prior studies have shown
that Nitrosomonas europaea and related lineages can persist at DO concentrations as low as 0.3 to
0.5 mg/L (Park & Noguera, 2007; Blackburne et al., 2008), supporting the capability of AOB function under
low DO conditions.

Additionally, a Nitrospira defluvii MAG—representing canonical NOB capable of oxidizing nitrite to
nitrate—was also recovered under low DO, confirming the presence of traditional NOB activity in the
system. The co-occurrence of both AOB and NOB under low DO at LACSD aligns with findings from other
full-scale WRRFs where step-feed or plug-flow designs have enabled successful nitrification at average DO
concentrations below 1.0 mg/L (Regmi et al., 2014; Wang et al., 2018). Relative abundance analysis
indicated that nitrifier populations remained relatively stable across DO levels, suggesting a resilient
nitrifying community structure under suboxic conditions.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 71

The absence of comammox MAGs at LACSD, despite their growing recognition in wastewater systems,
suggests that comammox organisms may not have been competitively selected under these moderately
low DO conditions—or may have been present at low abundance levels below the detection threshold.

Nitrifier Community Responses at HRSD

In contrast, the nitrifier community at HRSD, which operated at significantly lower average DO
concentrations (~0.2 mg/L), displayed a distinct pattern suggestive of a shift in nitrification pathway.
Under high DO conditions, Nitrospira MAGs closely related to N. defluvii were detected, consistent with
the presence of canonical NOB. However, under low DO, Nitrospira MAGs were dominated by Candidatus
Nitrospira nitrosa, a member of the comammox group—organisms capable of performing complete
ammonia oxidation (from ammonia to nitrate) within a single cell. Notably, Nitrosomonas MAGs were not
recovered from HRSD samples under either condition.

This pattern suggests a potential transition from conventional two-step nitrification to a one-step process
mediated by comammox under more extreme oxygen limitation. Similar shifts have been reported in both
lab-scale and full-scale systems where comammox organisms were enriched under low DO or low
ammonia concentrations (Roots et al., 2019; Camejo et al., 2017). Comammox Nitrospira are known to
have a high affinity for both oxygen and ammonia, making them particularly well-suited for energy-
limited or substrate-limited environments (Kits et al., 2017).

Relative abundance data further support this functional shift: under low DO, N. defluvii-like MAGs
declined while Cand. N. nitrosa emerged, indicating that comammox organisms likely displaced canonical
NOB and AOB. The lack of detectable Nitrosomonas could reflect true biological exclusion or
methodological limitations such as insufficient sequencing of low-abundance populations. Nevertheless,
the consistent detection of comammox under suboxic conditions at HRSD suggests that these organisms
may play an important role in enabling stable nitrification at DO concentrations near or below 0.2 mg/L.

6.4 Conclusions and Implications
Although methodological limitations (e.g., differences in sequencing depth and library preparation) must
be acknowledged, the observed differences between the two facilities demonstrate the influence of DO
levels, operational scale, and system configuration on nitrifier community structure. In total, the recovered
MAGs spanned over 20 phyla, including novel or poorly characterized members of Accumulibacter,
Nitrosomonas, and Nitrospira. These findings contribute to a growing body of research showing that
microbial communities in wastewater systems are highly adaptable and can support key treatment
functions through diverse pathways, including the incorporation of comammox into the nitrification
process (Lawson & Lücker, 2018; Pinto et al., 2016).
The divergent nitrification populations at LACSD and HRSD also have practical implications for the design
and operation of low DO BNR systems. Utilities targeting energy savings through reduced aeration may
inadvertently select for different microbial communities depending on the specific DO regime, carbon
availability, and reactor design. Understanding these community shifts is essential for predicting treatment
performance and optimizing process control in suboxic environments.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 72

In summary:
 Microbial communities showed distinct responses to high and low DO conditions, with limited overlap
 in recovered genomes and evidence of functional shifts in key nutrient removal populations.
 At HRSD, operation at lower average DO (~0.2 mg/L) was associated with a clear transition in nitrifier
 populations—from canonical nitrite oxidizers (Nitrospira defluvii) to comammox (Cand. Nitrospira
 nitrosa)—suggesting a shift toward complete ammonia oxidation under more oxygen-limited conditions.
 LACSD, operating at moderately low DO (~0.7 mg/L), supported a more stable two-step nitrification
 pathway involving distinct AOB (Nitrosomonas) and NOB (Nitrospira), indicating a resilient nitrifying
 community structure at full scale.
 Accumulibacter MAGs were detected only in high DO samples from LACSD but were present under
 both DO conditions at HRSD. Given that phosphorus removal performance at LACSD remained stable,
 the absence of Accumulibacter under low DO may reflect methodological limitations or the activity of
 alternative PAOs, such as Tetrasphaera. No definitive conclusions about Accumulibacter population
 shifts can be drawn from these data alone.
 The detection of novel Nitrosomonas and Accumulibacter MAGs and the emergence of comammox
 organisms at HRSD expand our understanding of microbial diversity and adaptation under low DO but
 also highlight the need for complementary methods (e.g., qPCR, transcriptomics) to confirm function.
 Differences in sequencing depth between samples were largely due to technical factors—such as DNA
 extraction experience and sequencing strategy—rather than biological responses to DO, underscoring
 the importance of standardized methods in comparative metagenomic studies.
 These findings reinforce the need for site-specific process control strategies and provide critical
 insights into how microbial communities adapt to low-energy, SBNR operation—supporting the
 optimization of treatment performance while reducing aeration energy demand.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 73

SECTION 7 LCA/TEA RESULTS
7.1 Background
As part of this project, periodic LCAs and TEAs are being completed on the POWRP and its transition to
suboxic operations. The scope of the TEA at the POWRP encompasses the entire secondary treatment
stage, including all equipment associated with the secondary treatment such as blowers, mixers, and
pumps (see Figure 7-1).

Figure 7-1 TEA Boundary Conditions

An evaluation of the baseline operations for the POWRP was completed as part of Task 1 of this project.
The baseline operation was defined as process operation in a representative period when the facility
operated with its previous blowers and under conventional high DO conditions, i.e., May 2021 to
October 2022.

This report summarizes the TEA during the period of (1) March 2023 when tuning of blowers and controls
from April to December 2023 at normal operating DOs > 2.0 mg/L and (2) the process operating at a DO
of 0.75 mg/L in all basins in September 2024. Key TEA metrics are energy, treatment performance, costs,
GHG emissions, control, and process stability.

The set of metrics used for the baseline TEA assessment encompasses operational conditions, electricity
consumption, treatment performance, costs, GHG emissions, control accuracy and stability.

7.2.1 Energy Use
The installation of the new turbo blowers was completed in March 2023, and the APG-Neuros blower
commissioned April 2023. There is a clear improvement in This decrease in DO was accompanied by a
progressive decrease in the specific energy use (kWh/MG) of the blowers (see Figure 7-2). The blowers
were tuned from April through August 2023. From September through December 2023, the controls were
tuned under DO setpoint control to determine the accuracy of the blower and aeration system control.
Starting January 2024, the DO targets in the aeration basins were gradually decreased starting from
2.5 mg/L. For more details on the strategy used to progressively lower the DO the reader is referred to
Chapter 4.

Installation of new blowers and diffusers reduced energy consumption by 34 percent in early 2023. In
March 2023, the older centrifugal blowers and ceramic membranes replacement was completed, with full
system commissioning of APG-Neuros turbo blowers and fine bubble diffusers occurring April 1, 2024.
This improvement was seen even though DO concentrations were higher than baseline conditions (3 to
4 mg/L). Following the initial tuning period, DO concentrations were gradually lowered, with a consequent
increase in energy savings compared to baseline conditions starting in April 2023. However, specific
energy intensity varies monthly and seasonally, with higher energy demands during warmer months. This
is primarily due to reduced oxygen mass flow rates from the blowers and lower oxygen transfer rates, as
the solubility of gases decreases at higher liquid temperatures. Therefore, when estimating the relative
energy savings, it is more appropriate to compare the relative energy savings on a month-by-month basis.
Figure 7-2 shows the volumetric energy intensity estimated during the baseline period and during August
2024. Figure 7-3 illustrates energy savings on a month-by-month basis. Except for the month of April, the
DOE energy savings target range of 30 to 50 percent was consistently achieved.

7.3 Economic Analysis
A financial analysis was performed as part of the TEA analysis. The purpose of this analysis is to present
the capital improvements that were required to provide the necessary operational flexibility to achieve
low-DO conditions. The savings in operational expenses (OPEX) that resulted from lowering DO
concentrations and air flow rates in the aerobic zones

7.3.1 Capital Improvements to Achieve Low DO Operation
Table 4-10 summarized the capital improvements required to provide the operational flexibility to low DO
in the aeration tanks of the POWRP. Not all upgrades were required for the project, including blanket
indicators (beneficial but not required), nitrate sensors (not required) and high-quality level of permanent
outside conduit and piping associated with the instruments and controls (not required but delays longer
term capital investments). In addition, LACSD was already planning on replacing their 50-year-old
centrifugal blowers for more efficient blowers. Based on this information, it is estimated that $2,000,000 of
the costs were associated with essential operations for SBNR.

7.3.2 Energy Cost Savings
The reduction in DO concentration resulted in a decrease in volumetric treatment costs, from $108/MG to
$48/MG, representing a 55.5 percent cost reduction (Figure 7-4). Assuming an influent flow rate of
8.4 mgd (baseline period) and a cost of energy of $88/MWh, yearly savings of approximately $184,000
were estimated.

7.3.3 Return on Investment analysis
Based on the capital investment to achieve low DO conditions at the POWRP and based on the estimated
$200,000 per year in electrical energy savings (at $0.08 per kWh), the estimated ROI is approximately
11 years. These estimates assume constant flows and constant electrical energy pricing signals. In
addition, based on the ability to control ammonia to a specific setpoint, LACSD's estimate up to $46,000
could be achieved by reducing or eliminating supplemental ammonia dosing for chloramination and
disinfection. This further lowers the ROI to 8.5 years.

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 79

SECTION 8 COMMERCIALIZATION
This project established DO/Nmaster as a commercializable technology for accurate control around
ammonia and DO setpoints. APG-Neuros developed a marketing plan and materials for integration of the
blower system and for low DO controls. Ekster and Associates developed marketing materials for the
controls system and is actively marketing DO/Nmaster and SRTmaster for low DO operations. Additional
information is in Appendix G.

APG-Neuros served as a critical commercialization partner for this project through the development of
their Ventura Control AI (VENTURA) system. Leveraging decades of expertise in aeration technology and
wastewater treatment optimization and a diverse team of controls, aeration, and blower experts,
APG-Neuros developed VENTURA to address the dynamic demands of secondary treatment processes,
providing substantial energy savings, improved effluent quality, and enhanced operational stability.
VENTURA employs sophisticated MPC algorithms that dynamically adjust DO setpoints based on
real-time ammonia concentrations and influent loading conditions information. Unlike traditional PID
control methods, VENTURA's MPC approach anticipates process variations, significantly reducing the
energy required for aeration while ensuring compliance with stringent effluent ammonia standards. The
system comprises three integrated control loops: ammonia-based DO setpoint optimization, airflow
control to meet DO targets, and precise valve positioning for optimal airflow distribution. This aeration
level control is then directly coupled to the control of the blower output. This technology was later
implemented at Metro Water Recovery's Northern Treatment Plant (NTP).

Based on the success of VENTURA at NTP, APG Neuros has been working diligently to lead the
commercialization effort of this technology. Eight individual WRRFs spread over the country are in various
stages of installing or operating VENTURA systems including two additional LACSD facilities (Los Coyotes
and San Jose Creek Water Reclamation Plants).

DOE DE-EE0009509 TRANSFORMING AERATION ENERGY IN WATER RESOURCE RECOVERY 80

SECTION 9 NATIONAL SURVEY
The scope of this work was expanded to include a performance evaluation of low DO facilities across the
United States. The goal of this analysis was to identify facilities that are running low DO, identify their
configurations, and determine any trends and commonalities in performance. The results of this analysis
are limited since there was not sufficient budget allocated to the data analysis.

9.1 Methodology
As part of this analysis, two rounds of survey were performed. Data requested in each survey round are
summarized in Table 9-1. Twenty-nine facilities across the United States and in Europe were asked to
contribute data to the initial survey. If the facility demonstrated low DO operations in all or part of its
aeration operations, it was asked to contribute additional data as part of the second survey.

The initial survey demonstrated that only one facility in the United States ran fully suboxic, i.e., both
spatially and temporally. Based on this, a second survey with additional questions was sent to facilities for
clarification on their operations. All data was anonymized except for any POWRP data shown in this
section.

9.2 Results
The survey of nine facilities found that facilities of a variety of different configurations are either believed
they were performing or are performing partial or full low DO or SBNR operations, including fully suboxic
to partially suboxic based on spatial or temporal demands and CSTRs, i.e., oxidation ditches versus plug
flow (PFR) configurations like MLE, AO, anaerobic-anoxic-oxic (A2O), and 4- or 5-stage Bardenpho
configurations. The details of data provided and operations are summarized in Table 9-2. The results of
the survey are present in Figures 9-1, 9-2, and 9-3. In these graphs,
 The dots represent individual data points.
 The box represents 25 and 75 percentiles.
 The solid and dotted lines in the boxes represent the average and median, respectively.
 The whiskers represent 5 and 95 percentiles.

POWRP data is not reflected in this survey.

Figure 9-1 shows that, in general, plants in the survey operated at average DOs less than 1.0 mg/L.
However, the effluent performance of all plants varies significantly regardless of type of process (PFR
versus CSTR and subcategories within) and where and when low DO is being operated. For example,
plants C, E, G, and H achieved average effluent ammonia concentrations less than 1.0 mg N/L. CSTRs
generally achieved low effluent ammonia (less than 1.0 mg N/L except for facility I) and TP (less than
1.0 mg/L) concentrations. PFR reactors had more varied performance, with effluent ammonia ranging
from 0.2 to 3.5 mg N/L and TP below 1.0 mg P/L. Regardless of configuration, SVIs were diverse, ranging
from 90 to 200 mL/g.

Ultimately, the national survey that was started demonstrated that plants across the country with different
process configurations can perform low DO operations; however, different sites define low DO differently
and approach implementation in a variety of ways. Low DO operations are largely dependent upon
regulatory requirements and other needs of the site.

APPENDIX A HAMPTON ROADS SANITATION
 DISTRICTS PILOT-SCALE TESTING FINAL
 REPORT

Summary of Pilot Performance

Pilot testing objectives
This section outlines the outcomes of the pilot-scale investigation conducted at the Pilot
Testing Facility. Pilot testing was a critical aid in addressing knowledge gaps,
establishing kinetic relationships, determining optimal strategies for microbial
acclimation to low dissolved oxygen (DO), and validate operational hypotheses. Key
hypothesis of interest were the kinetic adaptation of nitrifiers, denitrifiers, and PAOs to
suboxic operation, and the stability of the process at these conditions.

The research objectives focused on evaluating the adaptation of SNR under steady
operation to suboxic conditions over the course of the testing period. To do this data
were collected on effluent quality (carbon, nitrogen, and phosphorus removal) and
stability, operational parameters, kinetics, settling, microbial community shifts, and GHG
emissions. This pilot work and resultant data was then used for assessing energy
savings potential, calibrated process modeling, assessing organic carbon demand and
diversion potential, and informing aeration control strategies for optimal SNR.

Pilot Process Layout
The HRSD BNR Research Pilot, located at the HRSD VIP WRRF in Norfolk, Virginia,
USA, consists of continuous flow primary and secondary treatment processes fed raw
wastewater from the VIP WWTP influent. The raw influent goes through grit removal,
screening (2mm), primary clarification, and equalization, and was temperature
controlled to 20°C. The primary effluent from this process is then fed to the secondary
BNR treatment process comprised of 6 completely stirred tank reactors (CSTRs) in
series. The pilot BNR process is operated as an A/O configuration with a single
anaerobic reactor followed by five fully aerobic reactors (273 liters each), a secondary
clarifier (276 liters), and a final effluent equalization tank (19 liters). The forward flow of
the process is held constant at 3.8 lpm, as was the RAS. The BNR process has an HRT
of 7.2 hours and the aerobic SRT is maintained via direct wasting at 6.5 – 9 days. A
photograph showing the layout of the pilot process is given in Figure 1.

 1
Oral Exam
Kester McCullough

Figure 1 Photograph showing the layout of the entire pilot process. Flow process from preliminary treatment to
primary treatment to the BNR process. (ANA = anaerobic zone, AER = aerobic zone)

Each CSTR has a top entry mixer for constant and complete mixing, a single membrane
diffuser for fine bubble aeration, and a variety of sensors. The anaerobic reactor has a
floating cover to minimize air transfer through the liquid surface. Each aerobic reactor
has a DO probe (Insite Model 10, LA, USA) and is held at the same DO setpoints at
each step of the DO reduction phase of the experiment. This constant DO setpoint is
achieved using PID feedback loops to control airflow via five separate Aalborg
(Orangeburg NY, USA) thermal mass flow controllers. pH is measured at the midpoint of
the process (HACH, Loveland CO, USA) and sodium-bicarbonate is added to the
process to maintain a pH of 6.8 or greater. Two ISE ammonia probes (YSI VARiON,
Yellow Springs OH, USA) are installed in the process in the first two aerobic reactors.
Control and data collection is integrated with the full-scale process Distributed Control
System integrated with the Emerson Ovation Electronic Data System (EDS) (Ferguson,
MO, USA). Extensive sampling is done to monitor low DO adaptation and pilot
performance. Raw Influent, Primary Effluent, and Final Effluent 24-hour composites
were taken 5 times per week along with daily grab samples. Composites samples were
used to generate process performance data; grab samples were used for
measurements such as SVI, filamentous organism counts, and floc size measurement
via laser-diffraction. Kinetic batch testing was conducted to measure the oxygen kinetics
of nitrifiers, PAOs, and denitrifying OHOs at each steady-state DO setpoint as the
system was adapted to low DO.

 2
Oral Exam
Kester McCullough

Results
The pilot study demonstrated the feasibility of operating a biological nutrient removal
system under low DO conditions, achieving complete nitrification, excellent enhanced
biological phosphorus removal, and stable settleability. Process stability was evident
through robust nitrification and phosphorus removal, consistent settleability, and low
filament counts, despite variations in floc size distribution. The adaptation of nitrifiers to
low DO, highlighted by a reduced oxygen half saturation coefficient, underscores the
microbial community's capacity to adapt to low DO while maintaining complete
nitrification and excellent ammonia removal performance. These findings highlight the
potential for energy-efficient low DO operation strategies, maintaining treatment efficacy
while leveraging microbial adaptation to optimize performance.

Operations
The pilot was successfully transitioned to low DO conditions by incrementally
decreasing the DO concentration in the aerobic zone from 2.0 mg DO/L to 0.2 mg DO/L
over 19 months. This was done over across 11 set points, while maintaining a
consistent aerobic solids retention time (SRT) of 8.5 days. The pilot operated at a DO of
0.2 mg DO/L for over 3 months. The pilot achieved stable nitrification at each DO set
point which resulted in an average ammonia removal of 99 ± 4%, with minimal process
upsets throughout the transition to low DO. At pilot DO set points of 2 mg/L and 0.2
mg/L, the average percent phosphorus (P) removed was 94 ± 7% and 90 ± 11%,
respectively. Although there is a minimal decrease in average P removal, the decrease
is not statistically significant. These results suggest that excellent EBPR can be
achieved under low DO operations, even in an A/O system with RAS returning nitrate to
the anaerobic zone. The reduction in operating DO from 2.0 mg/L to 0.2 mg/L
corresponded to a 39% reduction in airflow required for the process (68.0 lpm to 41.5
lpm).

Settleability, Filaments, and Floc Size
Settleability was maintained throughout the transition to low DO, with settled sludge
volume index (measured at 30 minutes) consistently ≤ 100 g/L. Filamentous bacteria
counts remained low during the study. These findings demonstrate that settleability can
be maintained under low DO conditions. Throughout the operating period floc size
measurements fluctuated and ranged from 50 to 350 µm with a lower average size of
about 75 µm, the changes and variability in floc size did not appear to impact
settleability (e.g., SVI).

Nitrifier Kinetic Adaptation
Nitrifier adaptation was observed in both in-situ and batch tests. Complete nitrification
was achieved at each DO set point and the ammonia uptake rate observed in-situ did
 3
Oral Exam
Kester McCullough

not decrease meaningfully. The average nitrifier oxygen half saturation coefficient
decreased from 1.4 mg DO/L to 0.20 mg DO/L as the pilot operating DO was reduced
from 2.0 mg/L to 0.20 mg/L. These results indicated that even in a controlled
environment, with consistent temperature, SRT, and flow/load, a change in oxygen half
saturation coefficient could be induced by a reduction in operating DO. Additionally, the
reduction in nitrifier oxygen half saturation coefficient demonstrates that nitrifiers were
capable of achieving a higher affinity for oxygen throughout the adaptation period while
the maximum ammonia removal rates remained relatively constant.

Denitrifier Kinetic Adaptation and SND
As the pilot transitioned to low DO, heterotrophs also appeared to adapt to low DO
conditions, similar to nitrifiers. SND (TIN removal across the aerobic zone) was
expected to improve under low DO conditions, however, steady and minimal SND was
observed. At an operating DO set point of 2.0 mg/L, SND contributed an average of
22% of TIN removal, which unexpectedly decreased to 20% at 0.2 mg/L, but this
difference was not statistically significant. This suggests that heterotrophs adapted
similarly to how nitrifiers adapted, with a reduction in oxygen half saturation coefficient.
Because SND is a function of heterotrophic oxygen half saturation coefficient and
operating DO, the constant SND observed in this study as the operating DO was
lowered suggests that there was a reduction in heterotrophic oxygen half saturation
coefficient. It is relevant to note that mixing energy remained approximately the same at
low DO, relative to high DO, with mechanical mixing throughout the aerobic zone. The
pilot reactors were well-mixed, reducing the likelihood of anoxic pockets that could drive
SND. This differs from full-scale operations, where less consistent mixing may create
pockets of anoxic zones within an aerobic basin that could facilitate SND.

 4
Oral Exam
Kester McCullough
