Nitrifier Growth Rates

Nitrifier growth rates are a critical parameter for BNR system design and for obtaining reliable modeling
results. Conventional approaches to BNR operation are to operate the aeration basins with DO
concentrations of 2.0 mg/L or higher to increase nitrifier growth rates and reach optimum nitrification
performance (Metcalf & Eddy, 2003). However, several studies have demonstrated complete nitrification in
systems with low oxygen (Hanaki et al.,1990; Park & Noguera, 2006; Camejo et al., 2016; Keene et al., 2017)
with some reporting that a subset of the nitrifiers had been enriched at the low oxygen conditions (Belluci et
al., 2011; Fitzgerald et al., 2015).
The data obtained will test one of the key hypotheses of this study (that the nitrifying community will
acclimate to low DO over time with a higher affinity for oxygen [half-saturation constant, Ko, decreases]

while the maximum nitrifier growth rate (µmax) remains constant). If this hypothesis is true, this means that
capacity is not impacted and longer SRT is not required compared to conventional DO operation.
We aim to evaluate and compare the nitrifier growth rate at steady-state high-DO conditions (traditional
operation) and steady-state suboxic conditions. The data will not only test a key hypothesis of this study but
also guide process model calibration.

Three different approaches to quantify the maximum nitrifier growth rates in activated sludge systems
include the low F:M test, the high F:M test, and the washout test (WERF, 2003). Each method is described in
detail in the WERF Methods for Wastewater Characterization in Activated Sludge Modeling which describes
each of these methods in detail (WERF, 2003).

In summary:

 • Low F:M test allows for distinction between AOM and NOM growth rates but is labor intensive and
 requires long-term (~6 weeks) batch reactor operation.
 • High F:M test can be completed in 5-7 days and is the simplest procedure but is not recommended
 due to high sensitivity to inconsistencies.
 • Washout test can be completed in approximately 5-7 days but does not differentiate between
 nitrifier growth rates and is more complex in terms of reactor operation and maintenance.
A final decision on which of the three test methods will be performed at the pilot and full scale sites is still
outstanding and pending additional team investigations.

Method references
WERF (Water Environment Research Foundation) (2003) Methods for wastewater
characterization in activated sludge modeling. Project 99-WWF-3, ISBN 1-893664-71-6. Alexandria, Virginia.
Carollo Internal Projectwise Link: IWA-Methods-WW-Characterization-AS-Modeling.pdf
Foundry Link: https://foundry.openei.org/167/references/literature?resources[]=5225755
Oxygen half-saturation constants and maximum oxygen uptake rates

The oxygen half saturation coefficient and maximum oxygen uptake rate (OUR) are critical parameters for
nitrification process design and estimating aeration requirements for BNR systems. In process models, these
parameters have a direct impact on the nitrification capacity.
Batch tests will be performed using activated sludge from both field sites (pilot- and full-scale) before and
after microbial community adaptation to low DO conditions to determine and compare the following:
 1. Apparent oxygen half-saturation constant (Ko) for nitrifiers (ammonia oxidizing and nitrite oxidizing
 microorganisms) and ordinary heterotrophs responsible for endogenous respiration
 2. Maximum oxygen uptake rates (OURmax) for nitrifiers and ordinary heterotrophs responsible for
 endogenous respiration, and maximum specific OUR (calculated by dividing the OUR by the VSS
 concentration in the batch experiment.)
This data obtained will test one of the key hypotheses of this study (the nitrifying community will acclimate
to low DO over time with a higher affinity for oxygen [half-saturation constant, Ko, decreases] while the
maximum nitrifier growth rate (µmax) remains constant.

It is important to note that mixing, floc size, and site-specific operating conditions can affect nitrification
kinetics. Greater oxygen and nutrient diffusion limitations are attributed to poor mixing conditions and large
floc sizes (Manser et al. 2005). In addition, greater OUR of activated sludge biomass can also impact
observed nitrification rates due to greater oxygen limitations in the activated sludge floc (Stensel et al.
1993). Therefore, floc morphology will also be analyzed from the activated sludge samples (see Section 9.0).
Refer to Section 7.4 for the nitrate utilization rate (NUR)-tests that should be performed in parallel with the
OUR-tests in order to estimate the reduction factor for the biomass respiration activities under anoxic
conditions in comparison with aerobic conditions.

 Two different approaches to determine oxygen half-saturation coefficients for nitrifiers are the
 Substrate Utilization method and the Declining DO Method. The Substrate Utilization method is the
 most common method for measuring half-saturation constants (van Loosdrecht et al., 2016), however,
 this method is labor and time intensive. The Declining DO method saves significant time and effort and
 can generate comparable results to the Substrate Utilization method in addition to providing the
 oxygen uptake rates for nitrifiers and heterotrophs responsible for endogenous respiration (Malin,
 2021). For this reason, the Declining DO method will be utilized to determine the oxygen half saturation
 constants and maximum oxygen uptake rates for nitrifiers and ordinary heterotrophs. These tests will be
 performed using activated sludge from both field sites (pilot- and full-scale) before and after acclimation
 to low DO. The detailed description of the Declining DO method is described in Malin, K. (2021).
Method references
 Malin, K. (2021). Development of Kinetic Parameterization Methods for Nitrifying Bacteria using
 Respirometry (M.S. Thesis).
 Carollo Internal Projectwise Link: 2021_Malin_KG_Thesis_Development-of-Kinetic-Parameterization-
 Methods-for-Nitrifying-Bacteria-using-Respirometry.pdf
 Foundry Link: https://foundry.openei.org/167/references/literature?resources[]=5217189

 van Loosdrecht, M. C., Nielsen, P. H., Lopez-Vazquez, C. M., & Brdjanovic, D. (Eds.). (2016).
 Experimental methods in wastewater treatment. IWA publishing.
 Carollo Internal Projectwise Link: Experimental-Methods-in-Wastewater-Treatment.pdf
 Foundry Link: https://foundry.openei.org/167/references/literature?resources[]=5225758

Reduction factor for heterotrophic denitrification (not performed)

Denitrification design is primarily based on influent BOD5 and kinetics of heterotrophic biomass. Modeling
the denitrification process often uses aerobic kinetics, multiplied by a reduction factor which reflects the
decreased activated sludge activity under anoxic conditions. Parallel respirometry tests under aerobic (OUR-
test discussed in Section 7.3) and anoxic conditions (nitrate utilization rate (NUR)-tests) can be conducted to
evaluate the reduction factor for endogenous respiration. The results provide and assessment of the
denitrification potential of the activated sludge.

NUR tests are used to characterize denitrification kinetics. NUR-tests performed in parallel with the OUR-
tests discussed in Section 7.3 allow for an estimation of the reduction factor for the biomass respiration
activities under anoxic conditions in comparison with aerobic conditions. These tests will be performed using
activated sludge from both field sites (pilot- and full-scale) before and after acclimation to low DO using the
method described by van Loosdrect et al., 2015. The reduction factor for endogenous respiration rate can be
calculated by comparing the measured specific endogenous respiration rates from corresponding OUR- and
NUR-tests, using the following relation:
 2.86*NUREND/OUREND

 Where:
 2.86 = oxygen equivalent of nitrate-N in oxidation-reduction reaction (mgCOD/mgN )
 NUREND = anoxic endogenous respiration rate
 OUREND = aerobic endogenous respiration rate
Method references
 van Loosdrecht, M. C., Nielsen, P. H., Lopez-Vazquez, C. M., & Brdjanovic, D. (Eds.). (2016).
 Experimental methods in wastewater treatment. IWA publishing. (PDF pages 189-190)
 Carollo Internal Projectwise Link: Experimental-Methods-in-Wastewater-Treatment.pdf
 Foundry Link: https://foundry.openei.org/167/references/literature?resources[]=5225758
Specific Nitrification Rate (SNR)

In low DO/suboxic systems, nitrification and denitrification can occur concurrently in the same reactor with
low DO; this phenomenon is referred to as simultaneous nitrification and denitrification (SND) (Bertanza,
1997; Helmer and Kunst, 1998). If SND occurs via the nitrite pathway (also referred to as the nitrite shunt), it
not only saves energy through the low DO operation but also save approximately 40% of the organic matter
required for denitrification when compared to typical nitrification and denitrification processes (Turk and
Mavinic, 1986). The specific nitrification rate (SNR) test it used to evaluate the ratio of nitrate and nitrite
produced to determine if NOB are repressed and assess if the nitrite-shunt is occurring in a system.

The SNR test will be performed using activated sludge from both field sites (pilot- and full-scale) before and
after acclimation to low DO using the method described by Dold et al., 2015.
The SNR test produces a profile of the nitrogen species shown in Figure 6. Capturing the nitrite peak during
this test is key since nitrite is related to ammonia oxidation, nitrite oxidation, and denitrification. This
evaluation can also help guide modeling efforts and take some uncertainty out of adjusting model
parameters during calibration (Dold et al., 2015).

Figure 6 Ammonia removal and oxidized nitrogen species produced over time in SNR test
            (Dold et al., 2015).

Method literature review
Dold, P. et al. (2015). Is Nitrite Shunt Happening in the System? Are NOB Repressed?. WEFTEC 2015
Proceedings.
Carollo Internal Projectwise Link: 2015_Dold et al- Nitrite Shunt.pdf
Foundry Link: https://foundry.openei.org/167/references/literature?resources[]=5225758

Denitrifying PAO Activity Test

Denitrifying PAO (DPAO) simultaneously reduce nitrate or nitrite while taking up phosphorus into their cells.
Enrichments of denitrifying PAO (DPAO) have been frequently reported in low-DO BNR systems (Camejo et
al., 2016; Flowers et al., 2009; Kim et al., 2013; Oehmen et al., 2010; Zeng et al., 2003). Camejo et al.
demonstrated the greatest P uptake when nitrate and oxygen were simultaneously present in a bench-scale
activated sludge system and proposes these PAO may contribute to SND within the aerobic zone of low DO
systems (Camejo et al., 2016). A simple anoxic DPAO batch activity test can confirm if DPAO activity is
present in an activated sludge sample.

This test will be performed using activated sludge from both field site before and after acclimation to low
DO. The method for detecting anoxic phosphorus uptake by DPAO has been described in Keene et al. 2017
and simultaneous oxygen and nitrate uptake by DPAO in Camejo et al. 2016.

For anoxic detection of P-uptake, activated sludge can be collected from the middle of the anaerobic zone
(where the greatest P-release is observed) and maintained anaerobic during transport and testing. Once in
the laboratory, air is not supplied during denitrification experiments. Instead, either sodium nitrite or sodium
nitrate are introduced to the system to create initial nitrite and nitrate concentrations of approximately 20
mgN/L. Samples are collected over a 4-h period to determine if P-uptake is observed in the presence of
nitrite or nitrate.
Method references
Camejo, Pamela Y., Brian R. Owen, Joseph Martirano, Juan Ma, Vikram Kapoor, Jorge Santo Domingo,
Katherine D. McMahon, and Daniel R. Noguera. "Candidatus Accumulibacter phosphatis clades enriched
under cyclic anaerobic and microaerobic conditions simultaneously use different electron acceptors." Water
research 102 (2016): 125-137.

anaerobic-microaerobic-conditions.pdf

1 INTRODUCTION

APG Neuros (APG Neuros) provided the following scope of supply to LACSD as part of the WERF project:
 1. NX700D-C060 High Speed Turbo Blower
 2. 9 butterfly type air control valves with actuators
 3. Blower Master Control Panel
 a. Includes aeration control for 9 aeration control zones
 b. Includes interface to DO/Nmaster system
APG Neuros stands out as a premier choice for providing blowers and controls expertise due to its innovative
technology, proven efficiency, and extensive industry experience. Founded in 2005, APG Neuros has been a
pioneer in introducing high-speed turbo blower technology to the wastewater treatment markets in North
America, Western Europe, and the Middle East. Their turbo blowers are known for their energy efficiency,
reliability, and low maintenance, making them an ideal partner for projects focused on energy savings.
One of the key benefits of APG Neuros’ turbo blowers is their ability to achieve significant energy savings. The
blowers can reduce energy consumption by up to 40%, which translates to substantial cost savings for large-
scale projects. Additionally, their products are environmentally sustainable, oil-free, and designed to minimize
operational costs. This combination of efficiency and sustainability aligns perfectly with the goals of the
project.
APG Neuros also offers several advantages over its competitors. Their blowers are equipped with advanced
aerospace technology, ensuring high performance and durability. The company has a strong track record with
over 1,900 units installed in North America and Europe. This extensive experience and proven success in
various industrial applications and wastewater treatment processes highlight their capability to deliver reliable
and effective solutions.
Moreover, APG Neuros is committed to continuous innovation and quality improvement. Their focus on
developing technologically advanced products and aeration solutions ensures that they remain at the
forefront of the industry. This dedication to innovation, combined with their comprehensive customer support
and service, makes APG Neuros a trusted and valuable partner on the project.

APG Neuros has extensive experience in designing and implementing master control panels (MCPs) and
control systems, particularly for aeration processes in wastewater treatment plants. Their MCPs are integral to
optimizing the performance and efficiency of high-speed turbo blowers, which are a critical component in
these systems.
The MCPs developed by APG Neuros are designed to provide precise control over the aeration process, which
is essential for maintaining the correct levels of dissolved oxygen (DO) in the treatment tanks. This control is
achieved through the use of advanced sensors, flow meters, and DO probes that continuously monitor the
system’s performance. The MCPs then adjust the operation of the blowers and modulating flow control valves
to ensure optimal aeration, which is crucial for the biological processes involved in wastewater treatment.
One of the significant advantages of APG Neuros’ control systems is their ability to handle varying loads and
conditions efficiently. The MCPs can adjust the blower capacity and air flow dynamically, which helps in
maintaining energy efficiency and reducing operational costs. This flexibility is particularly important in
wastewater treatment plants, where the demand for aeration can fluctuate significantly.
Moreover, APG Neuros’ control systems are designed with safety and reliability in mind. They include features
such as start-stop procedures for multiple blowers, surge control to prevent instability at low flows, and
various safety protocols to ensure smooth operation. This comprehensive approach to control and automation
not only enhances the performance of the aeration system but also extends the lifespan of the equipment.
In summary, APG Neuros’ expertise in master control panels and control systems provides significant benefits
in terms of energy efficiency, operational flexibility, and system reliability. These advantages make them a
preferred choice for large-scale energy savings projects and set them apart from their competitors in the
industry.

2 BLOWER SELECTION

The new high speed turbo blower for Pomona WRP was selected to match the performance of the existing
blower, Process Air Compressor (PAC) #1, it was replacing. LACSD provided APG Neuros a test report with
performance curves and tables with design points for the existing blower. After have reviewing the
performance test report on the blower, APG Neuros optimized the new blower design for maximum efficiency
with a wide flow turn down and the ability to vary the operating discharge pressure. A APG Neuros NX700D-
C060 turbo blower was selected with the following conclusions:
 1. The APG Neuros NX700D High Speed Turbo Blower meets and exceeds the five (5) testing operating
      points (14,995, 12,851, 10,715, 8,359 and 6,523 SCFM) in the test report at the five (5) test discharge
      pressure values (7.51, 8.15, 8.68, 9.03 and 9.17 PSIG).
 2. The APG-Neuros NX700D High Speed Turbo Blower can reach a maximum capacity of 17,241 SCFM and
 turndown to 2,625 SCFM at 68 °F ambient temperature at discharge pressure of 7.51 PSIG with a
 turndown capability of 84.8%.
 3. At an operating point of 12,851 SCFM and a discharge pressure of 8.15 PSIG, the APG-Neuros NX700D
 High Speed Turbo Blower is operating at 80% efficiency.

 4. At an operating point of 14,995 SCFM and a discharge pressure of 7.51 PSIG, the APG-Neuros NX700D
 High Speed Turbo Blower is operating at 78.5 % efficiency.
 5. The APG-Neuros NX700D High Speed Turbo Blower is 39.3% more efficient than PAC #1 blower at and
 operating point of 6,523 SCFM and a discharge pressure of 9.17 PSIG, 29.2% at 8,359 and 9.03 PSIG,
         24.7% at 10,715 SFCM and 8.68 PSIG, 21% at 12,851 SCFM and 8.15 PSIG and 16.3% at 14,995 SCFM
         and 7.51 PSIG.

3 VALVE/ACTUATOR SELECTION

The project required precise control of airflow to individual aeration zones in the activated sludge process. The
primary goal was to replace existing valves with more efficient and accurate control valves to enhance the
performance of the aeration system. The actuators had the following performance requirements:

 • Designed for continuous modulation
 • Valve travel position accuracy – The total maximum inaccuracy of the valve travel position due to any
 limitation, i.e. repeatability, deadband, resolution, hysteresis, etc. shall be equal or less than 0.2%.
 • Dead time < 1 second
 • Analog output of actuator position
 • The designed range of airflow (minimum to maximum) shall be achieved within less than 20 seconds.

3.1 Hardware Options

3.1.1 Butterfly Valves

Butterfly valves are widely used in wastewater treatment due to their simplicity, cost-effectiveness, and ease
of maintenance. They consist of a rotating disc that opens and closes to regulate flow. When the valve is fully
open, the disc aligns parallel to the pipe, allowing maximum flow. Butterfly valves are particularly suitable for

activated sludge operations because the required airflow typically falls within their linear range, providing
stable and predictable control.
One of the main advantages of butterfly valves is their economic benefit. They are generally less expensive
than other types of control valves, making them a popular choice in many wastewater treatment plants.
Additionally, butterfly valves are easy to maintain due to their simple design, which consists of fewer moving
parts compared to more complex valve types. However, a notable downside is that they require longer
straight runs of pipe upstream and downstream to ensure accurate airflow measurement. This is due to the
potential for turbulence caused by the valve’s disc, which can affect flow readings.

3.1.2 Egger Iris Valves

Egger Iris Valves use a unique mechanism involving interlocking segments to control flow. When the valve
opens, these segments retract into the casing, providing a centered and unobstructed flow path. This design
minimizes pressure drop and allows for precise control of high flow rates at low pressure drops. Iris valves are
particularly suitable for applications requiring energy-efficient control and can handle both liquids and gases,
including those containing particles.
The primary advantage of Iris valves is their ability to provide a linear flow characteristic, making them ideal
for applications where precise flow control is critical. The design ensures an even discharge, which is essential
in processes requiring uniform flow rates. Additionally, the flexible sleeve material used in Iris valves can
withstand various temperatures and pressures, enhancing their durability and performance. However, Iris
valves are generally more expensive than butterfly valves and may not be necessary for all wastewater
treatment applications.

3.1.3 Rotork CVQ Actuators

Rotork CVQ actuators are compact, electrically powered process control operators suitable for precision,
unrestricted modulation of most control valve types and sizes. They offer accurate and repeatable positional
control using a 4-20 mA signal, with digital communication options including HART®, Profibus®, and
Foundation Fieldbus®. The CVQ actuators are known for their high accuracy, reliability, and cost-effectiveness.
One of the standout features of Rotork CVQ actuators is their continuous unrestricted modulation duty. This
means they can operate continuously without the risk of overheating, making them ideal for demanding
applications.

3.1.4 Rotork IQ3 Actuators

Rotork IQ3 actuators are intelligent multi-turn actuators designed for isolation or regulating duties. They
feature a double-sealed enclosure, ensuring protection against water ingress and harsh environmental
conditions. The IQ3 actuators are equipped with non-intrusive local controls and a handheld setting tool,
allowing for rapid and secure commissioning and configuration.

3.1.5 AUMA Actuators

AUMA actuators are known for their versatility and reliability in various industrial applications, including
wastewater treatment. They offer a range of electric actuators that can be tailored to specific requirements.
AUMA actuators are equipped with intelligent controls that allow for precise operation and integration with
distributed control systems (DCS). These actuators feature a modular design, making them adaptable to
different valve types and sizes.
AUMA actuators come with advanced features such as position limit switches, torque switches, and local
control options. They are designed to withstand harsh environmental conditions, with excellent corrosion
protection and watertight enclosures. The actuators also support various communication protocols, enabling
seamless integration with existing control systems.

3.2 Selection Process

The selection process for the control valves and actuators was thorough and involved multiple stages of
evaluation and discussion among the project team. The primary goal was to identify a solution that would
provide precise control of airflow to individual aeration zones in the activated sludge process, ensuring
optimal performance and efficiency.

3.2.1 Initial Evaluation

The process began with an initial evaluation of the existing system and its limitations. The existing butterfly
valves were identified as needing replacement due to their inability to provide the required precision and
control. The team recognized the need for valves and actuators that could offer better modulation and
reliability to maintain optimal microbial activity in the aeration process.

3.2.2 Identification of Options

Several types of valves and actuators were considered during the selection process. The team evaluated
butterfly valves, Egger Iris valves, and various actuators, including Rotork CVQ, Rotork IQ3, and AUMA
actuators. Each option was assessed based on its technical specifications, cost, ease of installation, and
maintenance requirements.

3.2.3 Technical Discussions

The technical discussions focused on the specific requirements of the project. Key considerations included the
need for precise and accurate control of airflow, the ability to handle the required airflow range, and the
reliability of the valves and actuators under varying conditions. The team also considered the integration of
the actuators with the existing control systems and the potential for future upgrades.

3.2.4 Cost Analysis

Cost was a significant factor in the decision-making process. The team conducted a detailed cost analysis of
each option, considering both the initial purchase price and the long-term maintenance costs. Butterfly valves

with Rotork CVQ actuators emerged as a cost-effective solution compared to other options like Egger Iris
valves and actuators from AUMA and Rotork IQ3.

3.2.5 Performance and Reliability

Performance and reliability were critical factors in the selection process. The team reviewed the performance
data and reliability records of the different actuators. Rotork CVQ actuators were noted for their high
accuracy, and continuous unrestricted modulation duty. These features made the CVQ actuators particularly
suitable for the demanding conditions of the plant.

3.2.6 Final Decision

After a comprehensive evaluation of all the factors, the team selected butterfly valves equipped with Rotork
CVQ actuators. This decision was based on the cost-effectiveness, precision, and reliability offered by the CVQ
actuators.
The selection process was a collaborative effort involving detailed discussions, technical evaluations, and cost
analyses. The final decision to use butterfly valves with Rotork CVQ actuators was made with confidence that
this combination would provide a reliable and efficient solution for controlling airflow in the activated sludge
process, ensuring the optimal performance of the aeration system.

4 MASTER CONTROL PANEL

The blower master control panel (MCP) was supplied by APG Neuros and programmed by its subcontractor
SKM. The MCP supervises the turbo blower and optimizes its operation to meet process set points. It
integrates with the DO/Nmaster software to calculate optimal setpoints for dissolved oxygen (DO), airflow,
and valve positions, which are then used to adjust the blower and valve operations. The system supports both
local and remote control modes, allowing for flexible operation and monitoring.

4.1 Blower Control

The MCP operates the blower in various control modes:
 1. Manual Control - In manual control mode, the blower receives a Start/Stop command and Speed
 Command in percentage from the operator on the MCP HMI.
 2. Flow Control Mode – The MCP adjusts the speed of the blower to meet a total airflow setpoint. The
 total airflow setpoint is calculated from the sum of the airflow meters to the individual aeration zones.
 3. DO/Nmaster Control – The MCP receives a total airflow setpoint from the DO/NMaster and adjusts the
 speed of the blower to meet the setpoint

4.2 Aeration Control

The MCP can control the dissolved oxygen concentration in the individual aeration control zones using the
following modes:

 1. Cascaded DO Control – The MCP calculates an airflow setpoint required to maintain the DO at an
 operator set DO setpoint for each aeration control zone using a Proportional type controller. The
 airflow setpoint is cascaded to the valve control loop where a second Proportional type controller
 adjusts the position of the valve to meet the airflow setpoint.
 2. Constant Flow Mode - The operator enters a constant airflow setpoint for an individual aeration
 control zone. A Proportional type controller adjusts the position of the valve to meet the airflow
 setpoint.
 3. Manual Control Mode – The operator enters a fixed valve position for an individual aeration control
 zone.
 4. DO/Nmaster Control Mode: The MCP receives a valve position setpoint from the DO/Nmaster
 controller and adjusts the valve position to that setpoint.

4.3 Failure Responses

The MCP is equipped with failure response mechanisms to handle various types of failures, including blower
failures, communication breakdowns, power loss, and sensor failures. Alarms are generated to notify
operators of issues, and fallback control modes are activated to maintain system operation.
The MCP program includes a comprehensive list of alarms and warnings for various system components.
These alarms help operators identify and address issues promptly to maintain optimal system performance.

4.4 Operator Interface

The MCP features a Human Machine Interface (HMI) with various display types such as process displays, alarm
displays, and trend displays. Operators can interact with the system through the HMI to monitor and control
blower and aeration operations.

4.5 Remote Access

Remote access is provided to the MCP to allow monitoring and changes to the system without the need to
have an onsite presence. Cybersecurity is a key factor in allowing remote access, and multiply layers of
security were implemented to ensure the security of LACSD’s network.

 • LACSD provides internet service using a 4G cellular network
 • A CISCO Firewall is included in the MCP to control external access to the MCP
 • A Tofino security industrial firewall is used to control external access to the internal network
 o Blower local control panels and HMI’s are also provided remote access via the Tofino
 firewall
 • LACSD provides access via their VPN

5 SYSTEM COMMISSIONING

On August 15, 2022, an APG Neuros field service technician arrived on site and began by inspecting the blower
and performing the installation checklist. It was noted that the blower grille had been installed prior to arrival,
but the toolbox of spare parts and tools was missing. The existing blower was operating and could not be
turned off due to starting issues, necessitating testing with the APG Neuros turbo blower operating at
minimum capacity. The MCP was temporarily located with temporary power and network cabling, and the
instrumentation and valves on the basin were not yet installed. Power testing required the assistance of
district electricians to power on the unit.
Upon returning to the site on August 16, 2022, the technician conducted performance tests on the blower,
operating one core at a time due to restrictions. Network issues were identified, including problems with the
IP scheme, which were resolved with LACSD’s IT department. Missing patch cables for the RIO panels were
noted, and IP addresses were set in the MCP. Firmware issues arose, with the site requesting an unsupported
version for redundancy. A newer firmware version was loaded, and redundancy was turned off to complete
the tests. Communications with blower #2 were successfully tested.
On September 26, 2022, an APG Neuros field service technician installed an A/C unit. The interior temperature
of the panel was monitored, and a tarp was used as a temporary sunshade. The basin aeration control
installation was reviewed, revealing incomplete power and network connections. Coordination with the
mechanical contractor and IT department was necessary to address these issues. The technician also worked
on setting up the OPC server, configuring network addresses, and verifying communication with various
components. Issues with valve controls and network configurations were addressed. The system's operation
was monitored, and adjustments were made to ensure proper functionality. Training sessions were conducted
for the operations staff on system components such as the UPS.
In January 2023, an APG Neuros field service technician conducted a walkthrough of the project, noting
several incomplete tasks such as power, wiring, and network connections for various panels. The MCP panel
was still outdoors with minimal protection from the elements. The technician connected to the MCP and
added the RIO from multiple panels to the program, addressing networking issues with the assistance of the IT
department. During I/O testing, issues with valve controls and RIO configuration were identified and resolved.
Subsequent days involved further I/O testing, valve control verification, and network configuration updates.
The technician coordinated with various team members to ensure all components were functioning correctly
and made necessary adjustments to control parameters at the conclusion of the visit.
In February 2023, an APG Neuros field service technician conducted a system walkthrough to verify
installation completeness. Many instruments and panels still had temporary power wiring and network
cabling. The MCP was not in its permanent location, and the protective tarp was not in place. The technician
connected to the MCP and tested all instruments and readings, making necessary adjustments to the setup.
The technician verified the Hach readings and corrected the setup for consistency across basins. Extensive I/O
testing was performed, and adjustments were made to valve commands, feedback, and flow meters. The
technician also worked on verifying and updating the control program to ensure readiness for automatic
operation. Training sessions were conducted for the operations staff on system components.

In April 2023, an APG Neuros field service technician coordinated with the I&C staff to prepare the system for
I/O testing. Several incomplete tasks were identified, such as network cable connections and sensor setups.
The technician worked with the team to address these issues and ensure proper configuration of the RIO and
Hach controllers. The technician set IP addresses and configured the system for basin 2, performing I/O testing
and verifying instrument calibration. Adjustments were made to control parameters based on operational
feedback. The technician monitored system operation and made necessary tuning adjustments to optimize
performance.
In August 2023, an APG Neuros field service technician discussed system performance with the operations
team. Feedback indicated that the APG Neuros aeration controller provided smooth and functional control.
The technician worked with the team to turn the system into manual mode for testing and updates. The UPS
was checked and found to be in bypass mode, which was corrected. Firmware updates were performed on the
primary and secondary processor racks, and redundancy was tested successfully. The technician also
addressed issues with the RSLinx OPC Server and network connections, ensuring proper configuration and
operation of the system.

6 ACTUATOR ISSUES

During the installation and operation of Rotork actuators at the Pomona WRP, several significant issues were
encountered. Initially, it was discovered that the manual overrides, which were specified in the order, were
missing from the actuators. This oversight was not caught during quality assurance checks at multiple stages,
including at Rotork UK, Rotork Canada, and Pratt. Despite efforts to rectify this on-site, the manual overrides
were eventually installed after the actuators were shipped back to the UK. This process was fraught with
delays due to missing components and logistical challenges, causing operational disruptions.
In addition to the missing manual overrides, water infiltration was identified as a critical issue. In April 2023,
one of the actuators stopped responding to remote commands due to water entering the main control board
compartment. This issue necessitated sending the actuator to the UK for board replacement. Another actuator
stopped responding to remote commands in May 2023. Unlike the previous incident, no water was found
inside this actuator.
Further complications arose in October 2023, when an actuator developed a fault characterized by a
red/green flashing light and a clicking noise at 70% operation. This fault rendered the actuator uncontrollable
except manually. Ultimately, the main control board was replaced in the field to correct the issue. severely
impacting the ongoing advanced aeration control test for the US Department of Energy and the Los Angeles
County Sanitary District.
Throughout these incidents, communication and coordination with Rotork was challenging. Delays in
responses and a lack of timely updates were recurring issues, complicating the resolution process. Persistent
follow-ups and escalations were necessary to ensure that the issues were addressed, but these efforts often
met with slow progress.

7 INSTRUMENTATION ACCURACY

The success of this project heavily relied on the use of highly accurate and precise instrumentation. The DO/Nmaster
controller, developed by Ekster and Associates, necessitated the integration of high-performance field devices to ensure
optimal performance. Kurz airflow meters were selected for their exceptional accuracy, which is crucial for maintaining
the precise airflow required in the aeration process. Additionally, Rotork CVQ actuators were chosen for their reliability
and precision in controlling valve positions, while Horiba ammonium sensors provided accurate measurements of
ammonium levels, essential for monitoring and adjusting the DO setpoints for ammonia-based aeration control. The
combination of these high-quality instruments ensured that the system could operate efficiently and effectively,
meeting the stringent requirements of the DO/Nmaster sytem.

Additionally, the DO/Nmaster system's advanced control capabilities demanded more precise control of the blowers and
valves than traditional systems. The blower output needed to be adjusted at one-second intervals, which is three times
faster than typical control methods. This rapid adjustment capability allowed for more responsive and accurate control
of the aeration process, ensuring optimal DO levels in the treatment basins. Similarly, the valve positions are adjusted
every three seconds, up to ten times faster than traditional control systems. This high-frequency adjustment was
necessary to maintain precise control over the flow and distribution of air, ultimately leading to more efficient and
effective wastewater treatment.

8 FUTURE BLOWER SIZING

With the implementation of the DO/Nmaster system, the precision in controlling the aeration process has significantly
improved, resulting in a substantial reduction in airflow requirements. This advanced control system has optimized the
oxygen supply to match the real-time demand, leading to such low airflow levels that even at minimum loading
conditions, there is still an excess of air. To manage this excess air, a blowoff valve is currently used to vent it to the
atmosphere. However, this method is not energy-efficient, as it results in wasted energy that could otherwise be utilized
more effectively within the system.

To address this inefficiency, a future upgrade will include the addition of an APG Neuros turbo blower specifically
designed to meet the low airflow requirements more precisely. APG Neuros turbo blowers are known for their high
efficiency and operational flexibility, making them ideal for applications with variable airflow demands. By integrating a
turbo blower that can better handle the reduced airflow needs, the system will not only eliminate the need for energy-
wasting blowoff valves but also enhance overall energy efficiency and performance. This upgrade will ensure that the
aeration process remains highly efficient, further reducing operational costs and improving the sustainability of the
wastewater treatment plant.

APPENDIX C EKSTER AND ASSOCIATES PROJECT
 SUMMARY REPORT

 PERFORMANCE OF THE AERATION CONTROL SYSTEM

In 2018, the Chico Water Pollution Control Plant (WPCP) implemented an advanced aeration
control system provided by Ekster and Associates Inc. (Boca Raton, FL; referred to as Ekster)
that utilizes Python libraries for Machine Learning (ML) that reduced aeration energy demand
by more than 50% while maintaining full-compliance with wastewater discharge requirements
(Ekster et al., 2019; Ekster, 2020). A significant fraction of energy savings is attributed to
operating the activated sludge at dissolved oxygen (DO) of less than 1.0 mg/l.

One of the goals of this project was to determine whether the utilization of Ekster's
SRTmaster™ (a Model Predictive Control (MPC) solids retention time (SRT) controller) and
DO/Nmaster™ (a combination of ML-based ammonia, dissolved oxygen, airflow, and valve
controllers) could be applied to other plants and achieve reliable operation at suboxic DO
(<1.0mg/l). Detailed information about the transition of the Pomona Water Reclamation Plant
(WRP) biological nutrient removal (BNR) process from a DO of greater than 2.0 mg/L to suboxic
conditions are provided in other sections of this report.

Another goal was to evaluate the ease of integration and to confirm that the long-term
reliability of these control systems, observed at the Chico WPCP, could be replicated at other
facilities.

This section discusses the basis for selection of equipment and instrumentation and the
performance of DO/Nmaster and SRTmaster control loops at the Pomona WRP.

Methods and Materials.
Equipment & Instrumentation
Criteria for selection of equipment and instruments are discussed below.
Butterfly control valves
The existing manual butterfly valves were oversized for the precise air control required for
suboxic operation. As a result, the existing valves in the swing zones (Grid A), first aerobic zone
(Grid B), and second aerobic zone (Grid C) were reduced in size from 8-inch to 4 inch, from 12-
to 8 inch, and from 8-inch to 6 inch, respectively, in order to achieve a typical operating range
between 20 and 80 percent opening.
Figure 1 shows the actual dwell time graph for the selected butterfly valve at the air flows
realized for this project.

Figure 1. Dwell time graph for selected butterfly valve at actual air flows
Butterfly valves have highly non-linear characteristics (Figure 2) that can also make precise air
control difficult. Although valves with linear characteristics (i.e., diaphragm control valves) are
sometimes utilized for this reason, these valves are considerably more expensive than butterfly
valves. Properly-sized industry-standard butterfly valves with the right actuators have been
shown to be capable of precise air control (T. Jenkins, 2021 ).

Figure 2. Typical flow characteristic of a butterfly valve (courtesy of Chico WPCP)

Industry-standard API 609 (category A) butterfly valves, manufactured by Pratt Industrial (BF2-
8687), were selected based on availability, performance, and cost (Table 1).

Actuator selection criteria
Precise control with a butterfly valve can be accomplished with the appropriate
instrumentation and positioning actuator. Airflow repeatability of the actuator position is the
most important parameter of an actuator used for a control valve modulation. Another
important parameter for airflow control is the maximum number of actuations per hour

because the airflow set point continuously changes. The performance specification for air valve
actuators is provided in Table 2.
Table 2. Performance criteria for actuators

 15-20 seconds for

Position accuracy 0.1%

Based on this specification, Rotork actuator (Model CVA) was selected with quarter turn drive
action (CVQ) and the optional manual override. The manual override is important as it allows
valves to be manually adjusted by the operator in event of a communication or electrical issue
associated with the actuator control loop.

Air flow meters (thermal-mass flowmeter)
The air flow control loop is a generally a secondary loop in the DO-airflow cascade controls and
therefore accuracy is not as critical as time constant and repeatability.
The time constant (time it takes air flow sensor to reach 63.2% of its final reading after a change
in flow ) is a critical parameter for the airflow meter. Fast, rapidly moving actuators like those
selected for the Pomona WRP, require fast accurate, stable (low noise signal) air flow
measurements in order to obtain the precision needed for tight control. If the time constant for
the flow meter is not fast enough, the actuator needs to be slowed down to make sure the valve
positon isn’t changed before the flow measurement is stable. Repeatability is important,
especially in ML applications, where an exact valve position yields essentially the same flow
each time.
The air flow meter should be installed three line diameters upstream or more of the control
valve.

DO meters (optical luminescent or fluorescent technology)
DO control loops are either primary loops when DO set points are established by operators or
secondary loops in the case of ABAC when DO set points are determined by the ABAC control
loops. As described for air flow meters, the time constant needs to be fast enough so that the
DO sensor doesn’tcontribute to control loop delay.
In a secondary control loop, accuracy is less important than repeatability and the time constant.
For DO control, accuracy and repeatability is important especially at low DO. Since most
wastewater process applications are typically greater than DO of 1.0 mg/L, many manufacturers
have focused more on providing accuracy at higher DO levels than those under suboxic
conditions. Self-cleaning options (i.e., air-blast cleaning system) are recommended for
installations where DO control loops are normally primary control loops. HACH optical
luminescent sensors were already being used at LACSD’s WRPs and were selected. Fluorescent
DO sensors, a specific-type of luminescent sensors, (i.e., Insite IG®) are generally considered
faster than luminescent sensors and may be advantageous for applications using only DO
Control.
Ammonia meters (ion selective technology)
For the demonstration project at Pomona WRP, ammonia control is a primary loop. Although
accuracy is important, repeatibilty, especially at low ammonia levels, is the most important
parameter. Ammonium readings with Ion selective (ISE) sensors that are consistent and
repeatable with downstream wet-chemical ammonium analyzer data (i.e., secondary effluent)
can be relied on even with some inaccuracy. The ammonia loop is relatively slow; therefore, the
time constant is not as important as in the case of DO and, especially, airflow meters. ISE
sensors with self-cleaning functions (i.e., sonic, air blasts) are recommended. ISE sensors should
be located in well-mixed aerated zones for best performance. Although not ultimately utilized at
Pomona WRP, feed forward options that place ISE sensors upfront in the process (i.e., primary
effluent) should be thoroughly field tested as these zones are typically less mixed and more
prone to fouling.
Total suspended solids meters (optical technology)
When total suspended solids (TSS) meters are used for solids retention time (SRT) control,
repeatability is the most important parameter. If TSS meters are used for TSS control, accuracy
becomes the most critical parameter. TSS sensors with the self-cleaning features (i.e., wipers)
are recommended .
Based on these considerations, the following specifications for the meters were developed at
the beginning of the project (Table 3).

Identical DO, airflow , ammonia, and TSS meters were installed in all three aeration tanks. DO
and airflow meters were installed in the swing zone (Grid A) and in both aerobic zones (Grids B
and C). Ammonia meters were installed in the approximate middle in both aerobic zones (Grid B
and C). One immersion-type TSS meter was installed at the end of the last aeration zone (Grid
C). One in-line TSS meter was also installed in the return activated sludge (RAS) line from which
waste activated sludge (WAS) is wasted to the sewer for processing at LACSD’s A.K. Warren
Water Recovery Facility, in Carson, CA (formerly known as the Joint Water Pollution Control
Plant).

Control system
Both SRTmaster and DO/Nmaster were installed on an industrial PC that was incorporated into
APG Neuros Master Control Panel (MCP). This control architecture differed from the Chico plant,
where DO/Nmaster and SRTmaster were installed on a standalone server.
DO/Nmaster, is a multifunction ML-based control system consisting of the following blocks:

 • Database, where all incoming and outgoing data is filtered using a variety of data
 filtration techniques and stored.
 • ML modules calculate set points for dissolved oxygen, airflow, and valve position in each
 aeration grid. Each of control loops can operate independently or be integrated into a
 complex aeration control structure.

 • Visualization, used to generate customizable charts.
 • Output data filtration
 • Communication, exchanging data with Plant SCADA utilizing standard OPC (Object
 linking and embedding for process control) communication protocol, de-facto standard
 protocols for communicating between any SCADA and Windows-based system

SRTmaster is a Model Predictive Control (MPC) system consisting of the following blocks:

 • Database, where all incoming and outgoing data is is filtered using a variety of data
 filtration techniques and stored.
 • MPC, calculating waste flow set point
 • Visualization used to generate customizable charts.
 • Output data filtration
 • Communication, identical to the DO/Nmaster communication module

Cybersecurity
Due to the sophisticated nature of the state-of-the-art control algorithms in these control
systems, periodic vendor tuning and troubleshooting is required. As is customary for complex,
proprietary control systems, remote access is highly desirable (if not required) for occasional
and timely vendor tuning and troubleshooting. In addition, the demonstration project had
unique needs for research process monitoring and reporting.
LACSD understood the challenges related to providing remote access outside parties and
developed a cybersecurity architecture with firewalls that minimizes the risk related to remote
access (Figure 4). One firewall is responsible for controlling general remote access, and the
other firewall limits the access of the industrial comuter (DO/Nmaster and SRTmaster) to
aeration I/O only. The rest of the plant I/O remains inaccessible industrial computer and remote
computer. LACSD had the ability to fully monitor and control access throughout the duration of
the project.

Evaluation of DO/Nmaster performance methodology
Airflow control accuracy
Airflow accuracy and repeatability were evaluated over a 24-hour period using field data. The
frequency of data sampling was 30 seconds. Airflow set points were changed every 3 seconds
simultaneously at all grids. Airflows were monitored using the permanently installed airflow
meters described earlier.
DO control accuracy
The performance of DO/Nmaster DO control loops was compared to the traditional PID control
system. Accuracies of control were compared under variable DO setpoints that changed from
0.5mg/l to 2mg/l.
Evaluation of ammonia controller

As part of this project, criteria were developed to evaluate the performance of ammonia control
systems. Assessing ammonia control performance is not a trivial task due to several factors.

Firstly, ammonia removal depends on solids retention time (SRT) and dissolved oxygen (DO)
concentration. The SRT set point is determined based on multiple factors, such as sludge settling

and foam avoidance, rather than solely on ammonia removal efficiency. The SRT target was
maintained constant during the evaluation of the ammonia controller to ensure consistency,

Secondly, evaluating the ammonia control system presents challenges because the manipulated
variables, DO and airflow, were clamped. When DO or airflow reaches their upper or lower
limits, ammonia removal is no longer influenced by the ammonia control system.

Reliable and efficient ammonia control requires precise control during underloaded conditions
and quick adaptability during rapid load increases, such as those typically observed in the
morning (see Figure 5).

Figure 5. Typical diurnal pattern of incoming ammonia loading

Based on historical data analysis, a decision was made to exclude instances when DO reaches its
maximum or minimum values, as well as data within one hour of these events when assessing
the accuracy of the ammonia controller during underloaded conditions.

The agility of the ammonia controller was evaluated using data from within the hour preceding
the time at which DO reached its maximum value.

Data used in the evaluation of the ammonia controller was collected every 2 minutes.

Evaluation of SRT controller
The accuracy of the SRT controller was evaluated by comparing the SRT target with SRT, which
was calculated by SRTmaster using online TSS measurements in real-time and stored
information.

DISCUSSIONS
Airflow Control
Airflow control at the Pomona WRP was significantly more accurate than at the Chico WPCP
despite using an identical ML control library, airflow meters, and similar butterfly valves. The
improvements are attributed to the use of precise positioning actuators, which are specifically
designed for accurate process control. Unlike typical actuators, these are engineered for
continuous modulation and have an exact position accuracy of 0.1%.

The achieved airflow accuracy is comparable to valves with linear characteristics (i.e.,
diaphragm control valves). Combining ML control with butterfly valves and precise actuators is
more economical than using significantly more expensive valves with linear characteristics.

DO Control
The average error of PID control is more than three times greater than that of ML-based control.
The absolute error of ML-based control is nearing the error margin of HACH DO meters.
Although not tested on this project, the control error might be further reduced with a DO meter
with greater repeatability, such as those provided by Insite IG®
The DO/Nmaster control accuracy of the DO loops at the Pomona WRP surpasses that at the
Chico WPCP, likely due to improved airflow control and the upgraded DO control algorithm.
The DO meter models and ML control libraries used at both plants were identical.

Ammonia Control
Ammonia control demonstrated high precision, providing an opportunity for the reduction or
even complete elimination of aqueous ammonia addition required for chloramination. Daily
average ammonia concentrations, maintained under constant ammonia and SRT targets,
displayed remarkable consistency (see Figure 9), showcasing the accuracy and robustness of the
DO/Nmaster control. Composite samples analyzed in the laboratory revealed no significant
statistical differences in ammonia concentrations at the end of September and the beginning of
October, given the analytical method's standard deviation of 0.04 mg/L per Standard Methods.

Figure 9. Composite effluent ammonia concentration (typ)

Ammonia control at the Pomona WRP was more precise than at Chico WPCP due to the
following factors:

 1. Improved accuracy of DO, ammonia and airflow controllers, as previously discussed.
 2. Installing ammonia meters in each aeration compartment enables compartment-specific
 ammonia control rather than limiting control to one compartment per tank.
 3. Superior accuracy and repeatability of the ammonia meters used at the Pomona WRP.

Performance Indicators of Ammonia Control
The utilized accuracy indicator for ammonia control performance is a convenient tool for
benchmarking the effectiveness of ammonia controllers.

The proposed agility performance indicator could be applied to evaluate ammonia controllers at
other plants, provided the following limitations are addressed:

 1. The indicator must be scaled to account for changes in incoming ammonia loads, as
 controlling ammonia under rapidly fluctuating loads is significantly more challenging
 than during gradual increases.

 2. The indicator should be scaled to DO limits, as tighter DO limits can impact control
 error, although stricter upper control limits may result in energy wastage.

SRT Control
SRT control at the Pomona WRP demonstrated precision comparable to that observed at other
facilities where SRTmaster is installed (Ekster, 2016). Accurate SRT control allowed for better
adaptability of biomass to changing DO levels and timely adjustments to SRT set points when
biomass performance declined. Notably, even a less than 10% change in the SRT set point often
led to noticeable improvements in activated sludge performance.

Considering that manual SRT control typically results in an average error of 20%, it can be
argued that precise automated SRT control is essential for the robust operation of any activated
sludge system, particularly biological nutrient removal (BNR) systems operating at low DO
levels.

SRTmaster and DO/Nmaster Reliability and Support Overview

Reliability
SRTmaster and DO/Nmaster have proven exceptionally robust, with no recorded crashes. These
software systems are highly reliable and do not require continuous support. On rare occasions,
communication between these packages and the SCADA system was interrupted due to SCADA-
related communication issues. During these interruptions, the system seamlessly reverted to
SCADA's traditional PID algorithms for operation.

Support Requirements
SRTmaster and DO/Nmaster required initial tuning, which was completed remotely by the
vendor. Once this tuning was finalized, no further adjustments were necessary, and ongoing
remote access was not required. As with any software, periodic upgrades will be necessary. For
such instances, temporary remote access will be provided to the vendor on an "as-needed" basis.

Conclusions
The error in DO/Nmaster airflow control is less than 1%. Using inexpensive butterfly valves in
Pomona WRP achieved better accuracy than using valves costing 10 to 100 times more, even
those with linear characteristics. Using more precise actuators at Pomona WRP resulted in
superior flow control performance compared to Chico WPCP, despite both plants using the
same ML library and flow meters.

DO control provided by DO/Nmaster is over three times more precise than the previous PID
control system at the Pomona WRP. The DO/Nmaster system delivers DO control accuracy at
Pomona WRP that surpasses Chico WPCP, likely due to enhanced control algorithms, though
ML libraries are identical at both locations.

The ammonia control achieved by DO/Nmaster was highly accurate, enabling compliance with
strict effluent ammonia limits while significantly reducing ammonia use for chloramination.
Under consistent SRT and ammonia setpoint conditions, the effluent ammonia concentration in
24-hour composite samples remained remarkably stable over extended periods.

The precise control of SRT and DO provided an opportunity to observe significant changes in
activated sludge performance triggered by minor (<10%) adjustments to SRT and DO. This
finding highlights the importance of granular control of SRT and DO for optimizing activated
sludge performance.

Both SRTmaster and DO/Nmaster systems proved robust, requiring minimal maintenance from
LACSD staff. The vendor provided timely, as-needed support.

APPENDIX D LACSD KINETICS TESTING REPORT

 Pomona WRP Activated Sludge Kinetic Parameters Testing Program

Background:
In 2020, Carollo Engineers Inc. and The Los Angeles County Sanitation Districts (LACSD, Districts)
agreed to participate in a Department of Energy (DOE) funded project (DE-EE0009509) titled
“Transforming Aeration Energy in Water Resource Recovery Facilities (WRRFs) through Suboxic
Nitrogen Removal”. The contracts for the project were signed and the project officially began in October
of 2021. Hampton Roads Sanitation Districts (HRSD) was also part of the project and conducted pilot
studies demonstrating operation of activated sludge at “typical” dissolved oxygen (DO) levels of 2.0
mg/L and transitioning the activated sludge to suboxic DOs, defined as DOs at or below 0.7 mg/L. The
Pomona Water Reclamation Plant (WRP) was retrofitted with new aeration controls and modern
turboblower PACs which would allow precise control over aeration, and thus the DO levels, in the
activated sludge basins. Pomona then served as a full-scale demonstration of a transition from high DO
levels to low DO levels and potentially suboxic DO levels. The methodology used for this transition was
documented and the results made available through the project to other WRPs as a template for
implementation of suboxic operation. The main objective for operating at lower DOs is to reap significant
energy savings. Typical WRPs may already be operating at the upper end of nitrification capacity so the
decrease on DO cannot compromise nitrification capacity significantly.
The nitrification portion of activated sludge is a microbial process which can be described by the Monod
growth equation.
                                             𝜇= 𝜇 ̂𝑆/(𝐾𝑆+𝑆 )
Where:
μ = Specific growth rate at any substrate concentration
𝜇 ̂ = Maximum specific growth rate
S = Substrate concentration at any point in time
𝐾𝑆 = Substrate half-saturation coefficient
The Ks defines the point at which the growth rate is half of the maximum specific growth rate for the
nitrifiers. This is a value that is used by modeling software, such as BioWin, to describe and model the
nitrification reaction in activated sludge. Previous studies have demonstrated that nitrifiers are able to
acclimate to low DO conditions (Keene 2017) and not suffer any significant loss of nitrification capacity.
The exact response of the microbiology is an active area of research, but it is thought that the microbes
responsible for nitrification do not change but rather the kinetics are shifted in response to the different
DO environment. Figure 1 depicts an idealized Monod curve for nitrification at a maximum nitrification
rate of 4.0 and with the half saturation coefficient varied.

 Nitrification Rates
 4.0

  mg-NH3-N/g VSS-hr

 1.2 1.5

 DO mg/L

 Figure 1
Objectives:
The objective of this project was to perform kinetic tests to observe and document any changes as the
activated sludge transitions to low DO operation.

Methodology:
Five tests were performed as part of this study:
 1. Declining DO Test
 The declining DO test was one of the two main methods in the literature to measure the DO half
 saturation. This test that was run the most often and was perhaps the most important. This test
 documented any changes in the nitrification reaction with respect to the operating DO in the
 system. It is speculated that as the DO changes the activated sludge floc morphology changes to
 adapt to the different growth conditions. The physical change in the bulk sludge caused by
 differing oxygen mass transfer conditions may be observed and measured by obtaining data on
 the DO half saturation coefficient.
 2. Substrate Utilization Test
 The second method to measure the DO half saturation is through the substrate utilization test.
 Tests were run to determine the maximum nitrification rates for the activated sludge to
 demonstrate whether any changes took place over the course of the project.
 3. Maximum Denitrification Rate Test
 Tests were run to determine the denitrification rates to show whether the low DO operation had
 any impact on denitrification rates, positive or negative.
 4. Phosphorus Release Test
 It was thought that low DO operation might allow phosphorus removal in plants that were not
 designed for phosphorus removal. Kinetic tests for phosphorus release were run to characterize
 the phosphorus removal kinetics before and during low DO operation.

 5. Maximum Nitrifier Growth Rate Test
 The last test that was conducted was a nitrifier maximum specific growth rate test. This test was
 used to determine whether there are any inhibitors in the wastewater that are impacting the
 growth and thus reaction rate of the nitrifiers in the plant.
A detailed description and results of each test is given in the sections below.
Declining DO Test
The declining DO method used on this project is a variant of previous methods (Guisasola 2005)
appearing in the literature and was created by Kyle Malin at HRSD for use on this project (Malin 2021).
The basic procedure involves aerating activated sludge in a small tank until the DO reaches a high level
(>6.0 mg/L) and then cutting off the air and logging the decline in DO over time. The Oxygen Uptake
Rates (OUR) can then be calculated from the DO decline. OURs are measured for endogenous
respiration, nitrite respiration, and the nitrification reaction. The data was fitted to a Monod equation to
calculate the DO half saturation coefficient. The detailed steps and equipment utilized for the procedure
appear in Appendix 1. This procedure took about 4- 5 hours to complete, and the reproducibility of the
data is fairly consistent. The half saturation parameter can be calculated from the data in Excel via least
squares type analysis. For this project the parameters were calculated using an R script written by Malin
for use at HRSD. This script was used by the Districts for this project in order to be consistent with
HRSD’s methodology used. The script uses cubic splines to estimate the parameters, and can be found in
Appendix 2.
The declining DO test was the most frequent test run during the project. The impact of transition to lower
DOs in the activated sludge was of great interest to the project. HRSD had demonstrated in their pilot that
as the operating DO went from above 2.0 to 0.2 mg/L the DO half saturation coefficient did indeed shift
to lower values. The declining DO test was much easier to run and faster than the substrate utilization test
and gave more reproducible results. In the beginning of the project, only one reactor tank was available,
later a second reactor was constructed which allowed duplicates to be run side by side. The first reactor
had a Hach LDO2 for DO measurement and the second reactor had a Hach LDO2 as well as a Firesting
DO probe by Pyroscience. The Firesting probe is much smaller at 3 mm. in diameter and had a much
faster response time than the Hach unit (<15 seconds vs 30 seconds). This probe was used by HRSD in
their declining DO tests. Having 2 reactors side by side allowed for duplicate runs while not taking any
additional time to run the test. This also provided the opportunity to compare the kinetic parameters
calculated for each DO probe.
Figures 1 and 2 compare the calculated OURmax and Kdo based on the R-script calculation method. Both
DO probes yield similar OURmax determinations, however the Firesting probe consistently produces a
slightly lower Kdo value than the LDO probe. Figure 3 is a typical depiction of the declining DO test
results, the Firesting and LDO probes have similar slopes for the DO, offset slightly by time, perhaps
because of the faster response time of the Firesting. While the data is fairly similar at high DOs at lower
DO there is some separation that can be noted, Figure 4.

 Figure 4

A compilation of the Declining DO test data can be found in Appendices 3 and 4. Figure 5 is a box and
whisker plot which depicts the DO half saturation results for the project. The data indicates a decrease in
Kdo as the DO dropped to about an average of 1.0. Decreases in the average DO below 1.0 did not seem
to change the Kdo at all. Perhaps the difference between 1.0 and 0.8 were not significant enough. It
should also be noted that the DO at Pomona is not constant; in ABAC control, the DO varied throughout
the day. In 2023 the plant was operating one zone at high DOs (>3.0) with the second zone at low DOs
(<1.0); again the average may not be an adequate descriptor of the activated sludge conditions.

 Figure 5
Substrate Utilization Test
The substrate utilization method (Ghimire 2012, De Mulder 2014, Keene 2107) involves measuring the
nitrification rates in a small reactor at 4 to 6 DO setpoints. The DO must be maintained at the exact DO
set point for 30 – 60 minutes to measure the nitrification rates. This is done by removing samples and
analyzing the samples for ammonia, nitrate, and nitrite concentrations. The DO setpoints used would vary
from zero to three to four mg/L. There are several challenges to this method, including maintaining exact
DO concentrations in the reactor. The method is also quite time consuming, usually taking at least 8 hours
to complete one run. The nitrification rates at the DO setpoints were fit to a Monod curve and a best fit
procedure is used to calculate the half saturation coefficient. The detailed procedure appears in Appendix
1. Given the aforementioned issues, this method was run fewer times than the declining DO method.
The original plan was to run this test parallel to the Declining DO test. It was quickly determined that this
test was difficult to run, very time consuming, and was difficult to obtain results that were reproducible.
As a result, far fewer of these tests were run. It was very difficult to maintain the DO at the setpoints for
DOs at or below 1.0. In the beginning, on/off control was attempted using the Hach SC200 controller.

Later in the project, a mass flow controller was used. Neither technique yielded precise enough DO
control to have confidence in the setpoints being maintained at plus or minus 0.1 mg/L of DO. The data is
presented in Appendix 5. There were always data points that yielded nitrification rates that did not seem
reasonable based on the DO setpoints and comparable data. The variability of the data seemed in excess
to the normal variability of the lab procedures and the poor DO control. It was never determined what the
cause of this problem was. There were very few runs that yielded usable data at all DO setpoints, Figure 6
was one such run. More typical were runs such as shown in Figure 7, where one or more DO setpoints has
data that did not appear reasonable. In this test, the removal rate at about a DO of 1.0 appeared
unreasonably high.

Maximum Denitrification Rate Test
The maximum denitrification test is a batch procedure similar to that described in “Experimental Methods
in Wastewater Treatment”, IWA 2016. Activated sludge was placed in a small tank and spiked with COD
(acetate), nitrate, and nitrite. Samples were taken from the reactor and the nitrate and nitrite
concentrations were measured over time. The NO2, NO3, and NOx denitrification rates can be calculated
from this data. The detailed procedures can be found in Appendix 1.
The maximum denitrification test was run to determine whether there would be any changes in this rate
due to low DO operation. This was not likely to occur unless there was a shift in microbial populations
that resulted in poorer denitrification. While there was some variability in the test results, there did not
appear to be any impact on denitrification with respect to low DO operation. The test results are listed in
Appendix 6.
Phosphorus Release Test
The phosphorus release test is a batch procedure similar to that described in “Experimental Methods in
Wastewater Treatment”, IWA 2016. Activated sludge was placed in a small tank and spiked with COD
(acetate) in an anaerobic condition. Samples were taken from the reactor and the COD and phosphorus
concentrations were measured over time. Phosphorus release rates can be calculated from this data. These
rates gave some indication as to the activity of the phosphorus accumulating organism (PAO) population.
The detailed procedures are to be found in Appendix 1.
This test was done to chart any changes in phosphorus removal characteristics by the plant as it
transitioned to low DO operation. The assumption for this test was that a modified Ludzack-Ettinger

(MLE) plant not designed for phosphorus removal would have little to no phosphorus removal at high
DOs and as the DOs were lowered phosphorus removal may start to occur. However, the Pomona WRP
had 80-85% phosphorus removal at the beginning of the project. Evidently, the small anaerobic mix zone
in combination with the anoxic zone was able to grow and sustain a population of PAOs. This test
measured phosphorus release under anaerobic conditions and measured how much COD was needed. The
data in Appendix 7 shows significant variability and the test was only run six times since it did not appear
to be useful with respect to documenting low DO transformation at Pomona.
Maximum Nitrifier Growth Rate Test
The last test that was conducted was a maximum nitrifier growth rate test. There are several different
methods for this test found in “Methods Wastewater Characterization for Modeling Activated Sludge”,
IWA 2003. The test procedure utilized in this project was the High F:M test. The test started with a very
low MLSS content in a reactor spiked with ammonia. The ammonia concentration was such that the
nitrifiers growth rate was theoretically at maximum and was measured over the course of a week. Curve
fitting of the ammonia oxidation over the 7-9 days allowed estimation of the growth rate by curve fitting.
The detailed procedures are also found in Appendix 1.
This test is used to determine the growth rate of nitrifiers. This is most frequently used to model the
activated sludge process. The default value for this is 0.9 in most activated sludge models. Values that are
lower than this are a result of some level of nitrification inhibition in the activated sludge. This test was
run 4 times during the project. Two of the test runs had pH control issues and the results were not
calculated. The other two runs resulted in values on 0.69 and 0.55. While this is a fairly simple test to run,
past results for other plants seem to indicate substantial variability for test-to-test results. The results for
Pomona could be interpreted to indicate some level of inhibition; additional testing runs would be needed
to verify these values. BioWin model runs used to calibrate the plant data have used 0.8 and had
reasonably good agreement with the plant data.

Conclusions
The default half saturation value for DO used on Biowin is 0.7. The test results that were obtained on this
project seem to indicate that at high DOs this value could be greater than 1.0 and at DOs at or below 1.0
mg/L the values that were obtained on this project were between 0.7 and 0.8. Thus, running the activated
sludge at lower DOs had no clear impact on maximum nitrification rates, denitrification rates, and
phosphorus removal rates.
