 Appendix 1
 Kinetics Batch Testing at Pomona WRP Finalized (Updated January 2024)
Project Name
2022 – Kinetics Batch Testing at Pomona WRP
Project Staff
Research Manager: Bruce Mansell
Supervising Engineer: Phil Ackman
Project Engineers: Larry Wong, Patricia Hsia, Adam Horn
Support Staff: Charles Harris
Project/Task Number
1000009+14-02-00
Purpose
The purpose of this document is to write down the intended steps for doing kinetics batch testing with
Pomona WRP mixed liquor, such that parties involved in the subject project are aware of methodology.
Background
Wastewater Research (Research) is working with Water Reclamation Plant (WRP) Operations on
upgrading instrumentation and controls at Pomona WRP to perform ammonia-based aeration control
(ABAC). To better inform of the biological capabilities of Pomona WRP, several kinetics tests can be
performed to define constants based on Monod Kinetics.
Biological nitrogen removal (BNR) is primarily based on two stages: nitrification and denitrification.
Nitrification involves the oxidation of ammonia to nitrite, by ammonia oxidizing bacteria (AOB), and the
oxidation of nitrite to nitrate, by nitrite oxidizing bacteria (NOB). Denitrification involves the reduction
of nitrate to nitrite, and subsequently to nitrogen gas, by heterotrophic bacteria.
Bacterial growth rates can be modeled by the Monod equation, which involves the calculation of a
specific growth rate given a maximum specific growth rate, substrate concentration, and substrate half-
saturation constant. In the case of nitrifiers (AOB and NOB), the substrate is oxygen. The substrate
concentration can be measured (such as with a dissolved oxygen (DO) probe).
Additionally, Research is working with Carollo Engineers (Carollo) and Hampton Roads Sanitation
District (HRSD) to consolidate and normalize methodology for kinetics testing. As such, part of the
objective of these batch tests is to develop and establish kinetics testing methods.
Objectives
 1. Perform batch testing to determine constants for microbial kinetics for Pomona WRP mixed
 liquor (ML) under specific conditions.
 2. Develop and establish kinetics testing methods.

Methodology
Materials
 - Bench scale reactor for batch testing (2)
 - Supplies for running analyses:
 - Various Hach TNT test kits
 - DR2800 Spectrophotometer
 - 0.45 um filters
 - Glass fiber filters
 - Filter apparatus
 - Sample bottles
 - 10 L cubitainers
 - Coolers and ice for ML transport
 - Ammonium chloride
 - Sodium nitrate
 - Sodium nitrite
 - Sodium acetate
 - Gram scale
 - Weigh boats
   -   Bucket (2)
 - Funnel

Mixed Liquor/Feed Source
Primary effluent (PE), secondary effluent (SE), return activated sludge (RAS), and ML (collectively
called “Batch Test Sample” or BTS) will be collected from Pomona WRP primary effluent channel,
secondary effluent clarifiers, RAS sampling port in the gallery, or secondary reactors, respectively. For
nitrification testing, ML will be grabbed from the end of the aerobic zone. For denitrification testing, ML
will be grabbed from the end of the anoxic zone or RAS, SE, PE will be collected and combined as a
representative for ML.
BTS will be collected as follows:
 1. Obtain 2.5 gallons of the BTS from Pomona WRP using a bucket on a rope for collection from a
 tank and the sample collection tap for RAS
 2. Put BTS, RAS, PE, SE into a two 10 L cubitainers, filling up all the way such that there is no
 headspace.
As the batch testing will be done in the Research Building at San Jose Creek West WRP, the BTS should
be kept non-aerated during transport. Batch testing should be done as soon as feasible (within 24 hours).
Transport of the BTS can take up to an hour. As such, there might be a need to assess the effects of
moving the BTS from Pomona WRP to the Research Building. To determine if the transportation of
theBTS has an effect, some bench testing can be done at Pomona WRP, when the BTS is considered
“fresh”, and later bench testing can be done at the Research Building with the same BTS later, following
transport.
Reactor Set Up
A 18 L bench scale reactor is set up at the Research Building at San Jose Creek West WRP. Tests will be
done in the air-conditioned side of the Research Building to maintain temperature control. The reactor
includes the following equipment:
 1. pH probe (Hach) – to monitor pH to verify if it is within the range of 7.2-7.8; also includes
 temperature measurement
 2. DO probe (LDO2) – to monitor DO in the reactor
 3. Mixer
 4. Bubbler
 5. Air mass flow controller (Alicat)
 6. Air supply (aquarium pumps or compressed air)
 7. pH adjustment system – NaHCO3 for pH up, muriatic acid solution for pH down, diaphragm
 pump

Tests
Five batch tests are planned, each detailed in the sub-sections below.
Test 1: Declining DO – oxygen uptake rate
The purpose of this test is to determine the oxygen uptake rates of AOB and NOB. This will be done by
continuously measuring DO and fitting the results to a Monod curve. The test will be done in three
phases: first is to determine the uptake rate of endogenous bacteria; second is to determine the uptake rate
of the endogenous bacteria and NOB; and third is to determine the uptake rate of endogenous bacteria,
NOB, and AOB.

 1. Collect RAS and SE from Pomona WRP to create 50/50 ML mixture1
 2. Transport ML to Research Building
 3. Turn on mixer and sensors in reactor. Check pH and DO probe calibration; calibrate if
 necessary. Check logging interval of sensors – set at 30 seconds.
 4. Turn off heating blanket by setting Relay B setpoint to 10 C. Drain the tap water from the
 reactor.
 5. Check pH – maintain pH 7.2-7.8
 6. Dump RAS and SE 50/50 mixture into bench top reactor. Turn heating blanket back on
 by setting Relay B setpoint to 20 C. Check pH of ML. Adjust pH if necessary.
 7. Aerate overnight (12-16 hours)
 8. 3 phases
 1. Endogenous
 1. Aerate until maximum DO (~ 6 mg/L)
 2. Turn off aeration
 3. Watch DO decline
 2. Endogenous + Nitrite spike
 1. Aerate until maximum DO (~ 6 mg/L)
 2. Spike 5 mg/L nitrite (Add 24.64 mg of NaNO2 for every L of ML in the
 reactor); dissolve chemical in DI water before spiking
 3. Turn off aeration
 4. Watch DO decline
 3. Endogenous + nitrite and ammonia spike
 1. Aerate until maximum DO (~ 6 mg/L)

1
 (switched over from anoxic zone ML due to poor mixing at Pomona after April 2023)

 2. Spike 5 mg/L nitrite and 10 mg/L ammonia (add 24.64 mg of NaNO2
 and 38.2 mg of NH4Cl for every L of ML in the reactor); dissolve
 chemicals in DI water before spiking
 3. Turn off aeration
 4. Watch DO decline
 9. Repeat steps 1-6 two additional times
 10. Take grab sample for Lab VSS/TSS analysis
Results will be fitted to a Monod curve and charted (DO vs oxygen uptake rate).

Test 2: Declining DO – Carbonaceous uptake rate
The purpose of this test is to determine the oxygen uptake rates based on the carbonaceous oxygen
demand from heterotrophs, AOB, and endogenous bacteria. The test is similar to Test 1.

 1. Collect RAS and PE from Pomona WRP2
 2. Transport samples to Research Building
 3. Turn on mixer and sensors in reactor. Check pH and DO probe calibration; calibrate if
 necessary. Check logging interval of sensors – set at 30 seconds.
 4. Turn off heating blanket by setting Relay B setpoint to 10 C. Drain the tap water from the
 reactor.
 5. Dump RAS and PE to create 50/50 ML mixture into bench top reactor. Turn heating
 blanket back on by setting Relay B setpoint to 20 C. Check pH of ML. Adjust pH if
 necessary.
 6. Aerate until maximum DO (~ 6 mg/L). Should take about an hour.
 7. Turn off aeration
 8. Watch DO decline
 9. Grab 1L of sample in lab bottle for TSS/VSS testing. Send sample to the lab.
 10. Download data before leaving.
Results will be fitted to a Monod curve and charted (DO vs oxygen uptake rate)

2
 **(switched from anaerobic ML to 50/50 RAS/PE mixture in April 2023 due to poor anaerobic zone mixing at
Pomona)

Test 3: Simultaneous nitrification/denitrification and phosphorus uptake rate
The purpose of this test is to determine substrate utilization for AOB, heterotrophic denitrifiers, and
phosphorus accumulating organisms. In these tests, field samples will be taken and analyzed. Up to six
DO set points will be tested: 0, 0.2, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, and 4.0 mg/L.
For each DO set point, these steps will be followed for this test:
 1. Collect RAS and PE from Pomona WRP
 2. Transport sludge to Research Building
 3. Turn on mixer and sensors in reactor. Check pH and DO probe calibration; calibrate if
 necessary. Check logging interval of sensors – set at 30 seconds.
 4. Dump RAS and PE to create 50/50 ML mixture into bench top reactor. Turn heating
 blanket back on by setting Relay B setpoint to 20 C. Check pH of ML. Adjust pH if
 necessary.
 5. Spike reactor with NaNO3 if trying to capture SND, otherwise just NH4Cl as ammonia
 levels are depleted during the test
 6. Aerate at the target DO
 7. Collect a grab sample every 15 minutes; run each phase for 1.5 hrs (5 samples/hr (time=
                0, 15, 30, 45, 60, 75, and 90 minutes); 7 total)
 8. Filter grabbed samples with a glass fiber and 0.45 um filter
 9. Analyze each sample for NH3, NO2, and NO3. If COD and phosphorus data are
 required: Analyze only the first, middle, and last samples for PO4, first and last for
 soluble COD.
 10. Repeat Steps 1-8 for each target DO; keep eye on ammonia levels during Hach kit
 analyses as we may need to spike NH3 if re-using same sludge
 11. Grab 1L of sample in lab bottle for TSS/VSS testing. Send sample to the lab.

 12. Download data before leaving.
Results will be fitted to removal rate vs DO charts.

Test 4: Maximum Denitrification Rate
The purpose of this test is to determine a maximum denitrification rate.

 1. Collect RAS and PE from Pomona WRP
 2. Transport sample to Research Building
 3. Turn on mixer and sensors in reactor. Check pH and DO probe calibration; calibrate if
 necessary. Check logging interval of sensors – set at 30 seconds.
 4. Turn off heating blanket by setting Relay B setpoint to 10 C. Drain the tap water from the
 reactor.
 5. Dump RAS and PE as 50/50 mixture into bench top reactor. Turn heating blanket back on
 by setting Relay B setpoint to 20 C. Check pH of ML. Adjust pH if necessary.
 6. Spike 120 mg/L COD3, 25 mg N/L NO3, 2 mg N/L NO2 (add 256.4 mg of sodium
 acetate, 151.8 mg of NaNO3, 9.847 mg of NaNO2 for every L of ML in the reactor)
 7. Collect a grab sample every 10 minutes; run test for 90 minutes or when there is at least
 25% NOx reduction.
 8. Filter grabbed samples with a glass fiber and 0.45 um filter
 9. Analyze each sample for NO2, and NO3. Analyze only time 0, 60, and 90 minute
 samples for soluble COD
 10. Grab 1L of sample in lab bottle for TSS/VSS testing. Send sample to the lab.
 11. Download data before leaving.
Results will be plotted vs time.

3
  **no need to spike COD starting with 7/2023 test due to adequate COD in 50/50 mixture; sCOD was not
significantly different before and after sample transport

Test 5: Phosphorus Release/COD Uptake rate
The purpose of this test is to determine the phosphorus release and COD uptake rates (AOB KO, PAO KO,
Heterotrophic Oxygen Inhibition Constant KIO)

 1. Collect aerobic zone ML from Pomona WRP
 2. Transport sample to Research Building
 3. Turn on mixer and sensors in reactor. Check pH and DO probe calibration; calibrate if necessary.
 Check logging interval of sensors – set at 30 seconds.
 4. Turn off heating blanket by setting Relay B setpoint to 10 C. Drain the tap water from the reactor.
 5. Dump ML into bench top reactor. Turn heating blanket back on by setting Relay B setpoint to 20
 C. Check pH of ML. Adjust pH if necessary.
 6. Spike 200 mg/L COD as acetate
 7. Collect a grab sample every 5 minutes for 25 minutes (6 samples, times: 0, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25)
 8. Filter grabbed samples with a glass fiber and 0.45 um filter
 9. Analyze each sample for NO2, NO3, sCOD, and soluble PO4
 10. Download data before leaving.
Results will be plotted vs time.

Test 6: High F/M Test
The purpose of the high F/M test is to determine nitrifier grown rates in an environment with a large
ammonia concentration (food, “F”) to a relatively small nitrifier biomass (mass, “M”). Activity of the
nitrifiers will be monitored through the measurement of soluble ammonia, nitrate, and nitrite.
The steps below will be followed for this test:
Sludge Collection
 1. Collect two 1-liter samples of SJCE Unit 1 aerobic zone mixed liquor
 a. Place one of the 1-L bottles of mixed liquor in the fridge for lab TSS and VSS analysis
 b. use the other bottle of mixed liquor of for seeding the batch reactor
 2. Collect 20 liters of SJCE Primary Effluent
Prep Spiking Chemicals Solutions

 • Alkalinity:
 o Weigh out 25.4 grams of Sodium Bicarbonate (NaHCO3)
 o Dissolve in 150 mL of DI water
 • Ammonia
 o Weigh out 8.12g Ammonium Chloride (NH4Cl)
 o Dissolve in DI water
Reactor Set Up
 1. Use reactor 2: the reactor with the new mixer and further away from the wall
 2. On HACH SC200 set logging interval for (a) pH, (b) pH probe Temp, (C) DO and (d) DO probe
 temp all to 5 minutes
 3. Set reactor heating blanket temperature to 20°C on Hach SC200
 4. Add 17 liters of SJCE Primary Effluent
 5. Add 180 mL of SJCE mixed liquor
 6. Turn on Mixer (set @ 160 rpm)
 7. Record Start time
 8. Add Alkalinity and Ammonia spiking solutions to the reactor
 9. Set aeration to 6 mg/L DO (sc200 setup, relay, activation, setpoint = 6.00mg/L)
Sampling
 1. Take 50 mL sample from the reactor 2x/day (one in the morning, one in the afternoon).
 2. Record sampling time, readings from Hach instrumentation: DO, pH, Temperature
 3. Filter sample using fine filter kit (0.45um + GF filter); put sample in labeled bottle. With HACH
 kits analyze for NH4, NO2, NO3. Store extra sample in fridge.

Reactor Maintenance
 Top off water to 17-L mark if it is noticed that there is evaporation.
 Automatic acid addition for pH control will be used for the first 3-4 days of the test, in which
 carbon dioxide in the system will be naturally stripped from solution, causing elevated pH. After
 3-4 days, nitrifiers will begin to acclimate and produce acid, which should result in a lower pH.
 Manual spiking of NaHCO3 dissolved in DI water in increments of 0.5 g will need to be done.
 The pH range to be maintained for this test is between 6.8 and 7.3.

Test Completion
 1. Continue operating reactor and sampling until ammonia falls below 20 mg/L

Data will be input into a high F/M calculation spreadsheet to determine nitrifier growth rates.

 Appendix 1-A: Preliminary Testing Plan
Preliminary Testing
As some additional information is needed before we perform the tests for developing the kinetics
constants, some preliminary testing will first be done
Preliminary Test 1: Need for floating cover
Hampton Roads Sanitation District (HRSD) has done kinetics testing for their facilities. Some of the
batch testing methods to be used are adopted from HRSD. In their reactor set up, they have a floating
cover to prevent atmospheric air entrainment from the mixer. To determine if there is a significant impact
from the mixer and not having a cover, a batch test can be performed.

 1. Collect aerobic zone ML (does not need to be from Pomona WRP)
 2. Transport ML to Research Building
 3. Turn on sensors in reactor
 4. Dump ML into bench top reactor
 5. Spike 10 mg/L ammonia (38.2 mg of NH4Cl for every L of ML in the reactor)
 6. Turn on mixer. Do not turn on air.
 7. Grab samples at 1, 2, and 4 hours. Filter a sample after each grab with a glass fiber + 0.45 um
 filter.
 8. Analyze samples for ammonia
If the ammonia does not decline significantly with the mixer on, there is likely not enough air to promote
significant nitrification. If this is the case, there is no need to make a floating cover for the reactor, as it is
likely the air from mixing has a very small contribution.
Preliminary Test 2: Need for pH adjustment
HRSD has included pH adjustment with their batch testing set-up. This might be necessary if there is a
huge change in pH during the batch testing process. To inform of us of whether this is necessary, a
simple batch test can be done to see if the pH drops to a level which needs adjustment during testing.

 1. Collect aerobic zone ML (does not need to be from Pomona WRP)
 2. Transport ML to Research Building
 3. Turn on sensors in reactor
 4. Dump ML into bench top reactor
 5. Spike 5 mg/L nitrite and 10 mg/L ammonia (add 24.64 mg of NaNO3 and 38.2 mg of NH4Cl for
 every L of ML in the reactor)
 6. Turn on mixer and aerate.
 7. Monitor pH continuously.
If the pH declines significantly and falls below the target operational range of 6.3 to 7.3, then there might
be a need for a pH adjustment mechanism. If not, no additional work is necessary for pH adjustment.

 Appendix 2

title: "Simultaneous_Declining_DO"
author: "Kyle Malin"
date: "8/2/2021"
output: html_document

```{r}
library(deSolve)
library(rootSolve)
library(coda)
library(FME)
library(tidyverse)
library(gridExtra)
library(dplyr)
library(here)
```
Import a csv file with data in two columns: the first column in time "t" and the second is the experimental DO data
"DO". Using a csv file is better for some of the later statistical analysis
```{r}
data <- read_csv('Test1-08-08-24B.csv', col_names = TRUE)
```
create spline function to provide ability to differentiate DO with respect to time
```{r}
dDO <- splinefun(x = data$Time, y = data$DO)
```
create dDO in dataframe df2
```{r}
df2 <- data.frame("x" = data$DO, "y" = -dDO(data$Time,deriv = 1))
names(df2)=c("x","y")
```
separate out each run from the test data (starting at some high DO, often 2mg/L for Endogenous and 4 mg/L for all
others). This cuts out the reaeration portions of the data. Finally, bind the resulting data frames together into a single
data frame "df_data"
```{r}
df_END <- df2[1:154,]
df_NO2_1 <- df2[188:252,]
df_NO2_2 <- df2[302:356,]
df_NH3_1 <- df2[395:417,]
df_NH3_2 <- df2[489:506,]
df_data <- rbind(df_END, df_NO2_1, df_NO2_2, df_NH3_1, df_NH3_2)
```
This function represents the forward model, which simultaneously fits Monod curves to each run of data from the
test. Each phase (i.e. endogenous, nitrite, ammonia+nitrite) is represented by an individual Monod term.
Each Monod term has it's own set of parameters representing OURmax and oxygen half-saturation (Ko)
the resulting curve fits are again bound together so the output is a single data frame, which is necessary for the
residual function that follows

$$ y = OURmax(DO/(Ko+DO)) $$
```{r}
resp_mod <- function(p) {
  #df_END <- as.data.frame(df_END)
  #x = df_END[,1]
  x1 = df_END$x
  y1 = p[1]*x1/(p[2]+x1)

  df_1 <- data.frame(x1,y1)
  names(df_1)=c("x","y")
  x2 = df_NO2_1$x
  y2 = (p[1]*x2/(p[2]+x2))+(p[3]*x2/(p[4]+x2))
  df_2 <- data.frame(x2,y2)
  names(df_2)=c("x","y")
  x3 = df_NO2_2$x
  y3 = p[1]*x3/(p[2]+x3)+p[3]*x3/(p[4]+x3)
  df_3 <- data.frame(x3,y3)
  names(df_3)=c("x","y")
  x4 = df_NH3_1$x
  y4 = p[1]*x4/(p[2]+x4)+p[3]*x4/(p[4]+x4)+p[5]*x4/(p[6]+x4)
  df_4 <- data.frame(x4,y4)
  names(df_4)=c("x","y")
  x5 = df_NH3_2$x
  y5 = p[1]*x5/(p[2]+x5)+p[3]*x5/(p[4]+x5)+p[5]*x5/(p[6]+x5)
  df_5 <- data.frame(x5,y5)
  names(df_5)=c("x","y")
  df3 <- rbind(df_1, df_2, df_3, df_4, df_5)
  x6 = df3$x
  y6 = df3$y
  return(df3)
 }
```
Residual function
```{r}
ModelCost <- function(P) { out <- resp_mod(P) return(df_data$y-out$y) # residuals}
```
Minimize residual function by adjusting parameter inputs in the forward model. The parameters can be constrained
if necessary.
```{r}
ini_para <- c(6, 0.039, 10, 0.5, 20, 0.5)
fit_iter <- modFit(f = ModelCost,
 p = ini_para,
 method ="Marq")
              #upper = c(20, 0.039, 25, 1, 30, 1.5),
              #lower = c(0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0))
summary(fit_iter)
sfit_iter <- summary(fit_iter) # we summarize the outcome of the iterative process
#print(sfit_iter)

summary_END_OURmax <- Simult_Summary$par[1]
summary_END_k <- Simult_Summary$par[2]
summary_NO2_OURmax <- Simult_Summary$par[3]
summary_NO2_k <- Simult_Summary$par[4]
summary_NH3_OURmax <- Simult_Summary$par[5]
summary_NH3_k <- Simult_Summary$par[6]
```

APPENDIX E GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS TESTING
 REPORTS

Evaluation of Nitrous Oxide
Emission from the Los Angeles
County Sanitation Districts’ Pomona
Water Reclamation Plant
Part of “Transforming Aeration Energy in Water Resource Recovery
Facilities (WRRFs) through Suboxic Nitrogen Removal”

Diego Rosso, Ph.D.

Summary Report
Final Version

Irvine, 28 January 2025

Results and Conclusions
Aeration Tank 3 of the LACSD’s Pomona Water Reclamation Plant was tested on
November 4th, 2024 according to the ASCE Standard Guidelines for ln-Process Oxygen
Transfer Testing (ASCE, 2018). The testing was carried out to verify whether the
operation under an advanced aeration control system (ammonia-based aeration control
or ABAC) would be associated with lower the nitrous oxide (N2O) emission than
operations at high low dissolved oxygen (DO). This testing is a repeat of the test in 2023
carried out at this facility, although not on the same tank since Tank 1 was off-line at the
time of this test.

This testing part of the project titled “Transforming Aeration Energy in Water Resource
Recovery Facilities (WRRFs) through Suboxic Nitrogen Removal”, led by Carollo
Engineers, Inc. and funded by the United States Department Of Energy (USDOE).

We present in Table 1 the airflow-weighted results for all measurements (except for DO).
This is the test after low DO conditions have reached steady-state. Here the DO
measured with handheld meters never exceeds 1 mg/L. However, a comparison with the
plant readouts show consistency with prior observations, i.e. plant optical DO sensors
usually record less variable but higher values than handheld electrochemical sensors.
This is not an issue since signal stability is the priority for process control.

       DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com             1|42

During this test, the swing zone at the head of the process was aerated so it was included
in the testing. Contrary to the expectation that N2O flux is highest at the head of a plug-
flow aerated reactor, the N2O flux profile showed an ascending trend along the process
tank, mirroring the increasing trend of air flux and DO.

The profiles of oxygen transfer efficiency (expressed as OTE and aSOTE) increase along
the process train, with a peak towards the middle. The oxygen uptake (OUR) is adequate
for a fully denitrifying process. Note that the OUR values reported here are calculated
using the air flow measured with the off-gas analyzer, not the plant metered air discharge.
There can be a difference between the two, since the air flow measured with the off-gas
hood is a spatially distributed map over the aeration tank surface, while the plant air is
usually metered at a manifold or drop leg (hence, it averages over large areas).

This report is divided in multiple sections: background and scope, nomenclature, testing
team details, existing process conditions, sampling details, N2O flux calculations, and
plots of the results. In the appendices are reported the theory of analysis for the off-gas
technique, the full data sets, and photographs of the testing.

 Table 1. Summary of results from the off-gas tests performed at LACSD’s Pomona
Water Reclamation Plant on November 4th, 2024. The N2O calculations are reported in
 the sections below. Full data sets are reported in Appendix B.

Background and Scope
The USDOE-funded research project, aimed at assessing the benefits of suboxic (i.e.,
low-DO) operations in secondary treatment, is led by Carollo Engineers, Inc. The
LACSD’s Pomona Water Reclamation Plant is one of two sites of investigation for the
project, the other being the VIP pilot plant at the Hampton Roads Sanitation District in
Virginia.

At the time of this testing, Tank 3 had been operating under ammonia-based aeration
control (ABAC), which is associated with periods of DO below 1 mg/L, which can be
considered low for a traditional nitrifying-denitrifying activated sludge process. This test
follows the previous sampling to assess the N2O emission profile along a reactor under
high-DO aeration conditions (i.e., 2 mg/L or higher). A comparison between the two tests
is discussed below. The goal of reducing the DO set point is to curb significantly the
aeration energy, while meeting the effluent targets of secondary treatment, and to test the
hypothesis that N2O emissions are lower when ABAC is implemented.

DRH2O LLC is under contract with Carollo Engineers, Inc. to perform off-gas testing, gas
sampling, coordinating with an external laboratory for N2O measurements of the gas
samples, and assessment of N2O emission fluxes from the sampled processes.

Nomenclature
 Parameter Name Units ____
 OTE Oxygen Transfer Efficiency in field conditions %
 aSOTE Standard OTE in process water %
 SOTE OTE in clean water at standard conditions %
 a factor = aSOTE / SOTE dimensionless
 DO Dissolved Oxygen massO2 / (H2O volume)
 OUR Oxygen Uptake Rate massO2 / (time • H2O vol.)

Testing Dates and Team
Preparations took place off-site on October 31st. Testing was carried out on November
4th. For DRH2O, Diego Rosso performed the test aided by C. Porter and D. Cambron. N.
Razaei witnessed the tests. For LACSD, C. Alfaro facilitated the tests and T. Weiland
witnessed part of the test. The gas samples were processed by UCR, using the same
method as in the previous tests.

Existing Conditions
The Los Angeles County Sanitation Districts own and operate a number of facilities that
can be grouped as upstream, downstream, or standalone plants. The Pomona Water
Reclamation Plant is one of the upstream facilities, which means that the facility reclaims
water for local use and delivers the solids to the centralized handling facility at the Joint
Water Pollution Control Plant in Carson. The service area of the Pomona WRP includes
approximately 130k people, in the eastern boundary of the Los Angeles County area.

Following preliminary and primary operations, the wastewater undergoes secondary
treatment in an activated sludge process with MLE configuration. The secondary reactors
are 3 parallel and identical tanks, 30ft wide and with an initial anoxic zone (60ft long)
followed by a swing zone (~27ft long, equipped with 115 diffusers). The tank layout is
schematized in Fig. 1. For approximately the past year, the secondary process has been
operating under the ABAC (ammonia-based aeration control) scheme, which alters the
DO set point based on the ammonia sensor readings.

The aeration system here consists of 9in Sanitaire Silver Series II EPDM discs, arranged
in 3 grids. The first grid is under the swing zone and was active at the time of testing
(unlike the prior test). The second and third grids are approximately 87ft in length each,
and are equipped with 1240 and 625 discs each, respectively. The tank depth is 14.7ft
and the diffusers submergence is 12.5ft.

The plant was tested during a normal dry-weather day of operation. Details about the
operating conditions of the plant at the time of testing are reported in Table 2. In this table
we report both the values from the LACSD and values calculated by Carollo from process
data averages.

N2O Sampling Details
The gas sampling for N2O was performed by collecting gas in bags connected to the off-
gas analyzer. Prior to collection, the gas had already transited through the off-gas
analyzer, equipped with a gas-conditioning column (containing desiccant and NaOH
pellets). The entire analyzer is fitted with Teflon tubing.

One gas sample per off-gas testing position was collected, providing duplicate samples
for each of the testing stations (Stations 0-5). All samples arrived at destination intact and
their measurements proved consistency across the tank width (i.e., consistent readings
between samples A and B for each of the Stations 0-5).

In total, 12 samples were collected, since the swing zones were sampled as well. The
labeling scheme is reported in Table 3. The samples were delivered in a rigid pressure-
controlled case, were received in good condition, and with proper preservation. Using a
gas chromatograph with an electron capture detector, the samples were processed soon
after arrival. Selected samples were processed as laboratory duplicates, for quality
control.

The laboratory results tabulated above indicate that the partial pressure is uniform
through the process, i.e. it is within the same order of magnitude, with the exception of
the last testing station (positions 5A-B). Since the emission flux is calculated using the
partial pressure and the off-gas flux, the positions 5A and 5B show the largest contribution
to the overall emissions since they are associated with the highest partial pressures and
off-gas fluxes. The next section reports in detail all the calculations.

The emissions of a greenhouse gas can be reported in volumetric (eq. 2) or ponderal

by multiplying the volumetric emission times the density of N2O at the temperature and
pressure of sampling (in this case, approximately 21oC and 1atm):

The N2O ponderal emission for each hood position is calculated by multiplying the N2O
ponderal flux through the hood and the hood area. The ponderal emission of the whole
tank would be the airflow weighted average of the N2O ponderal emission multiplied by
the aerated tank area.

The 18-18 ASCE standard prescribes that at least 2% of the aerated surface be sampled
in order for the off-gas sampling to be representative of the whole process. In this case,
this threshold was exceeded with 6 testing stations (i.e., 12 testing positions). The
calculation of the aerated surface area included the swing zone.

Comparison with Previous Results at LACSD
The results from this test are the effect of different operating conditions, when compared
to the tests carried out in 2023. The test could not be repeated on the same tank, since
Tank 1 was offline due to maintenance, at the time of this test. However, a meaningful
comparison between the two tests is possible. In 2023, the DO profile was much higher
in the first half of the process (Fig. 9). Note that these DO values are from the
electrochemical handheld probes, field-calibrated each time at the beginning of the test.
The LDO process sensors may have different values and would be limited to the points
where they are permanently installed. Also note that this secondary process is operated
as three parallel plug-flow reactors, so in the first half of the process the bulk of the load
is oxidized. The last two testing positions, at the 200ft mark and following in Fig. 9, have
more similar DO values for the two tests, although the values for 2024 appear in excess
of the definition of “low DO”. Also, the second test (2024) was carried out when the swing
zone was active, although with very low DO.

 Figure 9. Comparative profiles of the DO along Tank 1 (2023) and Tank 3 (2024).

       DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com             17|42

The implications of operating at a high DO in the first half of the process are twofold: 1)
higher aeration energy demand; 2) higher stripping of N2O in the initial part of the process,
i.e. where stripping of N2O produced in the aerated zone compounds with the stripping of
GHG produced the anoxic zone and carried over to the aerated zone. The goal of this
testing campaign was to verify whether the overall mass emission of N2O under different
DO conditions.

In order to achieve higher DO at the beginning of the process, a much higher air flux is
needed to be delivered to the tank (Fig. 10). The operations in 2023 were associated with
decreasing air flux, if we consider the exponential decay trend for load and air demand
along ideal plug-flow reactors. During this test, the air flux profile measured with the off-
gas hood is not decreasing and is more regular along the process than the prior test.

 Figure 10. Comparative profiles of the hood air flux along Tank 1 (2023 test)
                               and Tank 3 (2024 test).

       DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com            18|42

The comparison of the concentration profiles (Fig. 11) shows a high correlation between
DO and N2O partial pressure in the off-gas. During the current test the DO measured with
handheld electrochemical sensors is below 0.52 (all positions except for 4A-5B, at the
200ft mark and forward in the process), and here the N2O partial pressure is always below
the test results from 2023. However, when the DO was suddenly decreased in 2023
(positions 4A-5B, evident in Fig. 9), a result of much decreased air flux, the N2O partial
pressures are lower (orange bars vs. blue bars in Fig. 11).

Figure 11. Comparative profiles of the N2O concentration along Tank 1 (High DO, 2023
 test) and Tank 3 (Low DO, 2024 test).

tests, we encounter interesting findings. The averages calculated using all the positions
in the tank show comparable N2O fluxes: 1.89+/-0.99 vs. 2.16+/-2.08 gN2O/m2/d,

      DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com          19|42

respectively in 2023 and 2024. Note the large spread for 2024, due to the N2O partial
pressure towards the end of the process. Hence, when the values with only high DO in
2023 and only low DO in 2024 are isolated (i.e., all positions except for positions 4A-5B),
the results are distinct: 2.25+/-0.80 vs. 0.78+/-0.46 gN2O/m2/d, respectively in 2023 and
2024. Table 4 shows this effect. In the table we can observe that the average DO for the

points. However, when we exclude the data for positions 4A-5B, the DO is 1.96 vs. 0.34
mg/L, which is a much more pronounced difference.

In the following graphs we plot the N2O partial pressure and emission flux against the air
flux, which is also the driver for DO. These plots suggest that N2O emission is driven by
air flux, since N2O is stripped by the air flow. One must distinguish between N2O
production and emission, hence these graphs only focus on the latter since production
occurs in the liquid phases and is not subject of off-gas analysis.
In Fig. 13 it is clear that the N2O partial pressure exceeds 6 ppmv only when the air flux
is above 0.6 SCFM ft2. However, the opposite is not necessarily true, since in this air flux
range the N2O partial pressure is in most cases lower than 6ppmv.
Note that an increase in N2O stripped is not equivalent in an increase in N2O produced,
because the production of the greenhouse gas could have occurred earlier in the process
where the air flow was insufficient to strip it from the water to the gas phase.

       DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com           22|42

  Figure 13. Comparative profiles of the N2O concentration or (partial pressure) along
                    Tank 1 (2023 test) and Tank 3 (2024 test).

This challenge in maintaining low air flux (hence, low DO) may be associated with the fact
that this experiment is carried out at a full-scale facility with realistic constraints. In fact,
there is a minimum requirement to discharge air evenly through the diffuser grid. To
release air evenly throughout the water volume, membrane diffusers require a minimum
air discharge per unit (for membrane discs this is usually ~0.5 SCFM per diffuser). When
air demand is below the minimum air discharge requirement, the latter must be chosen
to operate the aeration system continuously, to avoid the uneven air discharge. This
action would result inevitably in high air flux and high DO, particularly where the oxygen
demand is low (i.e., towards the end of the process). An alternate strategy is to operate
the diffuser grids intermittently, at intervals sufficiently long to meet the mixing
requirements on a time-average basis but without exceeding in DO, as in the continuously
aerated strategy.

       DR H2O LLC       15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com               23|42

Figure 14 shows the same effect, in terms of N2O flux. Since this is calculated from the
air flux and the N2O gas concentration, the pattern of the data in the plot is consistent with
that in Fig. 13.

 Figure 14. Comparative profiles of the ponderal N2O flux along Tank 1 (2023 test) and
                                  Tank 3 (2024 test).

       DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com             24|42

Comparison with Previous Results at HRSD

One final point of discussion is about the comparison between tests carried out at this
facility and those carried out at HRSD’s VIP pilot. Due to the difference in process
configuration and geometry, it is difficult to compare the two sets of results. However, the
following points can be summarized:

 - The N2O flux at HRSD is consistently lower than the N2O flux at LACSD, for both
 tests;

 - The air flux at HRSD is much lower than the air flux at LACSD;

 - The sampling at positions operating at low DO (lower than ~0.50 mg/L) are
 associated with lower N2O emission flux for both processes. Conversely, the
 sampling at positions operating at high DO are associated with higher N2O (higher
 than ~0.50 mg/L) emission flux for both processes, regardless of their location
 along the process.

       DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com           25|42

Appendix A – Off-gas technical description
One of the challenges with aerobic wastewater treatment process design is the correct
specification of aeration capacity. A variety of techniques exist for estimating the oxygen
transfer capacity of an aeration system. Methods for estimating transfer can generally be
divided into three categories:

 • Clean water testing and conversion to field rates with alpha, beta, and theta
 conversion factors (ASCE, 2006).

 • Dirty water testing (ASCE, 1997) using methods to account for the biological
 consumption of oxygen during the transfer test.

 • Material balance methods which attempt to determine difference in input and
 outputs of oxygen consuming material.

All of these methods have advantages and disadvantages. When using clean water test
results it is very difficult to accurately estimate the alpha factor (ratio of mass transfer
coefficient in dirty water to its value in clean water). Process (or dirty) water testing
requires accurate estimation of oxygen consumption rate, which is often very difficult,
especially in oxygen limiting conditions, which occur in overloaded treatment plants.

A technique which has none of the above shortcomings is off-gas analysis. This method
requires the capture of a representative sample of the gas which exits the aeration basin
surface, and analysis of this gas for oxygen, carbon dioxide, and water vapor content. By
knowing the flow rates of gas entering and exiting the liquid, mass transfer efficiency can
be calculated. If flow rates are not known, the mass transfer efficiency can still be
determined by knowing the molar percent of the reacting or changing gas constituents
(oxygen, carbon dioxide, and water vapor) and assuming that the inert gas constituents
(nitrogen, argon) remain constant. It must be further assumed that the transfer at the fluid
surface and the atmosphere is negligible when compared to the transfer caused by the
aeration system, and that steady state conditions exist during the test. Both assumptions
are very good for wastewater treatment systems.

The concept of off-gas analysis is not new and was originally described in 1939 by Sawyer
and Nichols (1939). A number of later investigators continued the development of off-gas
analysis, including Hover et. al. (1954), Pauling et al (1968), Prit and Callow (1958) and
Downing (1960). More recently Conway and Kumke (1966) and Leary et al. (1968) have
used off-gas analysis. The ASCE/EPA subcommittee on oxygen transfer testing asked

       DR H2O LLC      15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com           27|42

Ewing Engineering (Redmon et al., 1982) to further develop the technique. Their results
reported at the 1982 WPCF meeting show that the off-gas technique is an accurate and
precise way of estimating aeration efficiency under process conditions. New
developments which make this method more precise are advances in oxygen analyzers,
and the use of large off-gas collection hoods which capture more representative samples.

Off gas analysis can be used for any subsurface system regardless of the oxygen uptake
rate and process conditions. Efficiencies of oxygen limited systems can also be
determined, although the transfer rate may be different than the transfer rate under
normal operation. It has been documented that alpha factors very greatly with such
conditions (Stenstrom and Gilbert, 1981).

THEORY OF ANALYSIS

To determine oxygen transfer efficiency using off-gas analysis, a mass balance must be
performed on the gas entering and exiting the liquid. The following description is provided,
and is based largely on the analysis by Redmon et al. (1982). If the flow rates of gas
entering and exiting the fluid are known, then the following mass balance can be made:

 dY
         VG r      = r (qi YR - qoYog ) - K L a(C ¥* - C )V                         (A-1)
 dt

where:

 r density of oxygen at temperature and pressure of gas flow,

 qi ,qo = total volumetric gas flow rates of inlet and outlet gasses,

 YR , Yog = mole fractions (equivalent to volumetric fractions) of oxygen in
 the inlet and exit gasses,

 K La = volumetric oxygen transfer coefficient,

 *
 C¥ = equilibrium dissolved oxygen concentration in the test liquid
 at the given conditions,

       DR H2O LLC            15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com             28|42

 C = oxygen concentration,

 V = liquid volume, and

 VG = gas hold-up volume.

At steady state the equation reduces to:

 * (A-2)
 r( q i - YR - q o Yog ) = K L a ( C ¥ - C) V

The left hand side of equation A-2 is the amount of oxygen transferred as determined
from the change in oxygen mass and flow rate of the inlet and outlet gas streams. The
right hand side of equation A-2 is the familiar "K rate" based upon the mass transfer
coefficient and driving force.

Since it is often difficult to measure the entering gas flow rate to an aeration system, a
procedure which does not rely on gas flow rates is needed. If one assumes that the inert
portions of the entering gas stream do not change, a mole fraction approach can be
developed which does not require gas flow rate. This assumption means that the
nitrogen, argon, and inert trace gases do not change as they pass through the aeration
system. The new technique (Redmon et al., 1983) relies upon this assumption to
calculate oxygen transfer efficiency (OTE).

OTE expressed as a fraction, can be derived as follows:

 mass O 2 in - mass O 2 out
 OTE = (A-3)
 mass O 2 in

 G i ( M o / M i ) MR o / i - G i ( M o / M i ) MR og / i
 = (A-4)
 G i ( M o / M i ) MR o / i

 MR o / i - MR og / i
 = (A-5)
 MR o / i
where:

 Gi = mass rate of inerts, which is constant (by assumption) in both the
 inlet and off-gas streams

      DR H2O LLC              15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com         29|42

 MoMi = molecular weights of oxygen and inerts, respectively

 MR o / i , MR og = mole ratio of oxygen to inerts in the inlet and off-gas streams

The mole ratio of oxygen to inerts is calculated by subtracting the mole fractions of
oxygen, carbon dioxide and water vapor, as follows:

 YR
         MR o / i =                                                                    (A-6)
                       1- YR - YCO ( R ) - YW( R )
 2

 Yog
         MR og / i =                                                                   (A-7)
                       1- Yog - YCO ( og) - YW( og)
 2

where:

 YCO ( R ) , YCO ( og ) = mole fractions of CO 2 in the reference gas(R), or
 2 2
 off-gas (og)

 YW( R ) , YW( og ) = mole fractions of water vapor in the reference gas (R) and
 off-gas (og)

The value of YR is the mole ratio of oxygen in air, and can be calculated by subtracting
the humidity from the known (handbook) mole fraction of oxygen in dry air as follows:

         YR = 0.2095(1 - YW ( R ) )                                                    (A-8)

The mole fraction of oxygen in the off-gas must be measured experimentally, as well as
the CO 2 and water vapor mole fractions. For early Ewing Mark V devices the CO 2 was
measured with an Orsat, which measures the CO 2 as a volume percent. The sample off-
gas is dried in the later version of the Mark V instrument, which means YW is zero. The
oxygen mole fraction is measured with a Teledyne Model 320B analyzer, which provides
a signal proportional to mole fraction, and can be calibrated directly at the pressure of the
inlet air. In later instruments the CO 2 is absorbed with sodium hydroxide which removes
it from the calculations.

       DR H2O LLC               15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com            30|42

FLOW WEIGHTED AVERAGING

The single value of OTE obtained from a single analysis represents the transfer at a single
"point" in the aeration basin. The size of the point is equivalent to the size of the collection
hood. In general, larger hoods provide more representative samples of the OTE of the
entire tank.

If only a few hood locations are used, erroneous results may occur. For example, if the
hood is located over a break in an air pipe line, very low OTEs will be measured. To
obtain a representative single average value of OTE for an aeration tank, it is necessary
to sample many locations and calculate an appropriate average. In the EPA sponsored
research project (US EPA, 1989), a protocol was developed which required sampling at
least 2% of the tank surface area.

To calculate an average OTE, the individual readings must be averaged. Since aeration
basins are usually tapered, each hood location generally has a different gas flow rate. If
the gas flow rate at each hood location is known, a flow weighted average can be
calculated. For this reason, the Ewing instruments include gas flow rate meters
(rotameters) for measuring hood air flow rate, and a manometer to indicate hood
pressure. When the hood pressure is stable, gas flow rate indicated by the instrument is
equal to the hood collection flow rate.

In designing an off-gas experiment it is also necessary to select hood locations that are
representative of specific areas of the tank. This is especially important if highly tapered
aeration tanks, or tanks with irregular geometries, are being tested. To calculate a tank
average, equation A-9 is used:

 m
 å A i Q i OTE i
 i =1
 OTE =
 m (A-9)
 å A iQi
 i =1
where
 i = hood location (sample number)

 Ai = area associated with hood location i,

 Qi = air flux associated with hood location i (equals the gas flow rate
 measured by the analyzer divided by hood area),
 OTE i = oxygen transfer efficiency measured at location i, and

        DR H2O LLC         15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com           31|42

 OTE = overall average OTE.

This equation represents a flow-weighted, area-weighted average OTE. In cases where
the tank geometry is uniform, such as in a fine pore, full floor coverage aeration tank with
equal sized grids, equal areas can be incorporated into the test design, and the area
terms in equation A-9 cancel.

If other indications of gas flow rate exist, they can be compared to the gas flow rate
indicated by the instrument. The denominator of equation A-9 represents the entire tank
gas flow rate. If reliable plant instrumentation exists, one should expect the hood and
plant flow rates to correspond very closely. The ability to accurately match the two flow
rates in full scale aeration tanks has been demonstrated (Stenstrom and Masutani, 1990).
One should not expect the air flux at each hood location to match the air flux indicated by
the plant instrumentation; however, if the plant instrumentation is accurate, the average
air flow rate indicated by the instrument and plant instrumentation should agree.

CORRECTION TO STANDARD CONDITIONS

It is useful to calculate the OTE of the aeration at standard conditions, insofar as this is
possible. If the mixed-liquor dissolved oxygen, temperature and TDS are measured at
the same time OTE is measured, and if the equilibrium DO concentration ( C *¥ ) is known,
it is possible to calculate aSOTE The correction is made in the same way as clean water
data are corrected to standard conditions, as follows:

The effective depth, d e , is defined as the depth of water under which the total pressure

Generally for fine pore diffuser systems that are mounted no more than 10% of the overall
water depth above the tank floor, the value of d e will range between 21 and 44% of the
overall water depth (US EPA, 1989).

      DR H2O LLC            15 WHITMAN CT IRVINE CA 92617 USA rossodr@gmail.com       33|42

If the standard oxygen transfer efficiency (SOTE) of the aeration systems is known from
clean water tests or from manufacturer's data, the factor can be calculated, as follows:

 aSOTE
 a= (A-14)
 SOTE

The a factor is the ratio of process water to clean water mass transfer coefficients K L a . It
is generally necessary to know its value when designing aeration systems. Its
measurement is often the goal of process water testing. A new factor, F, was introduced
in 1989 in the US EPA design manual (1989). This factor represents the state of fouling
of fine pore diffusers. Generally, fine poor diffusers foul and the a factor calculated after
several years of operation, especially without cleaning, can be 50% of the new a factor.
(Stenstrom and Masutani, 1989). When testing aeration systems that have been in
operation for any considerable period of time, the aFSOTE is determined when using
equation A-10.

To calculate overall, average, a F, or a SOTEs, equation A-9 is used by replacing OTE
with the desired parameter.

SECTION 1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
A continuous off-gas testing campaign was performed by Carollo Engineers (Carollo) at the Los Angeles
County Sanitation Districts' Pomona Water Reclamation Facility (PWRF). Carollo's I-FLOAT off-gas analyzer
was used to directly measure oxygen transfer efficiency (OTE), nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions and other
key aeration performance parameters. The testing phase spanned from 11:00 AM to 1:30 PM, on
November 4, 2024. This testing was conducted in parallel to an independent sweep testing performed by
DRH2O, in Aerobic Basin #3 (AB#3), allowing for a comprehensive assessment of the spatial and temporal
variability of aeration performance and N2O emissions. This approach provided additional context and
baseline data to further validate the results obtained at the PWRF.

The testing schedule consisted of:
 Developing a sampling plan
 Conduct a continuous off-gas testing campaign of the Aerobic Zone (Zone #2) of AB#3.

Table 1 summarizes the findings and metrics obtained from the off-gas testing campaign.
Table 1 Aeration Performance and Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Summary Metrics

 the total ammonia load during the time of testing.

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 1

The main findings stemming from the off-gas testing campaign at the PWRF are:
 Average OTE for Zone #2 is 13.5 ±0.6 percent.
 Measured diffuser air fluxes for the Zone #2 are 0.31 ±0.03 scfm/sf and 0.66 ±0.07 scfm/diff.
 The observed low air fluxes from operating at low DO allowed to operate at higher SOTE values
 (29.3 ±0.3), with consequently higher standard aeration efficiency (SAE – 5.8 ±0.5).
 The alpha factor (αF) values are found to be comparable to typical values, with Zone #2 having values
    of 0.54 ±0.03.
 Aeration performance metrics between the I-FLOAT test and the testing lead by DRH2O were found
 to be comparable for Zone #2.
 N2O concentrations between the two testing campaigns showed similar results, with the I-FLOAT
 showing slightly higher concentrations (2.98 ±1.26 parts per million by volume [ppmv]). However,
 these minor variations can be contributed to temporal variations.
 The estimated N2O emission factor for Zone #2 were relatively low, with an average of 0.018
 ± 0.007 percent of NHₓ-N during the testing period.

Based on these findings, Carollo offers the following recommendations:
 Low-DO operation can yield significant energy and OPEX savings as a result of lower overall air flow
 supplied, enhanced OTE and higher AE. However, the reduced air fluxes can accelerate diffuser fouling,
 which negatively impacts long-term system performance, as reflected by the "F" factor. To address this,
 periodic air bumping is recommended, with the frequency ideally aligning with manufacturer
 recommendations or exceeding them if necessary.
 Although the estimated emission factors appear low, the 2.5-hour testing performed on 11/4 shows
 only a limited time window of the N2O emissions of Zone #2, and, as shown by past work, N2O
 emission can display high diurnal variability. Therefore, to improve accuracy and confirm these
 findings, it is recommended that the district conduct additional continuous testing to capture multiple
 diurnal profiles.
 PWRF should evaluate the potential for future N2O emissions quantification in Zone #3, as it typically
 operates at lower DO levels compared to Zone #2. Lower DO concentrations could stress ammonia-
 oxidizing and heterotrophic bacteria, potentially increasing N2O emissions. However, these emissions
 might be mitigated by the absence of aeration stripping.

SECTION 2 OFF-GAS TESTING BACKGROUND
Aeration plays a crucial role in operating conventional activated sludge (CAS) processes within water
resource recovery facilities (WRRF), constituting a significant portion of the overall energy expenditure.
The electrical costs linked to the aeration of CAS processes typically range from 45 percent to 65 percent
of the total energy expenditure of a WRRF (Rosso et al., 2005).

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 2

The off-gas technique stands as the sole method capable of directly measuring the oxygen transferred to
wastewater. Developed by Redmon et al. (1983), the off-gas technique operates by measuring the partial
pressure of specific gas compounds in the off-gas stream exiting the wastewater. Off-gas is collected via a
floating hood and then is routed through a collection hose to an analyzer that contains the
instrumentation used to measure the gas concentrations. Figure 1a provides a conceptual depiction of an
off-gas analyzer and the off-gas capture hood. Figures 1b and 1c show the installed hood and analyzer
case at a full-scale treatment plant.

Assessing the deviations from clean water conditions involves determining an αF factor, representing the
ratio of the mass transfer coefficient of process water to that of clean water (Stenstrom & Gilbert, 1981).
The product of αF and SOTE yields the field oxygen transfer efficiency at standard conditions, denoted as
αFSOTE (%). Knowledge of the airflow of each sampled zone is not required for OTE calculations using the
off-gas technique, though they are required to calculate a tank flow-average value and other parameters
such as OTR, OUR, SOTE and αF. The hood collects and directs the off-gas stream to an oxygen cell
analyzer, wherein both reference air and off-gas are analyzed at each sampling point to ensure
recalibration and maximize accuracy. These measurements can be conducted continuously or periodically
over extended periods on activated sludge basins, facilitating the monitoring of efficiency decline due to
diffuser fouling/scaling. Such measurements serve as indispensable quantitative tools for determining
cleaning frequency and establishing precise cleaning schedules.

This method provides the best technique for determining the performance of diffused aeration systems.
Knowledge of the aeration performance is critical for:
 Designing diffuser systems.
 Sizing blowers.
 Calibration of process models.
 Assessment of existing operating conditions.
 Determination of optimal cleaning frequency of the diffusers.

Important aeration metrics that can be estimated through off-gas testing include: OTE, αFSOTE, αF, OTR,
OUR, air fluxes, OTR and AE. In addition, off-gas testing can be used to monitor the release of greenhouse
gases such as methane (CH4) and N2O.

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 3

Figure 1 a) Schematic of Off-Gas Testing Set-Up. Photos of (b) Deployed Off-Gas Testing Hood and
 (c) Analyzer Case

The performance of the aeration process can vary depending on both space and time. Aeration metrics
like OTE and αF tend to be lower in the initial zones and higher in the later ones. This is because
amphiphilic compounds (i.e., oils, grease, and surfactants) accumulate at the gas-liquid interface, affecting
how much oxygen is transferred across the air/liquid interface of the bubbles. Such compounds are
gradually removed along the process because of physical and bio-degradation processes. Moreover,
variations in oxygen transfer performance also occur due to daily, weekly, and seasonal changes in the
influent loading of such amphiphilic compounds. To assess these variations, an off-gas test can be

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 4

conducted either by sweeping through the zones to capture spatial differences or by continuously testing
individual zones to monitor temporal changes.

In a sweep test, off-gas from each aerobic zone is analyzed sequentially, but the measurements are not
taken simultaneously due to the time required to move the sampling hood between points. Consequently,
metrics derived from sweep tests reflect both spatial and temporal variations. Therefore, it is crucial to
note the exact sampling times in the final summary charts of sweep tests to correctly interpret their
results. Continuous off-gas testing usually consists of fixing the sampling hood in one zone and analyzing
the off-gas over time. Both the sweep and continuous sampling strategies are useful in providing a
holistic understanding of the aeration performance of the aerobic zones and its variability.

SECTION 3 OBJECTIVE AND SCOPE
As part of Department of Energy project DE-EE0009509, the goal of this off-gas testing campaign is to
assess the aeration performance and monitor N2O emission of Zone #2 of AB #3 of the PWRF.

The test occurred November 4, 2024, from 11:00 AM to 1:30 PM. The specific objectives of the site
visit included:
1. Collecting off-gas data from the first aerobic zone (Zone #2) of Tank #3, which will be used to
 estimate key aeration performance metrics and N2O emissions rates.
2. Comparing the results obtained to other off-gas testing campaigns that occurred in parallel.

Table 2 outlines the main parameters estimated as part of the off-gas testing campaign.

This report includes off-gas testing data, continuous plots, site-visit pictures, an assessment of the site

SECTION 4 FACILITY LAYOUT
PWRF is a 15 million-gallon-per-day (mgd) capacity Modified Ludzack-Ettinger activated sludge plant
with three aeration basins in parallel divided into three sequential zones: an anoxic zone (Zone #1) and
two aerobic zones (Zone #2 and #3). The process operates under low DO conditions, with average daily
DO operations for both Zone #2 and Zone #3 are below 1 milligram per liter (mg/L) with diurnal peaks
of 1 to 1.2 mg/L. The air supply to the zones is regulated through an ammonia-based aeration
control structure.

At the time of the testing, the plant was operating at an average reduced flow of 6 mgd due to AB #1
being offline for maintenance. Return active sludge (RAS) from the secondary clarifiers is discharged at the
head of Zone #1. Solids are wasted from the RAS line and sent back to the Joint Outfall System (JOS).
Figure 2 illustrates the process flow diagram of the PWRF.

Figure 2 Process Flow Diagram of Secondary Treatment Process at Testing Site

Oxygen is supplied via a fine bubble diffuser system. The supplier is Sanitaire, and the diffuser model is
the 9-inch Silver Series II EPDM discs. A summary of the aeration grid layout is provided in Table 3.
Appendix B – Specifications and Dimensions includes more information about zone specifications.

SECTION 5 MATERIALS AND METHODOLOGY
5.1 Materials

5.1.1 I-FLOAT Hood System
The I-FLOAT hood and analyzer design mirrors the flux chamber systems utilized by ETH Zurich (Gruber
et al., 2020, 2021) and the UC, Irvine WEX Center (Myers et al., 2021; Reifsnyder et al., 2018; Rosso, 2018).
This novel hood design is equipped with a self-contained ballast system that stabilizes the hood during
testing while still ensuring its portability and ease of installation. The self-ballasting design incorporates a
moat that can be filled with on-site water, creating an effective seal from ambient air and stabilizing it
during turbulent conditions.

Prior to deploying the hood, a collection hose, a pressure measurement line and a set of ropes are
connected to it. The collected hose routes the off-gas to the gas sensors in the analyzer case, and the
pressure measurement line provides accurate readings of the pressure buildup inside the hood.

5.1.2 I-FLOAT Analyzer System
Carollo's I-FLOAT analyzer is provided with the ability to continuously log over time aeration performance
metrics (OTE, αFSOTE, αF, OTR, OUR, AE, etc.) and direct N2O emissions. Aeration performance metrics are
continuously calibrated and estimated by cycling the gas sampling line between off-gas and ambient air.
The continuous referencing of off-gas oxygen concentrations to ambient is performed to constantly re-
calibrate the oxygen sensor, thus maximizing accuracy. Figure A.1 in Appendix A – Additional Materials
and Methods illustrates an example of the raw data measurements during these cycles.

N2O is continuously measured using two non-dispersive infrared (NDIR) gas sensors. The two N2O sensors
have different measurement ranges, a low range (0 to 100 ppmv) and a high-range (0 to 2000 ppmv)
sensor. The selection of two sensors with different measurement ranges allows the wide range of N2O
concentrations that has been previously reported in other studies (Chandran, 2016; Porro et al., 2017;
Valkova et al., 2021). The lower range N2O sensor provides readings with an accuracy of ± 2 ppmv. The
large range N2O sensor provides reading accuracies of ± 20 ppmv. Prior and during the test, a suite of
calibration gases is used to calibrate and check that the NDIR instruments provide accurate
measurements. During testing, these are passed through the NDIR analyzers once per day to ensure
readings are accurate. For each sensor, a span calibration is performed on-site prior to each test using a
zero-reference gas and a reference gas mixture with a concentration of the gas of interest that is
comparable to the upper measurement range of the individual gas sensors. Additional sensors that are
included in the analyzer case are DO, oxygen gas and carbon dioxide.

5.2 Testing Procedures and Sampling Plan

5.2.1 Sampling Methodology
The ASCE 18-18 standards recommend that off-gas testing to assess aeration metrics should sample at
least 2 percent of the tank surface area within aerobic zones to ensure a representative spatial
distribution. For the testing conducted at PWRF, a continuous temporal profile was collected by fixing the

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 7

hood in Zone #3, as the objective was to capture temporal trends rather than a spatial profile. Carollo's
I-FLOAT hood, used for this testing, has an internal collection area of approximately 32 square feet (sq ft).
According to the as-built drawings, the total surface area of Zone #2 is 2,600 sq ft. Using a single
sampling position, the I-FLOAT hood covered 1.2 percent of Zone #2's surface area.

Upon arrival at the site, the analyzer is turned on and allowed to warm up for 30 minutes to enable the
temperature stabilization of the instrumentation. Meanwhile, the hood/s are inflated, and the relative off-
gas lines (off-gas routing hose and pressure monitoring tube) and stabilizing ropes are connected. After
the instrument warm-up period, the calibration of the gas sensors is performed using the standard gas
mixtures. The hood is deployed in the tank and the outer moat ring is filled with water from the plant. This
step provides a seal to avoid contamination from the atmosphere and ensures stabilization of the hood.
The hood is then positioned in the desired sampling location and the off-gas lines are connected to the
analyzer system. Additional liquid-phase instrumentation can also be deployed side-by-side the hood to
capture the dynamics of the concentrations in the liquid phase (oxygen, nitrous oxide, etc.) The analyzer
then begins logging the real-time measurements of the gas and liquid process variables.

For the PWRF testing campaign, grab samples of the off-gas were collected from an independent off-gas
testing campaign lead by DRH2O and were sent to a local laboratory for independent analysis and
verification of the N2O concentrations using a gas chromatographer.

5.2.2 Real-Time Data Quality Control
During the data collection campaign, a router installed in the analyzer allows for real-time transmission of
the data under a dedicated cellular plan. The ability to remotely monitor the real-time data collection
phase allows the Carollo team to screen incoming data continuously to ensure the quality of the data
being collected. The identification of anomalous behavior is followed by an alarm system that swiftly
notified the Carollo team.

5.2.3 Sampling Plan
The testing period was performed as follows:
 11/04 at 11:00 AM: Deployment and continuous testing of Zone #2.
 11/04 at 1:30 PM: End of testing and decommissioning.

Before testing, an initial sampling plan was created to assess accessibility requirements and identify
sampling points. This plan guided the testing process by outlining key details such as dimensions,
structural components, sample locations, and potential obstacles, including surface pipes, on-site
instrumentation, or walkways. Figure 3 shows an aerial view of the bioreactor layout and the designated
sampling locations. The positions of the hoods are denoted by blue octagon icons. Rectangular shapes
indicate the entry and exit points of the hood that allow free of obstacles access points.

Table 4 shows the timeline schedule of the off-gas testing campaign at the PWRF.

SECTION 6 AERATION PERFORMANCE RESULTS
Off-gas metrics are estimated at 15-minute intervals. This sampling frequency is well-suited for large-scale
aeration systems, given their inherent time constants, which typically range between 15 to 30 minutes.
This means that any adjustments made to the aeration control system will reflect their complete impact
on the oxygen transfer process and dissolved oxygen levels within this timeframe. OTE was corrected to
standard conditions (i.e., αFSOTE, %) using site-specific temperature and pressure data, and process DO
values. Appendix A includes details on the OTE data processing methodology.

Figure 4 shows the hood positions during the off-gas measurement campaign.

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 9

Figure 4 Photos Taken during Testing Campaign: a) Deployed Hood and b) Analyzer System

6.1 Oxygen Transfer Efficiency
OTE values were estimated by measuring the oxygen molar ratios to inert gas fractions of the inlet air
(ambient) and the outlet (off-gas) streams based on Redmon et al. (1983). OTE can be corrected to
standard conditions in process water (i.e., αFSOTE, %) using site-specific temperature and pressure data,
and process DO values. For plug-flow reactors, the OTE along the tank will typically display a
monotonically increasing trend. This trend is usually due to tapered airflows and higher αF value in the
direction of flow. However, when primary effluent step-feed points are present along the tank, OTE and
αFSOTE values can be depressed by re-introducing amphiphilic compounds that hinder the oxygen
transfer across the air/liquid interface of the bubbles. Figure 5 shows the estimated OTE and αFSOTE
profiles, along with the measured air fluxes for the entire testing period. Nominal conditions were met
throughout the testing period. OTE and αFSOTE values that were independently measured from DRH2O
directly upstream and downstream of the I-FLOAT hood are also reported in Figure 5. The values obtained
independently from sampling positions (1A, 1B, 2A, 2B, 3A, and 3B – see Figure 3) were weighted and
averaged using the corresponding independently measured air fluxes.

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 10

Figure 5 Continuous Profiles of OTE And αFSOTE

OTE values measured by the I-FLOAT averaged 13.5 ±0.6, with values ranging between 12.5 to
14.3 percent. Air fluxes rates for Zone #2 were 0.31 ±0.03 scfm/sfBasin (0.66 ±0.07 scfm/sfDiff). These
relatively low airflow rates are a result of the low DO operation at PWRF. A visual assessment of the air
fluxes Zone #2 can be seen in Figure 4. No abnormal distribution of surfacing bubble plumes was
observed at the time of the testing (e.g., preferential plumes or "bubble boils"). The inverse correlation
between OTE and airflow rates stems from lower coalescence rates between bubbles, smaller bubble
diameters, higher bubble residence time and higher overall specific surface area of the bubble plume.
Average DO concentrations of 1.2 ±0.1 mg/L were observed throughout the testing period (see Figure C.1
in Appendix C – Additional Process Data).

6.2 Alpha Factor
αF values are estimated by taking the ratio of the αFSOTE and SOTE estimates. Therefore, to provide a
reasonably accurate αF factor, it is important to obtain representative estimates of the manufacturer clean
water test SOTE curves of the installed diffusers, along with the installed submergence depth. For this off-gas
testing campaign a power regression SOTE equations was considered to approximate the Sanitaire 9-inch
Silver Series II EPDM discs based on SOTE clean water test data provided in the specification from Sanitaire.
When juxtaposed with the manufacturer's clean water test SOTE data, the calibrated SOTE equation (%/foot)
was consistent with the manufacturer's performance prediction (see Figure A.2 in Appendix A).

Figure 6 shows the estimated αF values for the continuous testing of Zone #2, along with the respective
airflow rates. During peak loading times of the day, an increased airflow is supplied to match ammonia
setpoints. During this period, alongside the expected decrease in SOTE due to higher airflow, the αF factor
is also reduced due to a higher loading of compounds inhibiting the interfacial oxygen transfer of the
bubbles. This compounded effect exacerbates the energy demand, whereby precisely when oxygen

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 11

demand peaks, the efficiency of the oxygen transfer process declines, and the blower power requirement
is magnified.

At the start of the aeration process, αF factors are typically lower due to higher concentrations of
surfactants and other amphiphilic compounds, which hinder oxygen transfer by accumulating at the gas-
liquid interface. These compounds are progressively removed through bubble adsorption and bio-
physical degradation, resulting in increasing αF values along the flow path. Throughout the testing period,
αF factors of the first aerobic zone (Zone #2) ranged between 0.50 and 0.58 with an average of 0.54 ±0.03.

Figure 6 αF and Airflow Profiles during Testing Zone #2

SECTION 7 NITROUS OXIDE EMISSIONS
Off-gas testing proves effective for monitoring the release rate of GHG in aeration tanks. One of the most
impactful contributions to the carbon footprint of WRRFs is the release of N2O during the secondary
treatment, tertiary treatment, and side-stream treatment stages. Nitrous oxide is a potent greenhouse gas,
with a global warming potential approximately 270 times greater than carbon dioxide.

7.1 Nitrous Oxide Emissions Results
For this testing, the determination of the N2O fluxes and emissions rates follow the protocol outlined in
Chandran, 2013. By knowing the airflow rates, gas concentrations, and the surface area of the hood, flux
rates of gases like N2O can be estimated, typically reported as gN2O m-2 d-1 or gCO2,eq m-2 d-1. The
relatively large surface area of the I-FLOAT hood serves to minimize estimation errors of these GHG flux
rates.The emission factor is the ratio of nitrogen emitted as N2O over the influent nitrogen load. Emission
factors are usually reported as % of N2O-N over NHx-N, TKN-N or TN-N. In this study, the emission factor
is reported as a fraction of influent ammonia (% of NHx-N).

The data processing involves removing cross-interferences of CO2 on the N2O measurements to ensure
accurate readings from the NDIR sensor. This step was critical for isolating the N2O signal and eliminating
potential biases caused by overlapping absorption spectrum between CO2 and N2O. Grab samples were
taken using Tedlar bags from the parallel testing performed by DRH2O and shipped to a local laboratory
for an independent analysis.

Figure 7 shows the N2O concentrations in the off-gas for the entire testing period. After correcting cross-
interferences, the continuous profiles of the N2O measurements from the I-FLOAT show values that are
slightly higher than the ones obtained from the lab values reported by DRH2O although they are within
the same order of magnitude. No significantly relevant diurnal pattern could be recognized from the N2O
concentrations observed given the ±2 ppmv accuracy range of the I-FLOAT instrumentation. This is likely
due to the relatively low observed N2O concentrations between 0 to 5 ppmv. Table 6 includes a summary
of the NO2 emissions metrics.

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 13

Figure 7 Continuous Profiles and Grab Samples of N2O Concentrations throughout Testing Campaign

 and a daily composite ammonia concentration of 37 mg/L, representing N2O emissions from the individual zones relative to
 the total ammonia load during the time of testing.

The estimated N2O emission factor for aerobic Zone #2 of AB #3 at the PWRF facility is
0.018 ± 0.007 percent of the primary effluent NHₓ-N during the testing period. To improve accuracy, it is
recommended that the district conduct additional continuous testing to capture multiple diurnal profiles.
The 2.5-hour testing shows only a short time window of the N2O emissions of Zone #2, and, as shown by
past work, N2O emission can display high diurnal variability.

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 14

SECTION 8 CONCLUSIONS
Main findings stemming from the off-gas testing campaign at the PRWF:
 Average OTE for the aerobic zone (Zone #2) is 13.5 ±0.6 percent.
 Measured diffuser air fluxes for the aerobic zone (Zone #2) are 0.31 ±0.03 scfm/sf and
    0.66 ±0.07 scfm/diff.
 The observed low air fluxes from operating at low DO allowed to operate at higher SOTE values
 (29.3 ±0.3), with consequently higher standard aeration efficiency (SAE – 5.8 ±0.5).
 αF values are found to be comparable to typical values, with Zone #2 having values of 0.54 ±0.03.
 Aeration performance metrics between the I-FLOAT test and the testing lead by DRH2O were found
 to be comparable for Zone #2.
 N2O concentrations between the two testing campaigns showed similar results, with the I-FLOAT
 showing slightly higher concentrations (2.98 ±1.26 ppmv).
 The estimated N2O emission factor for aerobic Zone #2 of AB#3 at the PP facility is 0.018 ±0.007 percent
 of NHₓ-N during the testing period. However, to improve accuracy, it is recommended that the district
 conduct additional continuous testing to capture multiple diurnal profiles.

Carollo offers the following recommendations:
 Operating at low DO offers advantages such as lower aeration demand, higher OTE and higher AE
 However, the lower air flux associated with these conditions can lead to more rapid fouling of the
 diffusers, potentially accelerating the long-term decline in system performance, as reflected by the "F"
 factor. To mitigate this, regular air bumping is recommended, ideally following the manufacturer's
 suggested schedule—or more frequently if needed.
 While the estimated emission factors appear low, the 2.5-hour testing represents only a limited
 snapshot of N2O emissions from Zone #2. Previous studies have demonstrated that N2O emissions
 can exhibit significant diurnal variability. To enhance accuracy and validate these findings, it is
 recommended that the district undertake additional continuous testing to capture multiple
 diurnal profiles.
 PWRF should consider future quantification of N2O emissions in Zone #3, since Zone #3 generally
 runs lower DOs than Zone #2. Lower DO may cause stress to ammonia oxidizing and heterotrophic
 bacteria, which may lead to increased N2O emissions. However, any emissions may be offset by lack of
 aeration stripping.

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336) 15

APPENDIX A ADDITIONAL MATERIALS AND METHODS

INK DOE SUBOXIC NITROGEN REMOVAL (DE-FOA-0002336)

 APPENDIX A – ADDITIONAL MATERIALS AND METHODS

OFF-GAS SAMPLING CYCLE
The off-gas analyzer is built to cycle between air and off-gas and to determine the oxygen content of
each. The air and off-gas sample lines are cycled through a 3-way valve which is controlled by the
programmable logic controller (PLC) system. A vacuum pump constantly draws a vacuum on the sampling
line. For this test the air line is sampled for 5 minutes and the off-gas for 10 minutes. The average of the
last three samples for the air and off-gas are used to estimate oxygen tranfer efficiency OTE values. After
the valve, the samples go through a nafion tubing and a sorption column filled with indicating desiccant
(turning from blue to pink as more water is absorbed). The sorption column removes any water of the
samples before flowing through the instrumentation. Carbon dioxide and nitrous oxide are continuously
measured. The oxygen (O2) sensor transmits a signal to the O2 display which displays the oxygen content
in percentage and outputs a 4 to 20 milliampere (mA) signal in proportion to a 0 to 25 percent oxygen
content range. The 4 to 20 mA signal is transmitted to the PLC and HMI systems. Data is continuously
logged and is accessible via a router, which allows the ability to connect remotely to the analyzer system.

Figure A.1 Raw Data of Oxygen Partial Pressure Showing Sampling Cycle Between Air and Off-Gas Lines
