APPENDIX F MICROBIAL COMMUNITY ANALYSIS
 REPORTS

Metagenomic Analyses of Microbial Communities in Biological Nutrient Removal Plants
(HRSD and LACSD) Operated with High and Low Dissolved Oxygen Conditions

Blaise Enuh and Daniel R. Noguera
Department of Civil and Environmental Engineering
Wisconsin Energy Institute
University of Wisconsin - Madison

March 28, 2025

Background

This report provides a summary of the metagenomic analysis conducted on samples from the
biological nutrient removal (BNR) plants at the Hampton Roads Sanitary District (HRSD) and Los
Angeles County Sanitation Districts (LACSD) that were tested in the DOE project “Transforming
Aeration Energy in Water Resource Recovery Facilities (WRRFs) through Suboxic Nitrogen
Removal” led by Carollo Engineers.

HRSD operated a pilot plant to evaluate BNR when aeration was reduced stepwise, and all other
parameters maintained constant. For metagenomic analysis, samples were collected at the beginning
of operation, when the dissolved oxygen (DO) condition was high, and at the end of operation, when
the DO concentrations was low (Table 1).

LACSD used one of its full-scale plants (Pomona) to evaluate aeration control strategies that would
allow for efficient BNR operation with lower DO conditions. Activated sludge samples were
collected at the beginning of operation, with high DO conditions, and when aeration had been
reduced to the lowest condition in the study (Table 1).

 Table 1: Sample Source and collection dates

 BNR Plant Condition Collection Date Code Sample

                 LACSD        High DO      20/06/2023          152-5      Sample 1
                                                               152-2      Sample 2
                                                               152-1      Sample 3
                              Low DO       03/09/2024          458-5      Sample 1
                                           03/09/2024          458-1      Sample 2
                 HRSD         High DO      07/15/2022          22-07-15   Sample 1
                                           07/29/2022          22-07-29   Sample 2
                              Low DO       01/12/2024          24-01-12   Sample 1
                                           01/02/2024          24-01-02   Sample 2

 1

Sample Processing for Metagenomic Analysis

The collected activated sludge samples were sent via overnight shipping to our laboratory at the
University of Wisconsin – Madison. The samples were processed immediately upon arrival to the
laboratory. Genomic DNA was extracted using the DNeasy PowerSoil Kit (Qiagen), and the quality
of extracts was verified using a NanoDrop™ One spectrophotometer and Agilent FemtoPulse
System. DNA concentrations were determined via a Qubit™ fluorometer with the dsDNA High
Sensitivity assay.

The extracted DNA was submitted to the UW-Madison Biotechnology Center for metagenomic
analysis, where the samples were prepared for sequencing by mechanical shearing using Covaris
gTUBEs and size-selection with diluted AMPurePB beads. Metagenomic libraries were constructed
using a Pacific Biosciences HiFi workflow on a PacBio Sequel II instrument. After sequencing, the
metagenomic library quality was confirmed on the Agilent FemtoPulse, and quantification was
performed with a Qubit™ assay.

Sequencing depth significantly varied for the samples analyzed, with the high-DO samples from both
plants resulting in 2 to 4 billion nucleotides sequenced, whereas the low-DO sequencing from both
plants resulted in 24 to 60 billion nucleotides sequenced (Table 2). Given the differences in
sequencing depth observed in the metagenomic libraries, two different methods, metaFLYE and
metaMDBG, were used to assemble the CCS reads into contigs, and the best assemblies were
selected. Polishing of assemblies was carried out with racon (v1.4.20), and the resulting contigs were
refined by mapping the original reads using minimap2. Quality control measures ensured the removal
of low-quality or contaminated contigs prior to binning. After assembly, the number of resulting
contigs was also one order of magnitude higher for the low-DO sequences compared to the high-DO
sequences (Table 2), making comparisons difficult. Contigs were binned into metagenome
assembled genomes (MAGs) using metaBAT2 (v2.15).

Table 2: Bases sequenced, and number of MAGs assembled for each sample.

 Total length of Total MAGs with
 Pilot DO Number of bases Total
 Sample contigs assembled completeness >75% &
 Plant conditions sequenced (bp) MAGs
 (bp) contamination <10%

 LACSD     High DO      Sample 1      2,627,081,031            436,845,321        72            7
                        Sample 2      3,394,079,296            585,808,007        83           10
                        Sample 3      3,366,621,245            579,396,708       107           12
           Low DO       Sample 1     52,549,854,612          4,769,110,291      1675          213
                        Sample 2     60,299,141,438          5,170,479,726      1858          250
 HRSD      High DO      Sample 1      4,281,631,841            555,970,505       116           27
                        Sample 2      2,779,942,355            421,664,957        90           17
           Low DO       Sample 1     28,725,865,507          2,133,638,439       680          126
                        Sample 2     24,503,472,709          1,915,073,386       794          146

 2

Contamination in bins was identified using ProDeGe in conjunction with tetranucleotide frequency
analysis, and affected contigs were excised. MAGs were evaluated with CheckM (v1.2.2) to
determine completeness and contamination, with only those having >75% completeness and <10%
contamination retained for further analysis. Again, the number of MAGs obtained from these
samples was about an order of magnitude lower for the high-DO samples from both plants (Table
2). In total, 808 MAGs with greater than 75% completeness and less than 10% contamination were
recovered from these samples, with 492 MAGs obtained from the LACSD samples and 316 MAGs
from the HRSD samples. See supplementary table in excel file.

Taxonomic classification of MAGs was performed using GTDB-Tk (v2.1.0), and functional
annotation was achieved using Prokka and the NCBI Prokaryotic Genome Annotation Pipeline
(PGAP v6.8).

To determine which MAGs were unique, a redundancy analysis was performed to identify similar
MAGs and select the highest-quality representative from each group. To this end, a dereplication
was performed for low-DO and high-DO MAGs from each treatment plant using dRep (v0.6.1). A
95% threshold for average nucleotide identity (ANI) was used to cluster the MAGs (see excel Table).
This analysis resulted in 304 and 207 unique MAGs for LACSD and HRSD respectively with only
8 unique MAGs common between the low-DO and high-DO samples from LACSD, and 16 unique
MAGs common between the low-DO and high-DO samples from HRSD (Figure 1).

Figure 1. Unique MAGs for each DO condition and the number of unique MAGs common across
DO conditions.

We investigated the phylogenetic relationship among the representatives of the MAGs for each
facility. Phylogenetic relationships were inferred using maximum likelihood analysis in RAxML-
NG (v1.2.1). An alignment of the target sequences was analyzed under the GTR+GAMMA model,
and branch support was assessed with 100 rapid bootstrap replicates. The resulting tree was then
imported into TreeViewer for visualization, where clade structures and support values were
annotated and optimized for clear presentation. There were 21 and 17 phyla represented in the
LACSD and HRSD samples, respectively. The maps highlight observed groups relevant to BNR,
such as Accumulibacter, Nitrosomonas, and Nitrospira, while also showcasing the considerable
diversity of other microbial phyla present in the activated sludge communities (Figures 2 and 3).

 3

Figure 2. Phylogenetic map showing an overview of community structure based on the 304 unique
MAG from the LACSD plant assembled from both low and high DO samples. From the innermost
to the outermost ring: The innermost ring represents phylogenetic clustering of MAGs. The names
of each phylum are colored according to the clusters they represent. N/A indicates MAGs that were
not classified into any phylum. The Accumulibacter, Nitrospira and Nitrosomonas genera are
labelled with stars of different colors. The next ring in orange points to MAGs that were assembled
from the high DO samples while the blue ring immediately after it points to MAGs that were
assembled from the low DO samples.

 4

Figure 3. Phylogenetic map showing an overview of community structure based on the 207 unique
MAGs from the HRSD plant assembled from both low and high DO samples. From the innermost
to the outermost ring: The innermost ring represents phylogenetic clustering of MAGs. The names
of the phylum are colored according to the clusters they represent. The Accumulibacter and
Nitrospira genera are labelled with stars of different colors. MAGs corresponding to the
Nitrosomonas genus were not assembled. The next ring in orange points to MAGs that were
assembled from the high DO samples while the blue ring points to MAGs that were assembled from
the low DO samples.

Enhanced Biological Phosphorus Removal: Analysis of Accumulibacter MAGs

Two MAGs classified as Accumulibacter were recovered from the high-DO LACSD samples and
three from the HRSD samples (2 from high-DO and 1 from low-DO). No Accumulibacter were
obtained from the low-DO LACSD samples despite the higher sequencing depth (Figure 4).

The 2 LACSD MAGs from high-DO were of high quality, showed high similarity (with >98% ANI)
to the reference genome of Accumulibacter propinquus (see excel table), and phylogenetically
clustered with members of this genus (Figure 4).

The 2 HRSD Accumulibacter MAGs from high-DO were of high quality (100% completeness with
0% contamination) and formed a separate cluster with the lone Accumulibacter MAG from low-DO,
which was of medium quality (76.35% completion and 2.37% contamination). The phylogenetic

 5

analysis placed these MAGs in their own cluster and not associated with any of the recognized
Accumulibater genera (Figure 4).

Figure 4. MASH clustering based on an average nucleotide identity (ANI) threshold of 95%,
comparing all Accumulibacter MAGs recovered from both the LACSD and HRSD plants with
reference Accumulibacter genomes. The HRSD Accumulibacter cluster comprises three MAGs
distinctly forming a group separate from known Accumulibacter genera. The LACSD
Accumulibacter MAGs clustered with other known genomes of A. propinquus.

Nitrification: Analysis of Nitrosomonas MAGs

Only the low-DO LACSD metagenomes yielded MAGs from the genus Nitrosomonas that satisfied
the completeness (>75%) and contamination (<10%) thresholds (See excel table). GTDB-Tk
classification placed them near GCA_008015595.1 a Nitrosomonas sp. Bin 15_1 MAG (with ~99%
ANI) (See excel table). Phylogenetic clustering revealed that the LACSD-derived MAGs
(LACSD_LDO1_Nitrosomonas sp. and LACSD_LDO2_Nitrosomonas sp.) clustered closely
together, forming a distinct branch that did not correspond to known Nitrosomonas genera (Figure
5). Since the high-DO LACSD samples did not yield any MAG for Nitrosomonas, a comparison
between low and high DO samples could not be made. Likewise, the absence of assembled MAGs
for the HRSD samples does not provide information to assess Nitrosomonas in these samples.

 6

Figure 5. MASH clustering based on an ANI threshold of 95%, comparing the two Nitrosomonas
MAGs recovered from both the LACSD plant with reference Nitrosomonas genomes. The LACSD-
derived MAGs (LACSD_LDO1_Nitrosomonas sp. and LACSD_LDO2_Nitrosomonas sp.) did not
cluster with genomes from any recognized Nitrosomonas genera. No MAGs were assembled from
the HRSD samples.

Nitrification: Analysis of Nitrospira MAGs

One MAG classified by GTDB-Tk as Nitrospira was assembled from the LACSD samples and two
from the HRSD samples (see excel table). The lone LACSD MAG was obtained from the low-DO
sample and had 97.67% completeness and 3.18% contamination. Taxonomic analysis via GTDB-Tk
classified this MAG as belonging to the Nitrospira_A group, and a phylogenetic analysis revealed
that the MAG was closely related to the reference genome of Nitrospira defluvii, with an ANI >95%
to reference genomes (Figure 6). N. defluvii is a canonical nitrite oxidizing bacterium (NOB).

The 2 Nitrospira MAGs assembled from the HRSD samples were obtained from both the low-DO
and high-DO samples. Taxonomic analysis via GTDB-Tk classified the high-DO MAG as belonging
to the Nitrospira_A group (95.85% completeness and 5.45% contamination) and the low-DO MAG
(85.91% completeness and 2.05% contamination) belonging to the Nitrospira_F group (see excel
table). The phylogenetic analysis showed that the high-DO MAG was associated with N. defluvii
whereas the low-DO MAG was associated with Candidatus Nitrospira nitrosa (Figure 6). Cand. N.
nitrosa is a recognized complete ammonia oxidizer (comammox), suggesting an increase in the
abundance and activity of comammox organisms when the plant was operated at low DO conditions.

 7

Figure 6. MASH clustering based on an ANI threshold of 95%, comparing all Nitrospira MAGs
recovered from both the LACSD and HRSD plants to reference Nitrospira genomes. The low-DO
LACSD Nitrospira MAG clustered with Nitrospira defluvii, suggesting it belongs within this
recognized NOB genus. Similarly, the high-DO Nitrospira MAG from HRSD formed a separate but
closely related branch, indicating close alignment with N. defluvii, and suggesting that this
microorganism performs the canonical nitrite oxidation function. The low-DO Nitrospira MAG from
HRSD clustered with Candidatus Nitrospira nitrosa, suggesting that the MAG represents a
comammox microorganism.

Nitrification: Relative abundance of Nitrosomonas and Nitrospira MAGs

Since there was an apparent change in the Nitrospira community as the HRSD plant transitioned
from high DO to low DO, we evaluated the relative abundance of all the Nitrosomonas and
Nitrospira MAGs assembled from HRSD and LACSD (Figure 7). The relative abundance was
estimated for each metagenomic sample (Table 1) by mapping DNA reads to the assembled MAGs.
In the high-DO HRSD samples, the canonical NOB related to N. defluvii showed the highest relative
abundance, accounting for about 1% of the DNA reads. DNA reads also mapped to the N. defluvii-
related MAG that was assemble from the LACSD sample, albeit with a lower relative abundance.
No reads mapped to the N. nitrosa-related comammox MAG or to the LACSD-assembled
Nitrosomonas MAGs.

In the low-DO HRSD samples, the relative abundance of the N. defluvii-related MAGs decreases,
with the HRSD-assembled MAG showing a relative abundance of 0.1%. At the same time, the
comammox-related MAG becomes detectable in these low-DO samples, with a relative abundance
of about 0.1% (Figure 7). The LACSD-assemble Nitrosomonas MAGs are undetectable in these
 8

samples. This relative abundance calculations support the hypothesis that lowering DO in the HRSD
plant resulted in a shift of Nitrospira from canonical NOB to comammox.

The relative abundance of the Nitrosomonas and Nitrospira MAGs in the LACSD samples exhibits
a more balanced presence throughout the operation of this plant, without any significant changes in
the relative abundance of detected MAGs. For all samples, each Nitrosomonas MAG was detected
with a relative abundance of about 0.15%, and the N. defluvii-related MAG was present with a
relative abundance of about 0.4%. The only noticeable difference in this analysis is the detection of
the HRSD-assembled N. defluvii-related MAG in the low-DO samples only (Figure 7). Thus, this
analysis does not suggest a shift of nitrifying populations in LACSD as a result of lowering the DO
during operation.

Figure 7. Relative abundance of Nitrospira and Nitrosomonas MAGs recovered from HRSD and
LACSD plants in the high DO (HDO) and low DO (LDO) metagenomes. The Nitrospira MAGs
closely associated with the canonical N. defluvii NOB are denoted as Nitrospira_A, whereas the
MAG identified as comammox and related to N. nitrosa is denoted as Nitrospira_F and marked with
**.

Conclusions

This report provides a summary of the metagenomic analysis conducted on samples collected from
the LACSD and HRSD wastewater treatment plants operated at high and low DO conditions. There
were significant disparities in the sequencing depth obtained from the low-DO and high-DO samples
which made comparisons difficult and limited the ability to compare microbial communities at the
two DO conditions. Sequencing of the high-DO samples from both plants resulted in 2 to 4 billion
nucleotides, whereas the low-DO sequencing resulted in 24 to 60 billion nucleotides sequenced.

 9

After MAG assembly, application of completeness and contamination thresholds, and dereplication
to remove MAGs that represented the same microorganisms, we obtained 304 and 207 unique MAGs
for LACSD and HRSD, respectively, with most of the MAGs obtained from the better sequencing
that resulted from the low-DO samples.

In the context of enhanced biological phosphorus removal, Accumulibacter MAGs were assembled
from both treatment plants examined. Specifically, A. propinquus was detected in the samples from
LACSD (high DO), while an unclassified Accumulibacter MAG was recovered from the HRSD
samples (both high and low-DO). No shift in Accumulibacter populations can be inferred from the
metagenomic data obtained.

With respect to nitrification, the assembled MAGs and the relative abundance analysis support the
observation of a shift from canonical NOB to comammox in the HRSD plant, whereas no change
occurred in the LACSD plant. At HRSD, the relative abundance of N. defluvii-related NOB
decreased and the N. nitrosa-related comammox became detectable in the low-DO samples
compared to the high-DO samples. No MAGs of canonical ammonia oxidizing bacteria (AOB) were
assembled from the HRSD samples, and therefore, this metagenomic analysis did not produce
information to allow an analysis of canonical AOB.

 10

APPENDIX G COMMERCIALIZATION PLANS

VENTURA AI
 CONTROL
Maximum Treatment Performance
 Minimum Energy Consumption

 VENTURA AI
Advanced Aeration - Model Predictive Control
APG Neuros’ Ventura AI Control - Model Predictive Control System empowers Water
Resource Recovery Facilities to take control of their biological secondary treatment
processes. Ventura AI Control utilizes state-of-the-art algorithms to deliver
unprecedented performance from the entire aeration system—from the blowers to the
dissolved oxygen in the tank and everything in between. Including optional Ammonia
Based Aeration Control unlocks further savings potential by dynamically changing the
dissolved oxygen setpoints based on the loading.

Ventura AI - Model Predictive Control
Ventura AI - Model Predictive Control (MPC) is the brains behind Advanced Aeration Control’s
brilliance. Unlike traditional Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID) control algorithms, Ventura AI
MPC enables Advanced Aeration Control (AAC) to compensate for the nonlinearities inherent to
the secondary treatment process. The end result is accurate control over the full range of
operating conditions… all without the need for manual re-tuning.
Model Know How
The core of Ventura AI MPC is the prediction models. Ventura AI incorporates multiple model
types, including mechanistic, statistical, and machine learning, to predict how each component
of the process responds to internal and external changes. These predictions are used to calculate
the optimal control steps required to maintain process parameters at their setpoints. And, by
using models to forecast influent conditions into the near future, Ventura reacts to influent
disturbances before they can impact the final effluent.
Peace of Mind
A control system is only as good as the information going into it. Ventura AI includes robust fault
detection, isolation, and recovery (FDIR) procedures to identify instrument and other field device
malfunctions. Ventura’s fault detection goes beyond simple signal integrity and range checks. By
comparing instrument responses to historical, comparable instrument, and predicted responses,
Ventura AI can identify faults before they adversely effect the process. Once a faulty device is
identified, Ventura removes it from future calculations. For every device in the aeration system,
Ventura includes logic on how to minimize the disturbance on the rest of the system. Developing
this logic is a collaborative effort between APG Neuros engineers and plant operators during
commissioning.
Advanced Aeration Control Service
The Advanced Aeration Control Service (AACS) powered by Ventura AI is APG Neuros’
commitment to providing customers with best in class service and support. Ventura includes:
access to APG Neuros’ 24 hour a day—365 days a year customer service support line, an
extended warranty to keep Ventura always up to date with the latest enhancements, and
monthly reporting on the performance of the process by APG Neuros experts. Additionally,
with Ventura, APG Neuros engineers meet semi-annually with plant operators and annually
with plant management ensure the system is always exceeding expectations.

 VENTURA AI CONTROL LOOPS
vENTURA AI consists of up to four cascaded control loops working in harmony:

Ammonia Control Loop (Optional)
· Optimal DO setpoints are calculated to control the effluent ammonia to an operator setpoint
· Ammonia Based Aeration Control (ABAC)
DO Control Loop
· Airflow setpoints for each zone are calculated to meet ABAC or manually entered DO
setpoints
· Ventura AI accounts for nonlinear system dynamics, improving accuracy compared to
traditional PID control
Blower Control Loop
· The number of blowers and blower speeds are adjusted to match the total airflow
requirement
· Blower speed dynamically adjusts to ambient conditions
Valve Control Loop
· Each control valve is precisely adjusted to achieve the zone’s airflow setpoint
· Includes dynamic most open valve control to minimize system pressure
· Ventura reduces the number of valve starts/stops to prolong the actuator lifespan
· Linear valves and/or high precision actuators can extend the controllable airflow range, but
are not required
Process Performance
Historically, aeration control systems have required experts to constantly monitor and re- tuning
them to maintain performance. Without tuning, traditional control degrades over time until the
system is often run in manual. Ventura provides continuous control accuracy without the need for
manual re-tuning. This enables operators to manually set their system with confidence that the
setpoints and hence the treatment goals will be met. Alternatively, with ABAC, Ventura can
automatically set the DO setpoints to meet the required effluent ammonia limits. As permit limits
become more strict and treatment process complexity increases, the accuracy of Ventura is critical
to maximizing process performance.
Energy Savings
Ventura enables facilities to minimize the power consumption of the aeration system, which is
usually the most energy intensive process in a facility. Ventura’s dynamic most open valve control
enables the blowers to operate at a lower pressure, supplying the same amount of airflow at a
reduced horsepower. The accuracy of Ventura’s DO control allows DO setpoints to be set lower
than they would otherwise be set at, reducing the airflow requirement. Ventura’s DO control
accuracy also minimizes overshoot, further reducing the airflow requirement. Finally, ABAC
control only supplies enough dissolved oxygen to meet the desired level of treatment, preventing
overaerating. Combined, Ventura can reduce blower power consumption by 10% to greater than
40%, depending on the current conditions.

 SYSTEM DESCRIPTION

Each Ventura AI Control system is customized to the site specific needs requirements.
Generally, Ventura is implemented on an industrial PC that is incorporated into the
blower master control panel (MCP). The industrial PC reads instrument values from the
MCP PLC, performs its calculations, and writes optimized setpoints back to the MCP PLC
which then implements them in the plant. Field devices can be landed directly in the
MCP or communicated to the MCP PLC via SCADA/DCS.
